CHIM Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (cardio meaning heart, and pathy meaning disease process.): the combining form cardio consistently refers to the heart, and the suffix pathy denotes any disease process affecting a structure. Option A names the wrong combining form for this term. Option C confuses my with myo and misreads the suffix as oma. Option D applies the wrong suffix, since ectomy signals surgical removal, not a general disease process.
  2. The correct answer is D (An abnormally rapid breathing rate above the normal resting range.): the prefix tachy means fast or rapid and the root pnea refers to breathing, so tachypnea describes rapid respirations. Option A describes bradycardia, an unrelated cardiac term. Option B describes apnea, the opposite finding. Option C describes an arrhythmia, which concerns heart rhythm rather than breathing rate.
  3. The correct answer is A (A surgical incision made into an organ or body cavity.): the suffix otomy specifically denotes cutting into a structure, as in tracheotomy. Option B describes the suffix ectomy, a different procedure category. Option C describes ostomy, which creates a stoma rather than a simple incision. Option D describes plasty, which refers to reconstructive repair.
  4. The correct answer is C (The liver and the spleen, the two organs named by hepato and spleno.): hepato refers to the liver, spleno refers to the spleen, and the suffix megaly means enlargement, so the compound term names both organs together. Option A names structures represented by gastro and pancreato instead. Option B names structures represented by nephro and cysto instead. Option D names structures represented by cardio and pulmo instead.
  5. The correct answer is B (idio, meaning peculiar to the individual or unknown.): idio conveys that a condition arose spontaneously or has no identifiable cause, which is exactly how idiopathic is used clinically. Option A describes a different prefix seen in terms like dyspnea. Option C describes excess, as in hypertension. Option D describes absence, as in anemia.
  6. The correct answer is A (Direct visual examination of a joint using an instrument.): arthro means joint and scopy means visual examination with an instrument, together naming arthroscopy precisely. Option B describes arthrodesis, a different suffix entirely. Option C describes angiography, built from a different combining form. Option D describes arthrocentesis, which uses the suffix centesis for puncture and drainage.
  7. The correct answer is D (An increased count of white blood cells in circulation.): leuko refers to white blood cells and the suffix osis generally indicates an increase or abnormal condition, together describing an elevated white count. Option A describes anemia, an unrelated red cell finding. Option B describes thrombocytosis, which concerns platelets. Option C describes leukopenia, the opposite direction of change.
  8. The correct answer is C (Painful or difficult urination reported by the patient as a presenting symptom.): dys means painful, difficult, or abnormal, and uria refers to urination, so dysuria specifically describes discomfort during voiding. Option A describes nocturia, a different combining term. Option B describes incontinence, an unrelated clinical concept. Option D describes hematuria, which uses the combining form hemat for blood.
  9. The correct answer is A (Incision into the kidney to remove a stone.): nephro means kidney, litho means stone, and otomy means incision, together describing a surgical cut made to extract a renal calculus. Option B describes a nephrectomy, using a different suffix. Option C describes a cystostomy, referring to the bladder instead. Option D describes lithotripsy, which does not involve an incision.
  10. The correct answer is B (An abnormally low blood glucose concentration typically confirmed on a fingerstick or laboratory test.): hypo means below normal, glyc refers to sugar or glucose, and emia refers to a blood condition, together describing a low blood sugar state. Option A describes hyperglycemia, the opposite prefix. Option C describes hypocalcemia, a different root entirely. Option D describes hypernatremia, which involves sodium rather than glucose.
  11. The correct answer is C (A porous, weakened structure reflecting decreased density of bone tissue over time.): osteo means bone and porosis describes a porous, weakened condition, together naming the loss of bone density seen on imaging. Option A describes osteomyelitis, a different suffix entirely. Option B describes scoliosis, an unrelated spinal condition. Option D describes synovitis, which concerns joint lining rather than bone density.
  12. The correct answer is D (Pain localized specifically to whatever structure the accompanying medical root word names.): the suffix algia is used universally in medical terminology to signal pain, as demonstrated by terms like arthralgia and myalgia. Option A describes edema, a distinct finding with its own terminology. Option C describes plegia, a different suffix entirely. Option B describes the term paresthesia rather than a suffix built with algia.
  13. The correct answer is B (Cancer cells confined to the original layer with no invasion beyond it.): in situ literally means in its original place, describing malignant cells that have not yet invaded surrounding tissue. Option A describes metastatic disease, a much later stage. Option C describes invasive carcinoma, a stage beyond in situ. Option D describes a screening finding unrelated to the histologic definition of in situ.
  14. The correct answer is A (Inflammation of whatever structure the accompanying medical root word happens to name.): the suffix itis consistently denotes inflammation, so conjunctivitis specifically means inflammation of the conjunctiva. Option C describes a different pathological process with its own terminology. Option B describes the suffix oma, not itis. Option D describes a congenital condition unrelated to the inflammatory meaning of itis.
  15. The correct answer is D (A prefix indicating a location surrounding or completely around a given anatomical structure.): peri means around or surrounding, so pericarditis describes inflammation of the sac surrounding the heart. Option A describes the prefix sub or infra instead. Option B describes the prefix endo or intra instead. Option C describes the prefix trans or dia instead.
  16. The correct answer is C (The right atrium and right ventricle, the two chambers on the right side of the heart.): deoxygenated blood from the body enters the right atrium, passes through the tricuspid valve into the right ventricle, and is then pumped to the lungs via the pulmonary artery. Option A describes the chambers handling oxygenated blood instead. Option B mismatches an atrium and ventricle from opposite sides. Option D also mismatches chambers from opposite sides of the heart.
  17. The correct answer is B (The left atrium and left ventricle, the two chambers on the left side of the heart.): the mitral, or bicuspid, valve sits between the left atrium and left ventricle and closes during systole to prevent backward flow. Option A describes the location of the tricuspid valve instead. Option C describes the location of the aortic valve instead. Option D describes the location of the pulmonic valve instead.
  18. The correct answer is A (Prolonged interruption of coronary blood flow causing ischemic tissue necrosis.): a myocardial infarction occurs when a coronary artery is occluded long enough that the supplied myocardium becomes ischemic and dies, releasing cardiac enzymes such as troponin. Option B describes a venous process unrelated to infarction. Option C describes a rhythm effect, not tissue death. Option D describes a risk factor rather than the direct mechanism of cell death.
  19. The correct answer is C (The legal health record, as understood within Canadian health information management practice.): The legal health record is the formally defined set of documents an organization designates as its official business record, which may be produced in response to a subpoena or disclosure request. It typically includes clinical documentation generated during the episode of care by authorized providers. Organizations define its exact scope through a legal health record policy. This differs from an index or a national data submission, which are administrative or reporting tools rather than the record itself.
  20. The correct answer is D (To clearly identify what must be produced in response to legal requests or audits.): Defining the legal health record in advance ensures the organization knows exactly which documents, wherever they are stored, must be assembled and disclosed when a court, lawyer, or auditor requests the record. Without a clear definition, staff may omit relevant source documents or include material that is not actually part of the formal record. This policy work is a core health information management responsibility. It protects both the patient's rights and the organization from incomplete or inconsistent disclosures.
  21. The correct answer is B (Primary use directly supports ongoing patient care.): Primary use refers to using health information to directly support the care of the individual patient, such as a physician reviewing prior results before writing an order. Secondary use refers to using that same information for purposes beyond direct care delivery, including quality improvement, research, funding, and health system planning. The format of the record, the professional role, and the timing relative to discharge do not define this distinction. CIHI's national databases are a classic example of secondary use.
  22. The correct answer is B (A secondary use of health information, a point worth noting here.): Using aggregated, de-identified discharge information to study system-level patterns such as readmission rates is a secondary use, since it supports research and planning rather than the direct care of an individual patient. Primary use and point-of-care use both describe information supporting an individual's own treatment. Because the data has been de-identified for this purpose, it is also not automatically exempt from privacy obligations, since organizations must still follow proper de-identification and disclosure processes.
  23. The correct answer is B (Timeliness of documentation, a point relevant to how coded health data is used.): Timeliness requires that clinical documentation be entered as close to the time of the encounter as reasonably possible, so the record accurately reflects the sequence and content of care. A significant, unexplained delay like this raises concerns about the reliability and completeness of the note. Legibility concerns how documentation is written or typed, not when it was entered. Consent and terminology are separate documentation elements that are not described in this scenario.
  24. The correct answer is D (The entry should be accurate, complete, and attributable to the person who authored it.): Health record entries are expected to be accurate, complete, timely, and clearly attributable to their author, often through a signature, initials, or an electronic authentication mechanism. This attribution supports accountability and helps establish who is responsible for the clinical decisions documented. Re-signing by another clinician, patient-approved abbreviations, and translation are not recognized documentation quality requirements. These core qualities support both patient care and the record's usefulness as a legal document.
  25. The correct answer is C (An unauthenticated document may not be considered part of the finalized legal health record.): Authentication, such as a physician's signature or electronic sign-off, confirms that the author has reviewed the transcribed content and takes responsibility for its accuracy. Until that happens, the document may remain incomplete and not fully part of the finalized legal record, which can create problems for continuity of care, coding, and legal defensibility. The other listed outcomes are not standard consequences of a delayed authentication and do not reflect real record-completion risk.
  26. The correct answer is B (Record completion or deficiency monitoring.): Record completion, sometimes called deficiency monitoring, tracks how long it takes after discharge for a chart to become fully complete, with all required signatures, reports, and documentation in place. This process supports timely coding, billing, and legal defensibility of the record. Case mix grouping relates to classifying resource use, nomenclature mapping relates to terminology systems, and data linkage auditing relates to connecting separate datasets, none of which describe this completion tracking activity.
  27. The correct answer is A (Delayed completion can hold up coding, abstracting, and funding submissions.): When charts sit incomplete for long periods, downstream processes such as coding, abstracting, and funding submissions to bodies like CIHI can be delayed, and the record may not be readily available if needed for patient care or legal purposes. Completion turnaround targets help departments monitor and address this risk proactively. The other options describe unrelated hospital operations or consent processes that a completion target does not address.
  28. The correct answer is C (The master patient index identifies and links a patient's records across encounters.): The master patient index is an administrative tool that uniquely identifies patients and links their various encounters and record locations across a health system, supporting record retrieval and preventing duplicate patient identities. The legal health record, by contrast, is the actual body of clinical documentation from an episode of care. These serve related but distinct purposes, and one does not substitute for or contain the other in the way the incorrect options describe.
  29. The correct answer is C (SNOMED CT as a nomenclature-like terminology and ICD-10-CA as a classification system.): A nomenclature or clinical terminology, such as SNOMED CT, aims to capture clinical concepts with a very high degree of specificity and detail, often supporting direct point-of-care documentation. A classification system, such as ICD-10-CA, instead groups related concepts into a manageable, finite number of categories using codes, primarily to support statistical reporting, funding, and research. The other pairings reverse or misassign these roles and do not reflect how these systems actually function.
  30. The correct answer is D (A terminology captures fine clinical detail, while a classification aggregates concepts for reporting, funding.): This distinction matters because the two system types serve different purposes: a clinical terminology is designed to represent detailed clinical meaning as precisely as possible, while a classification system deliberately groups similar concepts together so that data can be aggregated, compared, and reported at a population or funding level. Neither system is restricted by profession, and both terminologies and classifications commonly use codes, so those distinctions are inaccurate.
  31. The correct answer is D (A statistical classification system used to code diagnoses for morbidity reporting in Canada.): ICD-10-CA is Canada's national statistical classification used to code diagnoses, primarily for morbidity data from hospital and other encounters, supporting national reporting, research, and health system planning. It is maintained by CIHI as the Canadian enhancement of the World Health Organization's ICD-10. It is not a fee schedule, a real-time nursing documentation tool, or a pharmacy database, all of which serve entirely different functions within the health system.
  32. The correct answer is C (A classification intentionally limits its category structure so that data can be meaningfully aggregated across many patients, facilities, and time periods.): Classification systems are deliberately structured with a finite, manageable set of categories precisely so that large volumes of clinical data can be grouped, compared, and reported meaningfully across many patients, facilities, and time periods. A clinical terminology, in contrast, is built to capture much finer clinical granularity for individual documentation. This design difference is intentional and functional, not a sign that classifications are outdated, and classifications are certainly used in both inpatient and outpatient settings.
  33. The correct answer is A (A system that organizes clinical concepts into a limited set of mutually exclusive categories.): A clinical classification system organizes health concepts, such as diseases or interventions, into a limited, structured set of categories, with each category assigned a code, so that the resulting data can support statistical analysis and reporting. Capturing every clinical synonym is more characteristic of a terminology system, while appointment scheduling and handwriting transcription describe entirely unrelated health information functions.
  34. The correct answer is D (A structured clinical terminology such as SNOMED CT.): A structured clinical terminology, such as SNOMED CT, is built specifically to represent detailed clinical concepts and their relationships, which makes it well suited to mapping clinician language to standardized terms, and in some environments, to underlying classification codes. Policy manuals, funding formulas, and satisfaction surveys are unrelated to representing or mapping clinical concepts and play no role in this coding support function.
  35. The correct answer is B (ICD-10-CA classifies diagnoses, while CCI classifies health interventions and procedures.): ICD-10-CA is the Canadian classification used to code diagnoses, signs, symptoms, and other reasons for a health care encounter. The Canadian Classification of Health Interventions, or CCI, is a separate, complementary classification developed in Canada specifically to code procedures and interventions performed during that encounter. Together they support a complete picture of why a patient was seen and what was done, and they are not interchangeable or limited to laboratory data.
  36. The correct answer is B (CCI was designed to provide a more current, detailed classification of health care interventions than earlier systems could support.): As health care interventions became more numerous, technologically advanced, and varied, the older procedure classification structures no longer captured this complexity well. CCI was developed in Canada with a more current, systematic, multi-axial structure to better classify contemporary interventions and support national data quality. It did not eliminate diagnosis coding, reduce reporting to CIHI, or have any connection to veterinary medicine, all of which misstate its actual purpose.
  37. The correct answer is D (The terminology captures detailed clinical documentation at the point of care.): Many modern electronic health record environments use a clinical terminology for detailed, structured point-of-care documentation, and separately rely on a classification such as ICD-10-CA or CCI to translate that clinical detail into standardized codes for statistical reporting, funding, and research. These two system types are complementary rather than mutually exclusive, and their use has nothing to do with facility location or the timing of patient registration.
  38. The correct answer is A (They all provide standardized ways to represent health concepts so that information can be consistently recorded.): Despite differing in their level of detail and structure, nomenclatures, clinical terminologies, and classification systems share the underlying purpose of standardizing how health concepts are represented, which supports consistent documentation, communication, coding, and data analysis across a health system. Patients do not select these systems, accreditation visits are not their defining purpose, and none of them originated as dental-specific tools.
  39. The correct answer is A (The Tabular List provides the full official code description along with any relevant instructional notes that apply to that code.): The Alphabetical Index helps a coder efficiently locate a candidate code based on the documented term, but it does not display the complete official code title or the instructional notes, such as includes, excludes, or additional coding instructions, that appear in the Tabular List. Verifying in the Tabular List ensures the code is applied correctly and completely. Skipping this step, regardless of a coder's experience, risks selecting an incorrect or incomplete code.
  40. The correct answer is A (It clarifies additional terms or conditions that are appropriately classified to that code category.): An includes note under a code category provides examples or clarifies terms and conditions that fall within that category, helping the coder confirm that a documented condition belongs there. It is a classification convention, not a clinical or administrative directive, so it has no bearing on medication orders, required signatures, or length of stay, none of which are addressed by classification notes.
  41. The correct answer is B (Recognize that the excluded condition should be coded elsewhere, using a different, more appropriate code.): An excludes note signals that a similarly named or related condition is classified elsewhere and should not be coded under the current category. Coders are expected to follow this instruction and select the correct code for the excluded condition from its proper location, rather than treating the note as optional. Excludes notes do not direct coders to double-code both conditions together, nor do they involve any communication with the patient's family.
  42. The correct answer is C (A single code that captures two related diagnoses.): A combination code allows a coder to represent two closely related concepts, such as a disease and a common associated manifestation, complication, or an underlying condition together with a secondary condition, using a single code rather than two separate ones. This differs from an arbitrary rule requiring a fixed number of accompanying codes, and it has nothing to do with equipment issues or automated length-of-stay calculations.
  43. The correct answer is C (The etiology and manifestation convention, often shown using dagger and asterisk symbols.): ICD-10-CA uses an etiology and manifestation convention for certain conditions where the underlying cause, or etiology, and its manifestation in another body system are both coded, traditionally distinguished using dagger and asterisk notation. This convention ensures both the cause and its clinical expression are captured. It is unrelated to consent processes, admission and discharge workflows, or the broader distinction between nomenclature and terminology systems.
  44. The correct answer is B (To add clinical detail and codes relevant to conditions and reporting needs specific to the Canadian health care system.): ICD-10-CA was developed by enhancing the World Health Organization's international ICD-10 classification with additional clinical detail and codes that reflect conditions, terminology, and reporting priorities specific to the Canadian context, while still remaining broadly comparable internationally. It does not remove infectious disease codes, does not prevent Canada from participating in international reporting, and was not designed to be incompatible with electronic systems.
  45. The correct answer is D (A 'code also' or 'use additional code' type of instructional note.): Certain ICD-10-CA categories include instructional notes directing the coder to assign an additional code to more completely describe a condition, such as identifying a causal organism alongside the primary diagnosis. This is a standard, intentional classification convention rather than an error, and it is not restricted to newborn patients or tied to any insurance verification process.
  46. The correct answer is B (The Alphabetical Index, organized by main terms with subterms and modifiers listed beneath each.): Coding conventionally begins in the Alphabetical Index, which is organized around main terms, often the condition itself, with relevant subterms and modifiers nested beneath them to help the coder narrow down the most specific and accurate code. Reading the entire Tabular List sequentially is not an efficient or standard coding method, and staffing schedules or insurance cards play no role in identifying a diagnosis code.
  47. The correct answer is D (They help the coder select the most specific and accurate code based on additional clinical detail.): Subterms listed beneath a main term in the Alphabetical Index capture clinically important variations, such as anatomical site, cause, or severity, that allow the coder to select a more specific and accurate code than the main term alone would provide. They do not eliminate the need to verify the final selection in the Tabular List, are not labeled as decorative, and this kind of index structure is also relevant to procedure classifications, not diagnoses alone.
  48. The correct answer is A (Identify the correct clinical main term for the condition.): When documentation uses an informal or non-standard term, the coder must determine the correct clinical concept, seeking physician clarification through a query process if necessary, and then locate the appropriate standardized main term in the index. Coders must never alter clinician documentation themselves, leave a legitimately documented diagnosis uncoded without justification, or select a code arbitrarily, since all of these approaches compromise data accuracy and integrity.
  49. The correct answer is C (It organizes diagnosis codes into chapters based broadly on body system or condition type.): The Tabular List arranges ICD-10-CA diagnosis codes into chapters generally based on body system or condition type, such as diseases of the circulatory system, with each chapter further broken down into progressively more specific categories and subcategories. This structure has nothing to do with a patient's name, the treating hospital unit, or the founding date of the World Health Organization, none of which organize the classification.

This indicator reflects how long, on average, a patient remained hospitalized before discharge during the reporting period. It is a core efficiency and utilization indicator used in resource planning and case-mix comparisons.

This indicator reflects how long, on average, a patient remained hospitalized before discharge during the reporting period. It is a core efficiency and utilization indicator used in resource planning and case-mix comparisons.

It is a core efficiency and utilization indicator used in resource planning and case-mix comparisons.

The correct answer is C (90.0%.): Bed occupancy rate equals total inpatient service days divided by the product of bed count and days in the period, multiplied by 100: 5400 ÷ (200 × 30) × 100 = 90.0%. This indicator shows what proportion of the facility's available bed capacity was actually used during the period and is essential for capacity planning and resource allocation decisions.
  56. This indicator shows what proportion of the facility's available bed capacity was actually used during the period and is essential for capacity planning and resource allocation decisions.

This indicator shows what proportion of the facility's available bed capacity was actually used during the period and is essential for capacity planning and resource allocation decisions.
  58. This indicator shows what proportion of the facility's available bed capacity was actually used during the period and is essential for capacity planning and resource allocation decisions.
  59. The correct answer is C (4.0 per 1,000.): Incidence rate is new cases occurring during a period divided by the population at risk during that same period, multiplied by the standard base: (48 ÷ 12,000) × 1,000 = 4.0 per 1,000. Incidence measures the frequency of NEW cases arising over time, which distinguishes it from prevalence, which counts all existing cases at a given point or period.
  60. The correct answer is A (1.88 per 1,000.): Incidence rate is new cases occurring during a period divided by the population at risk during that same period, multiplied by the standard base: (15 ÷ 8,000) × 1,000 = 1.88 per 1,000. Incidence measures the frequency of NEW cases arising over time, which distinguishes it from prevalence, which counts all existing cases at a given point or period.
  61. The correct answer is D (2.0 per 1,000.): Incidence rate is new cases occurring during a period divided by the population at risk during that same period, multiplied by the standard base: (6 ÷ 3,000) × 1,000 = 2.0 per 1,000. Incidence measures the frequency of NEW cases arising over time, which distinguishes it from prevalence, which counts all existing cases at a given point or period.
  62. The correct answer is C (200.0 per 100,000.): Incidence rate is new cases occurring during a period divided by the population at risk during that same period, multiplied by the standard base: (90 ÷ 45,000) × 100,000 = 200.0 per 100,000. Incidence measures the frequency of NEW cases arising over time, which distinguishes it from prevalence, which counts all existing cases at a given point or period.
  63. The correct answer is A (15.5 per 1,000.): Prevalence rate is calculated as all existing cases (new and pre-existing) at a point in time divided by the total population at that time, multiplied by the base: (620 ÷ 40,000) × 1,000 = 15.5 per 1,000. Prevalence captures the total burden of a condition in a population, whereas incidence only counts cases that are newly diagnosed during a defined period.
  64. The correct answer is B (8.95 per 1,000.): Prevalence rate is calculated as all existing cases (new and pre-existing) at a point in time divided by the total population at that time, multiplied by the base: (85 ÷ 9,500) × 1,000 = 8.95 per 1,000. Prevalence captures the total burden of a condition in a population, whereas incidence only counts cases that are newly diagnosed during a defined period.
  65. The correct answer is C (600.0 per 100,000.): Prevalence rate is calculated as all existing cases (new and pre-existing) at a point in time divided by the total population at that time, multiplied by the base: (1200 ÷ 200,000) × 100,000 = 600.0 per 100,000. Prevalence captures the total burden of a condition in a population, whereas incidence only counts cases that are newly diagnosed during a defined period.
  66. The correct answer is C (7.5%.): Case fatality rate is the number of deaths attributed to a specific disease divided by the total number of confirmed cases of that disease, multiplied by 100: (18 ÷ 240) × 100 = 7.5%. Unlike a general mortality rate, case fatality rate is calculated only among the population who actually contracted the disease, so it reflects the severity of that specific condition rather than its frequency in the general population.
  67. Unlike a general mortality rate, case fatality rate is calculated only among the population who actually contracted the disease, so it reflects the severity of that specific condition rather than its frequency in the general population.

Unlike a general mortality rate, case fatality rate is calculated only among the population who actually contracted the disease, so it reflects the severity of that specific condition rather than its frequency in the general population.
  69. The correct answer is D (20.0%.): Gross autopsy rate is total inpatient autopsies divided by total inpatient deaths for the same period, multiplied by 100: (28 ÷ 140) × 100 = 20.0%. This is the gross rate because it includes ALL inpatient deaths in the denominator, including coroner or medical examiner cases that the hospital was not permitted to autopsy, unlike the net autopsy rate.
  70. The correct answer is D (15.8%.): Gross autopsy rate is total inpatient autopsies divided by total inpatient deaths for the same period, multiplied by 100: (15 ÷ 95) × 100 = 15.8%. This is the gross rate because it includes ALL inpatient deaths in the denominator, including coroner or medical examiner cases that the hospital was not permitted to autopsy, unlike the net autopsy rate.
  72. The correct answer is D (An EMR is the digital record used within a single care setting.): In Canadian health information management terminology, an electronic medical record is the digitized version of a paper chart maintained by and for a single practice, clinic, or facility, containing that provider's own documentation of care. This is distinguished from an electronic health record, which is designed to aggregate and share clinical information across multiple providers and care settings. Option A misstates how imaging is generally handled and invents a distinction that does not reflect standard usage, option B reverses the actual scope relationship since an EHR, not an EMR, is the one intended to span multiple organizations, and option C is incorrect because the terms describe meaningfully different scopes of custody and sharing, not interchangeable systems.
  73. The correct answer is C (A personal health record that the patient herself manages and controls access to.): A personal health record is a tool that the patient creates, maintains, and controls independently of any single provider's system, which matches a patient accessing and updating her own allergy list, immunizations, and lab results through a portal outside her physician's office system. A master patient index (option A) is an internal administrative tool used to uniquely identify registered patients within an organization and is not something a patient views or edits herself. An enterprise clinical data repository (option B) is explicitly restricted to authorized clinicians, the opposite of patient-controlled access, and a laboratory information system interface (option D) is built for ordering providers to manage test workflow, not for direct patient self-management of a broader health summary.
  74. The correct answer is D (A PHR is controlled and often entered directly by the patient, whereas an EMR is the official record maintained by the provider.): The defining distinction between these two record types is custody and control: a personal health record is patient-owned and frequently populated by the patient themselves, while an electronic medical record is the official record created and maintained by a healthcare provider or organization using clinical software. Option A is backwards, since the EMR, not the PHR, functions as the official legal record of care created by the provider. Option B incorrectly describes a PHR as something a hospital department creates using approved software, which is actually a description closer to an EMR. Option C is false because there is no requirement that a PHR and an EMR share a hosting server for either to remain valid; they are typically separate systems maintained by different parties.
  75. The correct answer is A (An electronic health record designed to consolidate and transmit information across multiple providers and settings.): A system built specifically to let a discharge summary, medication list, and imaging reports follow a patient from a hospital to a family physician and a specialist in another city is functioning as an electronic health record, since the EHR's defining feature is its ability to aggregate and share clinical data across organizational boundaries. A departmental EMR confined to one hospital's radiology unit (option B) is far narrower in scope and does not extend to outside providers. A stand-alone PHR populated only by the patient (option C) does not match a scenario driven by provider-generated clinical documents moving between care teams, and describing a physically couriered paper chart (option D) contradicts the premise of an electronic interoperability project entirely.
  76. The correct answer is B (The software can securely exchange structured clinical data with other organizations' systems rather than only storing records locally.): The capability that genuinely distinguishes a true EHR from a basic EMR is interoperability, the ability to securely share structured clinical data with external organizations rather than keeping information siloed on one system. A single receptionist scheduling appointments for one clinic (option A) reflects a purely local, single-setting function typical of a basic EMR. Printing paper notes for a manual filing cabinet (option C) is not even a digital capability and undermines any claim to being an EHR. Storing data exclusively on one physician's desktop with no network access (option D) is the clearest example of an isolated EMR, the opposite of the cross-organizational exchange that defines an EHR.
  77. The correct answer is A (The patient, not the healthcare organization, decides what information is entered and who may view it.): The defining feature of a personal health record is that the patient holds control over its content and access, in contrast to a provider-held record such as an EMR or EHR, where the healthcare organization retains custody and governs what is documented and who may view it. Option B reverses this relationship, since it is the provider-held record, not the PHR, that the organization retains legal and physical custody of. Option C is factually wrong because patient-entered information is precisely what makes a PHR distinct and valuable, not something it is barred from containing. Option D is incorrect because a PHR is not automatically identical to a provider's EMR; the patient may include, omit, or add content independently of what the clinic has documented.

The correct answer is C (Interoperable electronic health record functionality spanning multiple care settings.): A planned system that lets emergency department staff view medication history originating from a separate family doctor's office is, by definition, extending information sharing across organizational boundaries, which is the core characteristic of EHR-level interoperability rather than any single clinic's EMR. Option A describes a single-vendor system confined to one physical location, the opposite of the cross-setting sharing described. Option B mischaracterizes the scenario as patient-managed when it is actually a provider- and system-driven data-sharing initiative with minimal patient control described. Option D describes a paper-based scheduling tool, which has no relevance to an electronic system designed for clinical medication history sharing between care settings.
  79. The correct answer is C (Information tends to remain siloed within the originating practice or facility rather than following the patient elsewhere.): A well-recognized limitation of a basic EMR compared with a fully interoperable EHR is that EMR data typically stays confined to the practice or facility where it was created, so a patient's information does not automatically follow them to another provider. Option A is inaccurate because EMRs are legally used to document clinical encounters across Canada and are the standard tool for doing so. Option B misrepresents how EMRs work, since clinicians, not patients, generally enter diagnoses, and requiring the patient to type every diagnosis is not a real feature or limitation. Option D is also inaccurate because EMRs are built primarily to support detailed clinical documentation, not merely financial billing, even though billing functions may coexist within them.
  80. Option A incorrectly assigns patient control to the EMR and vendor control to the EHR, both of which misstate actual custodianship. Option C swaps the EHR and EMR definitions entirely, incorrectly making the EHR patient controlled and the EMR multi-setting. Option D is incorrect because these three terms describe genuinely different scopes of custody and sharing, not merely different software branding of the same underlying system.
  81. The correct answer is D (Interoperable infrastructure allows clinical information to follow the patient across providers.): Provinces invest in interoperable EHR infrastructure specifically because it allows a patient's clinical information, such as medications, allergies, and recent results, to be accessible wherever that patient receives care, rather than remaining locked within each individual clinic's own EMR. Option B understates the clinical continuity value of EHR infrastructure by claiming it exists only for billing, which is not accurate. Option C is incorrect and actually describes a serious risk rather than a benefit, since interoperable systems still require robust privacy, consent, and access safeguards, not their elimination.
  82.   89.   94. The correct answer is B (Implied consent because the access falls within the circle of care.): Most provincial health information statutes, such as Ontario's PHIPA, permit custodians and their agents to assume implied consent for uses and disclosures within the circle of care, meaning providers directly involved in delivering the patient's current care. This recognizes that requiring express consent for every routine clinical communication would obstruct timely treatment. The patient may still expressly withdraw or limit this implied consent through a lock box or consent directive. Access outside the circle of care, such as for marketing, generally requires express consent instead.
  95. The correct answer is A (Implied consent, since the disclosure is for the purpose of providing health care within the circle of care.): Under PHIPA and similar provincial frameworks, a referral to a new specialist for the purpose of providing health care generally falls within the circle of care, so implied consent covers the disclosure even though the patient has not yet met or agreed to see that specific specialist. This is why the same principle correctly covers a new team member joining an existing care team in a similar scenario. Express consent would only be needed if the patient had expressly withheld or withdrawn consent to this kind of disclosure, or if the disclosure were for a purpose outside health care provision. The custodian should still document the referral and give the patient the opportunity to object.
  96. The correct answer is D (A group of providers directly involved in delivering the patient's care.): The circle of care describes the functional relationship between a patient and the providers actively delivering treatment, not a physical location or a single professional category. It typically includes physicians, nurses, allied health staff, and support personnel whose roles require access to the record for that care. The concept allows necessary clinical communication without imposing an unworkable consent burden on every interaction. It does not automatically extend to researchers, insurers, or unrelated departments.
  97. The correct answer is C (A lock box, restricting a disclosure that implied consent would otherwise permit.): Several provincial statutes, including Ontario's PHIPA, allow a patient to expressly withdraw or restrict the implied consent that would otherwise apply within the circle of care, commonly called a lock box or consent directive. The custodian must generally honour this restriction unless an exception applies, such as a risk of serious harm to the patient or others. The restricted information is typically flagged so downstream providers know something has been withheld. This mechanism balances patient autonomy against clinical safety.
  98. The custodian should decline the request unless the patient provides express consent or a valid substitute decision-making authority applies, neither of which exists here. Genuine family concern does not override a capable patient's own privacy rights. The department may suggest the son raise the matter directly with his mother.

  100. The correct answer is B (Implied consent is inferred from context such as circle-of-care treatment.): Implied consent is a legal presumption that a patient agrees to a use or disclosure because of the surrounding treatment context, most commonly circle-of-care communication. Express consent, by contrast, requires the patient to actively and clearly agree, whether in writing or verbally, typically for disclosures outside that immediate treatment relationship. The distinction matters because it determines how much affirmative action a custodian must take before sharing information. Both forms of consent can generally be withdrawn by the patient at any time.
  101. Best practice, and the approach favoured by most provincial privacy guidance, is to obtain the patient's express consent and confirm the correct address before sending any personal health information electronically. This also lets the patient choose a more secure channel such as phone or mail if preferred. Documenting the consent protects the custodian if a dispute arises later.
  102. The counsellor must obtain the client's express, informed consent before disclosing any treatment details to the employer, and the client controls how much information is shared. Employment-related documentation, such as a note confirming fitness for work, should generally be limited to what is necessary rather than clinical specifics.   103. The correct answer is D (The custodian must reasonably believe the individual would consent to the specific use or disclosure.): PHIPA permits a custodian to rely on implied consent within the circle of care when it reasonably believes the individual would consent to the particular use or disclosure, given that it is for the purpose of providing or assisting in the patient's healthcare. This reasonable-belief standard avoids the impracticality of documenting explicit agreement for every clinical interaction while still anchoring the presumption in the patient's likely wishes. Custodians must still respect any express instruction from the patient limiting or withdrawing that implied consent. This standard does not extend to purposes unrelated to the patient's own care.
  104. Even though the researcher shares an employer with the treating physician, that alone does not bring the research use within implied consent. Provincial statutes typically require either the patient's express consent, approval through a research ethics board process with appropriate safeguards, or another specific statutory authority before health information can be used for research. This distinction protects patients from having their records repurposed beyond what they reasonably expected.
  106. Provincial privacy statutes require the custodian to obtain the patient's express consent, often accompanied by a signed authorization form provided by the insurer, before releasing any health information for this purpose. The other three examples all involve providers actively delivering the patient's current care and can properly rely on implied consent. Confusing these categories is a common and consequential HIM error.

Requiring renewed written consent for every routine update would be impractical and would not meaningfully increase the patient's control over information already understood to flow between their treating providers. The patient remains free to place restrictions on this sharing if they have specific concerns. This reflects the everyday functioning of shared, coordinated care.
  109. The correct answer is C (Yes, most provincial statutes allow patients to place specific restrictions on disclosure.): Most provincial health privacy statutes recognize that a patient's right to control their information includes the ability to place granular restrictions, such as withholding certain notes or limiting disclosure to a specific provider, without necessarily withdrawing consent for all care. This flexibility respects patient autonomy while allowing treatment to continue. The custodian must implement a mechanism, often called a lock box, to flag and honour such restrictions in practice. Exceptions can still apply where withholding information would create a significant risk of harm.
  110. The correct answer is A (Express consent, typically obtained through a claim authorization signed by the patient.): An insurance provider processing a claim is not involved in delivering the patient's dental care and therefore sits outside the circle of care, so the office needs the patient's express consent before sharing treatment history. In practice, this is usually satisfied through a claim authorization or assignment form the patient signs, which specifies what information may be released and to whom. This protects the patient from having clinical details shared with a payer beyond what is necessary to process the specific claim. The dental office should retain a copy of the signed authorization in the record.
  111. The correct answer is A (Honour the verbal instruction and document it in the record.): PHIPA does not require a patient's instruction to withhold or restrict consent to be made in a particular written form; a clear verbal instruction is generally sufficient and should be respected. Good HIM practice is to document the instruction promptly in the record so that all subsequent providers are aware of the restriction, even without a formal signed document. This documentation protects both the patient's wishes and the custodian's ability to demonstrate compliance if questioned later. Staff should still confirm they understood the instruction correctly with the patient.
  112. Distinguishing genuine care-related access from unrelated access is a core HIM competency. Audit logs help detect the improper scenarios after the fact.
  113.
