This is a common and straightforward form of express consent in everyday practice, often documented through a simple release request. The travel clinic then becomes part of the patient's circle of care for the purpose of the travel consultation once the relationship is established. The physician's office should still verify the request came from the patient before releasing the records.
  114. The correct answer is D (No, ongoing automatic sharing with providers no longer actively involved in current care exceeds what implied consent within the circle of care actually permits.): The circle of care is tied to active, current involvement in delivering the patient's care, not to any provider who has ever treated the patient in the past. Automatically pushing updated information to former providers with no ongoing role exceeds what a reasonable patient would expect and what implied consent is designed to support, creating unnecessary privacy risk. A properly designed system should limit ongoing access to providers with a current, demonstrable care relationship, often reassessed periodically. This scenario illustrates a common technical design flaw that health information management professionals should be alert to when auditing system access rules.
  115. The correct answer is B (If the minor is assessed as capable of consenting to their own treatment.): Several Canadian jurisdictions recognize the mature minor doctrine, under which a young person assessed as capable of understanding the nature and consequences of a treatment decision may provide their own consent and correspondingly control who receives their health information. Where this applies, parents are not automatically included in the circle of care for that matter simply because of their parental status, and the minor's own instructions govern disclosure. Providers must still assess capacity appropriately and document that assessment. This reflects a balance between family involvement and a young person's evolving autonomy in Canadian health law.
  116. The correct answer is D (Public administration, meaning a non profit public authority runs the plan.): Public administration requires that a provincial or territorial health insurance plan be operated on a non-profit basis by a public authority that is accountable to the government for its decisions and is subject to audit of its accounts and financial transactions. This principle exists to keep the administration of medically necessary insured services outside for-profit private control. It does not govern which services are covered or who is eligible; those are addressed by comprehensiveness and universality respectively.
  117. The correct answer is A (Comprehensiveness, meaning coverage of all medically necessary hospital and physician services.): comprehensiveness requires that a provincial or territorial health plan insure all medically necessary services provided by hospitals, medical practitioners, and dentists working within a hospital setting. This principle sets the scope of coverage rather than who administers the plan or how residents access it. It is one of the more frequently tested principles because coverage disputes often turn on whether a service is medically necessary.
  118. The correct answer is C (Universality, meaning uniform entitlement to insured services for every eligible resident.): Universality requires that all insured residents of a province or territory be entitled to insured health services on uniform terms and conditions, meaning coverage cannot vary based on a person's income, employment status, or pre-existing health condition. This differs from accessibility, which focuses on removing financial and other barriers to actually obtaining care. Universality is about entitlement, while accessibility is about the practical ability to use that entitlement.
  119. The correct answer is B (Portability, meaning continued coverage for residents who move or travel temporarily.): Portability requires that a resident who moves to another province or territory continue to be covered for insured health services by their home province during a minimum waiting period, typically up to three months, imposed by the new province of residence. Portability also protects coverage for insured persons who are temporarily absent from their home province or Canada, such as while travelling or studying elsewhere. This distinguishes it from universality, which concerns equal entitlement within one plan rather than continuity between plans.
  120. The correct answer is D (Accessibility, meaning reasonable access to insured services free of financial barriers.): Accessibility requires that insured persons have reasonable access to insured health services without financial or other barriers, such as extra billing or user charges, and that access not be impeded by factors like unreasonable geographic barriers when reasonable alternatives exist. Significant unaddressed travel burden with no financial support can raise accessibility concerns even where the service itself is technically insured. This principle is distinct from comprehensiveness, which asks only whether the service is covered at all.
  121. The correct answer is D (To discourage charging patients directly for insured services and preserve accessibility.): the Canada Health Act discourages extra billing and user charges for insured health services because such charges create a financial barrier that undermines the accessibility principle. Provinces that permit extra billing or user charges can face a dollar-for-dollar reduction in their federal cash transfer under the Act. This mechanism gives the federal government a practical lever to enforce accessibility even though delivery of care is a provincial responsibility.
  122. The correct answer is B (Accessibility, meaning reasonable access to insured services free of financial barriers.): charging a patient an extra fee on top of what the public plan already pays is a form of extra billing, which directly undermines accessibility by creating a financial barrier to obtaining an insured service. Accessibility requires that insured services be provided on uniform terms and conditions without financial barriers that impede reasonable access. This scenario does not describe a gap in coverage scope, which would instead implicate comprehensiveness.
  123. The correct answer is D (Affordability, a term that does not appear among the five named principles.): the five Canada Health Act principles are public administration, comprehensiveness, universality, portability, and accessibility. Affordability is not a named statutory principle, although accessibility does address financial barriers such as extra billing and user charges. A CHIM professional should be able to name all five principles precisely, since exam questions frequently test recognition of the exact list.
  124. The correct answer is D (Public administration, meaning a non profit public authority runs the plan.): Public administration requires the health care insurance plan to be administered on a non-profit basis by a public authority that is accountable to the provincial or territorial government and subject to audit. A private, for-profit administrator retaining surpluses without government accountability would not meet this requirement. This principle governs who runs the plan, not what services it covers or how residents access them.
  125. The correct answer is A (Portability, meaning continued coverage and a permitted minimum qualifying period for new residents.): The Canada Health Act's portability condition allows a province to impose a minimum qualifying period, commonly up to three months, before a new resident becomes eligible for that province's health insurance plan. Universality instead concerns uniform entitlement to insured services once a resident is actually covered, not the waiting period itself. Accessibility concerns reasonable access free of financial barriers, and public administration concerns who runs the plan, neither of which explains a residency-based waiting period.
  126. The correct answer is B (Universality, meaning uniform entitlement to insured services for every eligible resident.): Universality requires that all insured residents be entitled to insured health services on uniform terms and conditions, so a province cannot restrict coverage of a medically necessary insured service based on a characteristic like age. Doing so would create unequal entitlement among residents who are otherwise eligible for the plan. Comprehensiveness would instead be implicated if the service itself were dropped from coverage entirely for everyone.
  127. The correct answer is B (Comprehensiveness concerns the scope of insured services, while universality concerns equal entitlement.): comprehensiveness sets out which services a provincial plan must cover, essentially all medically necessary hospital, physician, and applicable dental-surgical services, while universality requires that every eligible resident have equal entitlement to whatever services are covered. The two principles work together but answer different questions, one about scope and one about equal access to that scope. Confusing them is a common error on certification exams.
  128. The correct answer is C (Coverage for insured services generally continues during a defined temporary absence period.): portability requires that insured persons remain covered for insured health services during temporary absences from their home province, including travel outside Canada, though provinces may set limits on the duration of absence and on reimbursement rates for out-of-country care. This protects Canadians who travel or work temporarily abroad without forcing them to requalify for coverage upon return. It does not create indefinite or unconditional coverage abroad.
  129. The correct answer is D (Comprehensiveness, meaning coverage of all medically necessary hospital and physician services.): Comprehensiveness requires that a provincial health plan cover all medically necessary services rendered by hospitals and medical practitioners. Removing a medically necessary hospital surgical service entirely from the covered list would reduce the plan's required scope of coverage, directly implicating comprehensiveness. This differs from a scenario where the service remains covered but access to it is restricted by cost or geography, which would instead raise accessibility concerns.
  130. The correct answer is A (Health Canada, the federal department that administers the Canada Health Act.): Health Canada administers the Canada Health Act and monitors whether provincial and territorial health insurance plans meet the conditions of public administration, comprehensiveness, universality, portability, and accessibility, reporting annually to Parliament. The Public Health Agency of Canada instead focuses on public health surveillance and emergency preparedness, while CIHI compiles health system data and Accreditation Canada evaluates individual health organizations. Distinguishing the mandates of these federal bodies is a core CHIM competency.
  131. The correct answer is A (Accessibility, meaning reasonable access to insured services free of financial barriers.): Accessibility requires reasonable access to insured services unimpeded by financial barriers, so a mandatory premium tied to activating coverage could be scrutinized as a barrier to access, particularly for lower-income residents. The Canada Health Act discourages user charges and extra billing precisely because they can deter people from seeking necessary care. This is distinct from universality, which concerns whether coverage terms are uniform across residents rather than whether a financial barrier exists at all.
  132. The correct answer is D (Provincial and territorial governments, which hold constitutional responsibility for delivering health services.): under Canada's constitutional division of powers, provinces and territories are responsible for organizing and delivering health care services, including operating hospitals and administering physician payment, while the federal government sets national conditions through the Canada Health Act and provides funding support. This split explains why coverage details, wait times, and delivery models can differ across provinces even though all provinces must meet the same five Act principles. CHIM professionals must understand this division because it shapes how health information systems and funding are structured provincially.
  133. The correct answer is C (A province refuses to reimburse any emergency out-of-country care for a temporarily absent resident.): portability requires that insured persons continue to receive coverage for insured services, including a reasonable level of reimbursement for emergency out-of-country care, during temporary absences from their home province. Refusing any reimbursement whatsoever for a temporarily absent resident's emergency care would undermine this continuity of coverage. Waiting periods for new residents and prior authorization for elective care are permitted administrative practices and do not by themselves breach portability.
  134. The correct answer is A (Conduct a focused review of that coder's charts and provide targeted education or correction as needed.): An outlier pattern in severity coding warrants investigation to determine whether it reflects a genuine clinical pattern, a training gap, or a potential integrity issue such as upcoding, and the appropriate response is a proportionate, evidence based review. Assuming thoroughness or ignoring the pattern risks allowing systemic inaccuracy to continue unaddressed. Termination without review is a disproportionate response before the cause is even established.
  135. The correct answer is B (Submit a formal query to the physician to clarify the diagnosis before assigning a final code.): Ethical, accurate coding requires resolving genuine clinical documentation inconsistencies through the established physician query process rather than the coder independently choosing between conflicting sources. Selecting based on personal judgment about which source seems more reliable exceeds the coder's scope of practice. A permanent unresolved gap in the record can also affect patient care and funding accuracy, so the query process is the appropriate path forward.
  136. The correct answer is D (The combined access exceeds what is necessary for billing purposes and should be more narrowly scoped.): Applying the minimum necessary principle, billing functions typically require specific clinical details relevant to charge accuracy rather than unrestricted access to the full clinical record, so the policy as proposed likely grants broader access than the stated purpose requires. Familiarity with handling financial information does not justify unrestricted clinical access. A signed confidentiality agreement does not substitute for properly scoped technical access controls.
  137. The correct answer is C (Query the clinical team to confirm the correct measurement before it affects coding.): A clinical measurement that appears to be an obvious data entry error and could affect diagnosis or severity coding should be clarified through the clinical team before it influences a coding decision, since coders should never simply defer to an implausible value nor silently alter clinical documentation themselves. Using an implausible value uncritically risks a genuinely inaccurate code. Silently correcting the record oversteps the coder's role, since only appropriate clinical staff should amend clinical documentation.
  138. The correct answer is B (Include the relevant context about the temporary staffing gap alongside the accurate underlying data.): Ethical reporting requires presenting both accurate data and relevant context that helps readers correctly interpret the findings, since omitting known context can lead to an unfair or misleading impression of a department's ongoing performance. Adjusting the actual reported number, even informally, misrepresents the true data. Excluding the department entirely deprives leadership of information they need for accurate organizational oversight.
  139. The correct answer is A (Refuse to disclose information by phone and direct the caller to the written request process.): Identity cannot be reliably verified over the phone, and relationship status alone does not authorize disclosure without the patient's consent or a formal authorization on file. Following the organization's established release of information process protects the patient regardless of who the caller claims to be. Knowledge of a birth date is not sufficient verification since that detail could be obtained from many sources.
  140. The correct answer is C (Provide an honest, balanced reference and avoid making misleading positive claims about the colleague's record.): Integrity as a reference means avoiding affirmatively misleading statements, while typically not requiring unsolicited disclosure of confidential personnel matters that were not specifically asked about. Providing only glowing comments that create a false impression crosses into misrepresentation. Proactively disclosing confidential personnel file details without the colleague's knowledge or a specific relevant question raises its own confidentiality concern, so a careful, honest, and appropriately limited reference is the balanced approach.
  141. The correct answer is D (Integrity, since falsifying continuing education completion misrepresents her true reported achievement.): Claiming full credit for a session not fully completed misrepresents her actual continuing education achievement, which is fundamentally a matter of honest self reporting rather than a competence, confidentiality, or scope issue. Continuing education systems depend on individuals accurately reporting their own participation since attendance is often not independently verified. This situation is squarely about integrity in professional self reporting.
  142. The correct answer is D (Explain general terminology in the summary while encouraging the friend to raise concerns with her physician.): Helping a friend understand general documentation terminology is reasonable, but offering a clinical interpretation of findings exceeds a HIM professional's scope of practice regardless of the personal relationship involved, and genuine clinical questions should be directed to her physician. Declining to discuss the document at all is an overcorrection when general terminology help is appropriate. Reassuring the friend that findings are probably not serious risks giving inaccurate clinical guidance outside her expertise.
  143. The correct answer is A (Automated flagging tools should support, than replace, human review given the clinical judgment coding audits require.): Coding audits often require nuanced clinical judgment that current automated tools support but do not fully replace, so a vendor's claim of fully replacing human auditors should be treated with appropriate skepticism and the tool positioned as a support mechanism rather than a full substitute for human review. Adopting the tool exactly as marketed risks over-reliance on an unproven capability. Dismissing marketing claims as unimportant ignores that they could shape unrealistic organizational expectations about the tool's actual capability.
  144. The correct answer is B (Recommend a role based access agreement limiting the vendor to only data needed for billing.): Third party access, like internal access, should follow the minimum necessary principle, and a properly scoped role based agreement allows the legitimate billing function to proceed while limiting unnecessary exposure of unrelated patient information. Granting unrestricted access purely for efficiency, or relying on a signed agreement alone without technical limits, both leave real privacy risk unaddressed. Outright denial ignores a legitimate business need that can be met with proper scoping.
  145. The correct answer is C (Research the terminology through approved clinical references and consult a supervisor or coding specialist as needed.): Maintaining competence means actively closing knowledge gaps through legitimate clinical resources and appropriate consultation rather than guessing or defaulting to an imprecise general code. Guessing based on superficial similarity to familiar terms risks a genuinely inaccurate diagnosis code. Simply avoiding the chart does not fulfill her professional responsibility to either complete or appropriately escalate the assignment.
  146. The correct answer is B (Support a formal amendment process that allows correction requests while preserving the original documented entry.): A proper amendment process allows patients a meaningful right to request correction of inaccurate information while preserving record integrity by retaining the original entry alongside any documented amendment, rather than allowing direct unrestricted editing. Opposing any correction process entirely ignores a legitimate patient right recognized in health information practice. Limiting corrections only to administrative details is too narrow since clinical inaccuracies can also legitimately need addressing through a proper amendment process.
  147. The correct answer is A (Include appropriate risk adjustment or context so the data reflects true performance differences than patient mix.): Ethical analytical reporting requires providing appropriate context, such as risk adjustment for patient complexity, so that comparative data like physician level readmission rates genuinely reflects performance differences rather than simply reflecting differences in the patients each physician treats. Publishing raw unadjusted rates risks an unfair and misleading impression. Selectively excluding unfavourable physicians while including favourable ones would itself be a form of biased, incomplete reporting.
  148. The correct answer is C (Refrain from accessing the chart since curiosity about a relative is not a legitimate purpose.): Confidentiality obligations apply regardless of family relationship or the small size of a community, and access must always be tied to an actual work related need to know rather than personal concern or curiosity. Asking a coworker to access it on her behalf does not resolve the underlying lack of a legitimate purpose and simply extends the improper access to another person. Even limited access to a single detail like an appointment date remains an unauthorized disclosure without a legitimate reason.
  149. The correct answer is D (Recommend narrowing the query to include only the data fields genuinely necessary for the research question.): Applying the minimum necessary principle to technical work such as query design protects patient privacy by limiting data exposure to what a project genuinely requires. Building the query as originally requested, even with a later manual cleanup, still creates unnecessary exposure during the extraction and review process. An outright refusal is not proportionate when a collaborative narrowing of scope would resolve the concern.
  150. The correct answer is D (Recommend the policy be updated to define appropriate access, storage, and sharing rules for photographs.): Clinical photographs are sensitive personal health information that deserve explicit access, storage, and sharing guidance within policy, since an undefined gap creates real risk of inappropriate access or disclosure that a general chart entry policy may not adequately anticipate. Assuming photographs need no special treatment overlooks their particularly sensitive and identifiable nature. Discontinuing a clinically useful practice entirely is a disproportionate response when a policy update can properly address the actual gap.
  151. The correct answer is A (Disclose the relationship to the committee and recuse herself from voting on the decision.): Managing conflicts of interest requires transparency and stepping back from decisions where personal relationships could influence, or appear to influence, professional judgment, even when the individual believes she is being objective. Voting without disclosure undermines trust regardless of the proposal's actual merit. A silent abstention still fails the disclosure obligation that allows the committee to properly evaluate the situation.
  152. The correct answer is C (Evaluate whether the specific combination of demographic elements could allow indirect patient identification.): Even data described as de-identified can become identifiable when combined with enough demographic detail, so the ethical review should specifically evaluate whether the proposed combination of elements creates a realistic re-identification risk before approving the arrangement. Approving based on the general category of data alone skips this necessary analysis. A blanket rejection of any marketing use may be overly broad if a properly de-identified, appropriately scoped dataset could be developed instead, and campaign effectiveness is not the relevant ethical question.
  153. The correct answer is B (Consult legal counsel or privacy policy to determine the appropriate scope of records to release.): Subpoenas are legal documents that must be responded to, but organizations typically have established privacy and legal review processes to determine the proper scope of disclosure and to protect against overexposure. Releasing the entire record without review or ignoring the subpoena entirely both fail to properly balance legal obligations against privacy protection. Personal judgment about relevance without legal input risks improper disclosure or improper withholding.
  154. The correct answer is A (Recommend suppressing or combining small sample size categories to reduce the risk of patient identification.): True de-identification requires addressing small cell sizes that create realistic re-identification risk, not just removing direct identifiers like name and address, so suppressing or combining categories with very few individuals is a recognized and appropriate safeguard. Publishing as is or adding a disclaimer does not actually reduce the underlying identification risk to patients. Abandoning legitimate research is unnecessary when a proper statistical disclosure control method can resolve the concern.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Buildup of fatty plaque narrowing the arterial lumen.): atherosclerosis develops as lipid deposits and fibrous tissue accumulate within arterial walls, progressively narrowing the vessel and restricting blood flow. Option A describes a venous condition, not an arterial one. Option B describes vasospasm, a temporary and different process. Option D describes superficial thrombophlebitis, an unrelated venous condition.
  2. The correct answer is D (Chaotic, disorganized electrical activity replaces coordinated atrial contraction.): in atrial fibrillation, multiple rapid and disorganized impulses fire throughout the atria, preventing effective coordinated contraction and producing an irregularly irregular rhythm. Option A describes a different arrhythmia pattern entirely. Option B describes normal sinus conduction, the opposite of fibrillation. Option C describes sinus bradycardia, an unrelated rhythm disturbance.
  3. The correct answer is A (The left ventricle, which must generate more force against elevated resistance.): chronically elevated systemic vascular resistance forces the left ventricle to pump harder, eventually leading to hypertrophy and heart failure if untreated. Option B describes a chamber not primarily stressed by systemic afterload. Option C describes a conduction structure unrelated to mechanical workload. Option D describes a non-contractile membrane, not a chamber under load.
  4. The correct answer is B (The aorta, just distal to the aortic valve.): the right and left coronary arteries branch off the ascending aorta immediately above the aortic valve to supply the myocardium with oxygenated blood. Option A describes the origin of pulmonary circulation, not the coronaries. Option C describes a venous structure returning blood to the heart. Option D also describes a venous structure, not an arterial origin.
  5. The correct answer is D (The volume of blood ejected by a ventricle with each single beat.): stroke volume specifically measures how much blood one ventricle pumps out per contraction, and it directly contributes to cardiac output. Option A describes total blood volume, an unrelated measurement. Option B combines unrelated variables that do not define stroke volume. Option C describes blood pressure, a distinct hemodynamic parameter.
  6. The correct answer is C (The failing heart cannot maintain adequate forward flow, causing blood to back up.): when the ventricles fail to pump efficiently, pressure builds backward into the pulmonary and systemic venous systems, driving fluid into the lungs and tissues. Option A misattributes the mechanism to the kidneys rather than the heart. Option B describes a separate lymphatic pathology unrelated to heart failure. Option D ignores the primary cardiac mechanism entirely.
  7. The correct answer is A (The pulmonary arteries, carrying blood toward the lungs.): unlike systemic arteries, the pulmonary arteries carry deoxygenated blood from the right ventricle to the lungs for gas exchange. Option B describes pulmonary veins, which actually carry oxygenated blood. Option C describes renal arteries, which carry oxygenated systemic blood. Option D describes coronary veins, a venous structure not testing the unique arterial exception this question addresses.
  8. The correct answer is D (Venous stasis, endothelial injury, and hypercoagulability acting together.): Virchow's triad describes the three classic contributing factors to venous thrombus formation taught throughout biomedical science curricula. Option A lists metabolic risk factors associated with atherosclerosis rather than thrombosis specifically. Option B lists unrelated systemic findings not part of the classic triad. Option C lists vital sign abnormalities unrelated to clot formation mechanisms.
  9. The correct answer is B (The fetal lungs, which are not yet used for gas exchange.): the ductus arteriosus shunts blood from the pulmonary artery directly into the aorta, bypassing the non-functioning fetal lungs before birth. Option A describes an organ not bypassed by this particular structure. Option C describes the ductus venosus, a different fetal shunt entirely. Option D describes an organ unrelated to fetal circulatory shunting.
  10. The correct answer is C (Passive diffusion of dissolved gases moving down their own concentration gradient.): oxygen and carbon dioxide move across the thin alveolar-capillary membrane by simple diffusion from areas of higher concentration to areas of lower concentration. Option A incorrectly describes an energy-requiring process not used for gas exchange. Option B describes a mechanism relevant to renal filtration, not alveolar gas exchange. Option D describes water movement, not the movement of respiratory gases.
  11. The correct answer is D (Emphysema destroys alveolar walls; chronic bronchitis inflames and narrows the airways.): emphysema involves destruction of alveolar septa reducing surface area, while chronic bronchitis involves chronic airway inflammation and excess mucus production. Option B reverses the two disease mechanisms incorrectly. Option C misattributes both diseases to the pleura rather than lung tissue and airways. Option A misattributes both diseases to the upper airway rather than the lower respiratory tract.
  12. The correct answer is A (The right main bronchus, due to its more vertical angle.): the right main bronchus is shorter, wider, and more vertically oriented than the left, making aspirated objects more likely to travel there. Option B incorrectly assigns the steeper angle to the left side. Option C ignores the well-documented anatomical asymmetry between the two bronchi. Option D ignores that foreign bodies typically pass beyond the trachea into a bronchus.
  13. The correct answer is B (Turbulent airflow moving through bronchioles narrowed by bronchospasm and inflammation.): asthma causes airway smooth muscle constriction, mucosal swelling, and mucus production, and air moving through these narrowed passages produces the characteristic wheeze. Option A describes a pleural effusion, a different and unrelated finding. Option C describes atelectasis, which does not itself produce a wheezing sound. Option D describes bullae formation seen in emphysema, not the acute wheeze of asthma.
  14. The correct answer is C (Reduced oxygen concentration dissolved in the arterial blood.): hypoxemia specifically describes low arterial oxygen levels as measured on blood gas analysis, distinct from tissue-level hypoxia. Option B describes a different gas and the wrong blood source entirely. Option A describes anemia, a separate laboratory finding. Option D describes hypoglycemia in a different body fluid altogether.
  15. The correct answer is A (Air trapped within the pleural space causing lung collapse.): pneumothorax occurs when air enters the potential space between the visceral and parietal pleura, collapsing the underlying lung. Option B describes cardiac tamponade, a different traumatic finding. Option C describes pneumonia with consolidation, an unrelated process. Option D describes ascites, occurring in a completely different body cavity.
  16. The correct answer is D (Soft tissue in the upper airway repeatedly collapses and blocks airflow.): obstructive sleep apnea results from relaxation of pharyngeal muscles during sleep, allowing soft tissue to obstruct the airway despite continued respiratory effort. Option A describes central sleep apnea, a distinct diagnosis with a different mechanism. Option C describes an unrelated neuromuscular condition, not typical sleep apnea. Option B describes a cardiac finding unrelated to airway obstruction.
  17. The correct answer is B (The volume of air forcibly exhaled in the first second of a forced breath.): FEV1 stands for forced expiratory volume in one second, a key spirometry measure used to assess airway obstruction. Option A describes total lung capacity, a different spirometric measurement. Option C describes respiratory rate, an unrelated vital sign. Option D describes pulse oximetry, a separate diagnostic tool entirely.
  18. The correct answer is D (Surfactant, which normally reduces surface tension within the alveoli.): premature infants often lack adequate surfactant production, causing alveolar collapse and increased work of breathing. Option A describes mucus, which plays no role in this particular condition. Option C describes hemoglobin, an oxygen-carrying protein unrelated to surfactant deficiency. Option B describes bicarbonate, a buffering substance unrelated to alveolar surface tension.
  19. The correct answer is A (They provide further specificity, such as laterality, severity, or anatomical detail, beyond the three-character category.): Additional characters beyond the three-character category level in ICD-10-CA add further clinical specificity, which may include details like anatomical site, laterality, or clinical stage, depending on the chapter and category involved. These characters carry real classification meaning and are required where applicable, not optional decoration, and they have no relationship to an employee identification number or a patient's postal code.
  20. The correct answer is A (A condition still being investigated or ruled out at discharge may be coded as if it were confirmed, following the applicable coding guideline for uncertain diagnoses.): Coding standards in the Canadian inpatient context generally direct coders to code a documented condition that was still being investigated, or was to be 'ruled out,' as though it existed, provided it meets applicable coding guidelines, since this often reflects the resources used during the stay. This differs from how a real-time clinical terminology might represent diagnostic uncertainty. Coding an uncertain diagnosis does not require a second physician's confirmation or written patient consent, which are unrelated to this classification convention.
  21. The correct answer is B (The most responsible diagnosis, as understood within Canadian health information management practice.): The most responsible diagnosis refers to the condition identified, after investigation during the stay, as primarily responsible for the patient's need for hospital admission, and it is generally sequenced as the main diagnosis in Canadian inpatient abstracting and coding. A differential diagnosis refers to a list of possibilities being considered, a nursing diagnosis reflects a nursing care framework rather than coded classification, and a provisional diagnosis is a preliminary impression rather than the confirmed condition used for sequencing.
  22. The correct answer is B (Diagnoses and interventions represent conceptually different types of information.): Diagnoses describe why a patient sought care, while interventions describe what was actually done, and these are conceptually distinct types of clinical information that benefit from being classified, structured, and maintained separately according to their own logical frameworks. Keeping them separate does not prevent funding calculations, since funding models are specifically designed to combine data from both classifications, there is no such blanket legal prohibition, and the two systems were in fact developed to be used together in Canadian abstracting.
  23. The correct answer is B (They represent supplementary words that may or may not be present in the diagnostic statement without changing the code assigned.): Parenthetical terms in the Alphabetical Index are non-essential modifiers, meaning they may or may not appear in the actual clinical documentation without changing which code applies, since the main term and its essential modifiers already determine the correct code. They are not instructions to alter documentation wording, do not indicate discontinuation, and do not communicate anything about the code's original publication year.
  24. The correct answer is A (Assign the three-character code as reported, a point worth noting here.): When a category has no further subdivision available in the Tabular List, the three-character code is the correct and complete code to assign, since it already reflects the maximum specificity the classification provides for that concept. Adding an arbitrary character, refusing to code a legitimately documented and relevant diagnosis, or substituting an unrelated code would all introduce inaccurate or incomplete data into the record.
  25. The correct answer is A (Standardized notes promote consistent code assignment across different coders, facilities, and time periods.): Standardized instructional notes exist precisely so that different coders, working in different facilities and at different times, apply the classification in a consistent way, which is essential for producing comparable, trustworthy national data. This does not mean clinical judgment plays no role, since coders still interpret documentation and apply notes appropriately, but the notes themselves are not decorative padding, and they are developed and maintained nationally, not invented independently by individual hospitals.
  26. The correct answer is B (Elements such as the section, the body system or site involved.): CCI's multi-axial structure builds each code from several meaningful components, generally including elements such as the section of the classification, the body system or anatomical site involved, and the type of intervention performed, along with further qualifying detail. This structure allows for consistent, detailed classification of a wide range of interventions. It has no relationship to a patient's personal or financial characteristics, staffing levels, or hospital signage.
  27. The correct answer is D (A qualifying characteristic or attribute component of the code.): CCI codes include qualifying characteristics or attributes that capture important procedural detail, such as the approach used to perform an intervention, for example whether it was open or minimally invasive. This level of detail supports more precise reporting of how a procedure was actually carried out. Administrative details like an admission date, a physician's licence number, or a facility's postal code are not part of the CCI code structure itself.
  28. The correct answer is D (It was built to reflect Canadian clinical practice patterns and to work in conjunction with ICD-10-CA.): CCI was purpose-built in Canada to reflect the range of interventions performed within the Canadian health care system and to function alongside ICD-10-CA as a complementary classification within the national abstracting and reporting framework maintained by CIHI. It is used across publicly funded facilities, is not limited to dental procedures, and does not eliminate the ongoing need for physicians to document the care and interventions they provide.
  29. The correct answer is C (CCI was intended to capture the full range of health interventions performed, not just surgery.): CCI was deliberately designed with a broad scope covering diagnostic, therapeutic, and surgical interventions, among other categories, so that the classification can represent the full range of procedures performed during an episode of care, not surgery alone. This comprehensive scope is a defining design feature, and CCI is not restricted to infection control activities or limited to a specific patient age group.
  30. The correct answer is D (Confirming the selected diagnosis code against the Tabular List's instructional notes before finalizing it.): Just as a coder verifies a diagnosis code selection against the Tabular List and its instructional notes in ICD-10-CA, a coder assigning a CCI code similarly reviews the classification's own instructional notes and conventions to ensure the intervention code accurately and completely reflects the documented procedure. This verification step is unrelated to a patient's address, a coder's individual performance review, or hospital-level budget matters.
  31. The correct answer is D (CCI was developed later and structured to be flexible enough to accommodate evolving techniques and technologies.): CCI was developed more recently, using a flexible, multi-axial structure specifically intended to accommodate the ongoing evolution of clinical techniques and technologies, including newer minimally invasive approaches, more effectively than an older classification structure could. CCI does not exclude newer procedures based on a cutoff date, is not limited to pre-2000 interventions, and does not require purchasing an additional classification each time a new technology is introduced.
  32. The correct answer is B (ICD-10-CA for the diagnoses and CCI for the interventions performed.): For a Canadian inpatient stay, ICD-10-CA is used to code the diagnoses documented during the encounter, while CCI is used to code the interventions or procedures that were performed, and together these two classifications provide a complete coded picture of the episode of care for national reporting. The other pairings either reverse these roles or substitute unrelated system names that do not serve this coding function.
  33. The correct answer is C (Each distinct intervention performed is generally assigned its own appropriate CCI code.): When a patient undergoes multiple distinct interventions during a stay, applicable Canadian coding standards generally direct the coder to assign a separate, appropriate CCI code for each intervention performed, so that the record fully reflects the care provided. There is no rule limiting coding to only the first intervention, averaging separate procedures into a single code is not a valid coding practice, and there is no fixed limit of three interventions per stay.
  34. The correct answer is D (An attribute or qualifier that adds further descriptive detail to the base intervention code.): CCI code structures include attributes or qualifiers that add further descriptive detail beyond the base intervention, such as the extent of tissue or structure involved, which supports a more precise and clinically meaningful classification of the procedure performed. This kind of structural detail has no relationship to a patient's blood type, a hospital's fiscal year, or an individual coder's certification credentials.
  35. The correct answer is B (It allows CIHI and health system planners to compare intervention volumes, patterns.): Standardized CCI coding ensures that intervention data means the same thing across every facility submitting it, which allows CIHI and health system planners to reliably aggregate, compare, and analyze intervention volumes, patterns, complications, and trends across the country and over time. It does not permit facility-specific private code meanings, does not dictate a uniform clinical approach for every patient, and does not eliminate the clinician's responsibility to document the procedure performed.
  36. The correct answer is D (The Canadian Institute for Health Information, a point worth noting here.): The Canadian Institute for Health Information, known as CIHI, is the national body responsible for developing, maintaining, and periodically updating both ICD-10-CA and CCI, along with providing coding standards and educational resources to support their consistent use across the country. The World Health Organization maintains the base international ICD classification but not the Canadian enhancements, and a provincial finance ministry or a medical protective association play no role in maintaining these classifications.
  37. The correct answer is B (CIHI compiles, validates, and analyzes the data to produce national statistics, reports.): CIHI compiles data submitted from facilities across the country, validates it for quality, and analyzes it to produce national and jurisdictional statistics, reports, and comparative indicators that support health system planning, funding models, and research. CIHI does not delete submitted data upon receipt, does not forward identified data to insurers without safeguards, and does not generate individual patient billing, none of which reflect its actual mandate.
  38. The correct answer is D (The Discharge Abstract Database, commonly referred to as the DAD.): The Discharge Abstract Database, or DAD, is the CIHI database that collects abstracted administrative, clinical, and demographic information, including ICD-10-CA and CCI codes, from acute inpatient hospital stays across most of Canada. A national physician database, a provincial vital statistics registry, and an immunization registry are separate data holdings serving different purposes and do not describe this particular submission stream.
  39. The correct answer is B (National statistics and comparisons are only as reliable as the accuracy and consistency of the coded data submitted by facilities.): Because CIHI's national statistics, funding models, and comparative reports are built directly from the coded data that individual facilities submit, the accuracy and consistency of that facility-level coding directly determines how reliable and meaningful the resulting national picture is. CIHI does not re-code every submitted record from scratch, does not discard the majority of submissions in favour of a small sample, and its national output is fundamentally dependent on, not independent of, facility submissions.
  40. The correct answer is A (To promote consistent, accurate application of the classifications by coders across different facilities and jurisdictions.): CIHI's coding standards and educational materials exist to support coders in applying ICD-10-CA and CCI consistently and accurately, which helps ensure that data submitted from different facilities and provinces remains comparable at the national level. These resources supplement rather than replace the classifications themselves, and they have no role in setting professional salary scales or determining insurance eligibility, both of which fall outside CIHI's mandate.
  41. The correct answer is A (CIHI, drawing on standardized data such as the Discharge Abstract Database.): CIHI compiles standardized, comparable coded data, such as diagnosis and intervention information from the Discharge Abstract Database, from facilities across the country, which makes it the appropriate source for cross-regional comparisons like average length of stay for a given procedure. A hospital's internal human resources department, a pharmaceutical marketing firm, and a single physician's office do not maintain this kind of national, standardized comparative health data.
  42. The correct answer is D (CIHI's coded and grouped data, which some provinces use as an input to hospital funding formulas.): In provinces that use activity-based or case mix funding approaches, the coded diagnosis and intervention data submitted to CIHI, and the case mix groups derived from that data, can inform how resources are allocated across facilities based on the complexity and volume of care provided. CIHI does not unilaterally set hospital budgets independent of provincial ministries, is not unrelated to funding methodology, and its data holdings are not limited to private clinics.
  43. The correct answer is A (The use of standardized, nationally consistent classifications like ICD-10-CA and CCI across contributing facilities and provinces.): Because facilities across Canada apply the same standardized classifications, ICD-10-CA for diagnoses and CCI for interventions, using shared coding standards, the resulting data can be meaningfully compared and combined across years and jurisdictions, which is what makes national longitudinal research and trend analysis possible. This capability would not exist if facilities used their own invented codes, if CIHI discarded historical data after a single year, or if submission were rare and inconsistent, none of which reflect how the system actually operates.
  44. The correct answer is A (Responsibly collecting, managing, protecting, and making available health data for legitimate secondary uses such as planning.): CIHI's stewardship role centres on responsibly collecting, safeguarding, and making available standardized health information to support legitimate secondary uses, including health system planning, policy development, research, and public reporting, while respecting privacy obligations. This role does not extend to controlling individual patient care decisions, replacing provincial funding responsibilities, or directly employing health information professionals across the country, none of which fall within CIHI's actual mandate.
  45. The correct answer is B (Combining clinical and administrative data supports a more complete analysis of resource use, patterns of care, and outcomes.): Combining coded clinical information with administrative details, such as dates and length of stay, within a single abstract allows CIHI and data users to analyze how clinical complexity relates to resource use and health system performance in a more complete way than either data type could support alone. CIHI does not use this information for direct patient marketing, and clinical and administrative data are routinely analyzed together rather than being unrelated or legally barred from combination.
  46. The correct answer is C (Hospital departments generate and submit standardized coded and abstracted data.): In this ecosystem, hospital health information management departments are responsible for producing standardized, coded, and abstracted data from patient encounters, which they then submit to CIHI, and CIHI in turn aggregates, validates, and analyzes this data to produce national statistics and reports. CIHI does not perform the coding on-site at facilities itself, hospital departments do maintain an ongoing reporting relationship with CIHI, and data submission flows through facilities rather than directly from individual patients.
  47. The correct answer is D (All contributing jurisdictions apply the same national classifications and coding standards when abstracting their data.): Because jurisdictions across Canada apply the same national classifications, ICD-10-CA and CCI, along with shared coding standards, when abstracting data for national databases, information such as same-day surgery volumes can be meaningfully compared across provinces. Provinces are not required to translate data into differing classifications, CIHI's national reports routinely combine multiple jurisdictions, and same-day surgery activity is in fact a recognized area of national reporting, not an excluded category.
  48. The correct answer is B (Accuracy, meaning how closely recorded data matches the true clinical facts documented.): Accuracy refers to how closely coded or recorded data reflects the true clinical reality documented in the source record, so a strong match between coded diagnoses and chart documentation directly supports this dimension. Timeliness concerns how quickly data becomes available, comparability concerns whether data can be meaningfully compared across sources, and relevance concerns whether the data actually meets the needs of its intended users, none of which are what this particular audit finding demonstrates.
  49. The correct answer is D (Data can be highly accurate for one facility yet still fail to be useful if it arrives too late or cannot be compared across facilities.): Even highly accurate data can fail to serve its purpose if it arrives too late to inform decisions, cannot be meaningfully compared across facilities due to inconsistent coding practices, or does not address the actual information needs of its intended users, which is why data quality frameworks evaluate multiple dimensions together rather than accuracy in isolation. These dimensions are not restricted to financial audits, nor are comparability and timeliness limited to data collected outside Canada.
  50. The correct answer is D (21.5%.): Net autopsy rate excludes coroner or medical examiner cases from the denominator because the hospital had no authority to perform those autopsies: 28 ÷ (140 − 10) × 100 = 21.5%. This produces a higher rate than the gross autopsy rate calculated on the same data because it more fairly reflects the hospital's own autopsy performance on cases it actually controlled.

The correct answer is C (No, the physiotherapist should access only information relevant to the shoulder treatment.): Consent and the circle of care are generally understood to be purpose-limited, meaning access should be confined to information reasonably necessary for the specific care being delivered, in this case the shoulder injury. Browsing unrelated mental health notes out of curiosity falls outside that purpose and is not supported by the consent the patient gave for physiotherapy treatment. This is an example of impermissible access even though the physiotherapist is a legitimate provider with some connection to the patient. Role-based access controls and minimum-necessary principles exist precisely to prevent this kind of purpose creep.
  95. The correct answer is B (A patient may generally withdraw or vary consent at any time.): Most provincial health privacy statutes recognize that a patient may withdraw or modify their consent prospectively at any time, meaning future uses or disclosures must respect the new instruction. However, withdrawal generally does not retroactively undo disclosures that were properly made before the withdrawal took effect, nor does it require destruction of the existing record, which custodians typically must still retain. Practical implementation, such as flagging a chart to reflect the withdrawal, is an important HIM responsibility. Patients should be informed that withdrawal may limit a custodian's ability to coordinate their future care effectively.
  96. The correct answer is C (Patients generally have a right of access to their own personal health information.): Canadian provincial health privacy and access statutes generally establish a patient's right to access their own personal health information held by a custodian, reflecting the principle that individuals should be able to see and understand records about themselves. This right is not absolute, however, and is subject to specific, narrowly defined exceptions set out in the applicable legislation. The custodian typically must respond within a defined statutory timeframe and provide reasons if access is refused in whole or in part. HIM professionals play a central role in processing these requests correctly.
  97. The correct answer is B (Most statutes permit refusing or limiting access to only the specific portion posing a genuine risk.): Most provincial health privacy statutes include a narrow exception allowing a custodian to withhold access to a specific portion of a record where disclosure could reasonably be expected to result in a serious risk of harm to the individual's own health or safety, or to a third party. This exception is meant to be applied narrowly and to the specific harmful portion, not as a blanket basis to withhold an entire chart. The custodian must still provide the remainder of the record that does not pose this risk. Documentation of the clinical rationale for any refusal is essential.
  98. The correct answer is A (It may generally be severed or redacted from the disclosed record to protect the third party's own privacy.): When a record containing a patient's own personal health information also contains identifying information about a third party that is not itself the requesting patient's information, most access regimes allow the custodian to sever or redact the third party's information while still disclosing the patient's own record. This balances the patient's right of access against the third party's separate privacy interest in information that is genuinely about them. It does not require denying the entire request, only limiting disclosure of the specific third-party content. HIM staff performing redactions must be trained to identify this distinction accurately.
  99. The correct answer is D (Assess the requester's legal authority to act on the child's behalf under applicable family and access-to-information law.): Access to a minor's health record by a parent depends on that parent's legal authority to make decisions or access information on the child's behalf, which can be affected by custody arrangements, decision-making authority orders, or the child's own capacity to consent depending on age and maturity. The custodian must assess this legal status rather than assuming automatic access based solely on biological parenthood. This often requires reviewing supporting documentation such as a custody or parenting order. Getting this assessment wrong risks either improperly denying a legitimate parent or improperly disclosing a child's sensitive information to someone without authority.
  100. The correct answer is C (Disclosure could reasonably be expected to result in a risk of serious harm to the treatment.): A recognized exception across most provincial health privacy statutes allows a custodian to refuse or limit access where disclosure could reasonably be expected to result in a risk of serious harm to the treatment or recovery of the individual, or to the safety of the individual or a third party. This exception must be applied on a genuine, case-specific clinical assessment rather than a general policy preference. Record length and age are not recognized statutory grounds for refusal on their own. Custodians must be prepared to justify any refusal with specific reasons tied to a recognized exception.
  101. The correct answer is B (Generally no, purely internal administrative or quality assurance information that is not about the identifiable individual.): Provincial access-to-own-information rights generally apply to personal health information that is about the identifiable individual, such as their own diagnostic results, notes, and treatment history. Purely internal administrative, technical, or quality assurance data that does not relate specifically to the patient, such as instrument calibration logs, generally falls outside the scope of what the patient is entitled to access as part of their own record. The patient's own test results derived from that data, however, remain fully accessible. Distinguishing between the patient's own information and unrelated operational records requires careful review by HIM staff processing the request.
  102. The correct answer is B (To recover reasonable costs of locating, copying, and providing the record, not to generate profit.): Provincial statutes that permit access fees generally limit them to reasonable cost recovery for activities such as staff time locating the record, photocopying, and administrative processing, rather than allowing the custodian to profit from the request or use fees as a deterrent. Some jurisdictions cap fees or require a fee estimate to be provided to the patient in advance. Fee schedules are often set out in regulation and must be applied consistently. Excessive or punitive fees can themselves be challenged as inconsistent with the purpose of the access right.
  103. The correct answer is C (The custodian must generally provide written reasons for the refusal and information about the patient's right to challenge or appeal the decision.): Provincial access regimes generally require a custodian who refuses access, in whole or in part, to provide the requester with written reasons for the refusal and information about how to challenge that decision, typically through a complaint to the applicable privacy oversight body or a formal review process. This transparency requirement ensures patients are not left without recourse when access is limited. Simply ignoring a request or failing to explain the basis for refusal is generally inconsistent with these statutory obligations. HIM departments typically use standardized response templates to help meet these requirements consistently.
  104. The correct answer is B (A request for non-identifying aggregate information is generally handled differently.): A request for de-identified, aggregate statistical information is conceptually different from an individual patient exercising a personal right of access to their own identifiable health information, and it is often processed under a public institution's general freedom of information or access-to-information legislation rather than the personal health information access provisions. Because the requested information does not identify individuals, different considerations around personal privacy protection apply, though the custodian must still ensure genuine de-identification before release. This distinction matters for HIM professionals who may handle both types of requests using different processes and timelines. Aggregate requests are not automatically denied but are assessed under their own applicable framework.
  105. The correct answer is C (Patients are generally entitled to reasonable access, and many custodians can accommodate a request to review.): The right of access generally focuses on ensuring the patient can meaningfully see and understand their own information, and many custodians can reasonably accommodate a preference to review the record in person, sometimes with a clinician present to help explain entries, rather than insisting on a specific format such as mailed photocopies. Reasonable arrangements, such as scheduling and supervision to protect the physical record, are appropriate. This flexibility supports patient understanding, particularly for complex clinical notes. The custodian should still document that access was provided and address any subsequent request for a copy.
  106. The correct answer is A (No, uncertainty about accuracy is not itself a recognized exception to access.): Doubt about whether an entry is fully accurate is not one of the narrow, recognized exceptions to a patient's right of access under provincial health privacy legislation, so the record should generally still be disclosed. Instead, most statutes provide a separate mechanism allowing the patient to request a correction to inaccurate information, and if the custodian disagrees with the requested correction, to have a statement of disagreement attached to the record. This preserves both the integrity of the original clinical documentation and the patient's ability to flag a concern. HIM professionals should be familiar with both the access right and this related correction process.
  107. The correct answer is B (Most statutes set a defined response period, often around thirty days.): Most provincial health privacy and access statutes establish a defined statutory timeframe for responding to an access request, commonly around thirty days, to ensure requests are handled promptly rather than indefinitely delayed. Many statutes also permit a limited extension in defined circumstances, such as a large volume of records or the need to consult with a third party, provided the requester is notified of the extension and reasons. Failing to respond within the applicable period can itself be treated as a deemed refusal in some jurisdictions, triggering a right to complain. HIM departments typically track these deadlines closely to remain compliant.
  108. The correct answer is A (The custodian may decline to change the entry but generally must allow the patient to have a statement of disagreement attached to the record.): When a custodian, after genuine review, disagrees that a challenged entry is inaccurate, most provincial statutes do not require deletion or alteration of the original clinical documentation, since that could compromise the integrity of the record and the treating provider's professional judgment. Instead, the patient is generally entitled to have a statement of disagreement attached to or associated with the record, ensuring their objection is visible to anyone who subsequently reviews the entry. This approach balances the patient's concern with preserving an accurate historical account of what was actually documented at the time. The custodian should still explain its reasoning for declining the requested correction.
  109. The correct answer is C (Confirm the scope of the request with the patient and coordinate retrieval across the relevant facilities.): Rather than refusing a broad but legitimate request, the appropriate approach is to clarify its exact scope with the patient if needed and then coordinate internally to retrieve the relevant records, particularly where facilities operate under a shared custodian or regional health authority structure that may already support centralized access processing. Requiring the patient to submit numerous separate requests, absent a genuine legal or organizational reason, creates unnecessary burden inconsistent with a meaningful right of access. Fees, where applicable, should reflect actual reasonable costs rather than an arbitrary maximum. Good coordination reflects sound HIM practice in multi-site systems.
  110. The correct answer is D (Generally yes, since it forms part of the patient's own personal health information.): Clinical correspondence discussing a patient's diagnosis and treatment, even when candidly worded, generally forms part of the patient's own personal health information and falls within their right of access, since the correspondence is directly about them and created in the course of their care. It is not automatically exempt simply because it reflects internal clinical deliberation between providers. A narrow exception could apply if disclosure of a specific portion would create a genuine risk of serious harm, but this must be individually assessed rather than presumed for all clinical correspondence. Clinicians should be mindful that their documented reasoning may ultimately be seen by the patient.
  111. The correct answer is C (Treat the substitute decision maker as generally standing in the patient's shoes for the purpose.): Provincial health privacy statutes generally allow a validly authorized substitute decision maker, such as an attorney for personal care, to exercise many of the incapable patient's rights, including the right to access their health information, once the custodian verifies the authority is genuine and currently applicable. This ensures an incapable patient's interests can still be represented without requiring a separate court process for every routine request. The custodian should review the supporting documentation to confirm scope and validity before proceeding. This differs from a family member acting without any formal authority, who generally cannot exercise these rights on a capable patient's behalf.
  112. The correct answer is C (Personal health information access is built around an individual's own interest in their own sensitive information.): Personal health information access regimes are specifically designed around an individual's interest in seeing and controlling their own sensitive health records, with exceptions tailored to clinical risk and third-party health privacy. General freedom of information or access-to-information legislation, by contrast, is designed to promote transparency and accountability of public bodies more broadly, covering a much wider range of government records with its own separate set of exemptions. A hospital or health authority that is a public body may be subject to both frameworks depending on the nature of the request. HIM professionals need to correctly identify which framework governs a given request to apply the right process and exceptions.
  113. The correct answer is C (Litigation privilege may, in some circumstances, provide a narrow basis to withhold specific privileged material.): Litigation involving a patient does not, by itself, eliminate their underlying statutory right to access their own personal health information, though narrow legal principles such as litigation privilege may justify withholding specific materials genuinely created for the dominant purpose of the litigation. The custodian must still assess the request against the applicable statutory exceptions rather than assuming a blanket bar because litigation exists. This requires careful case-by-case review, often with input from legal counsel, to correctly identify what, if anything, is genuinely privileged. The remainder of the record not implicated by the privilege claim should still be processed under normal access rules.
  114. The correct answer is B (The custodian should make reasonable efforts to provide the record in an accessible format that meets the patient's needs.): Good health information management practice, informed by both privacy legislation and broader accessibility obligations, generally expects a custodian to make reasonable efforts to provide requested records in an accessible format, such as large print, audio description, or another suitable alternative, where feasible given the custodian's resources and systems. This reflects the underlying purpose of the access right, which is to allow the patient to meaningfully understand information about their own care, not merely to technically satisfy a paperwork requirement. Ignoring an accessibility need or imposing a punitive fee would undermine that purpose. Many organizations have specific accessibility policies that HIM staff should follow when processing such requests.
  115. The correct answer is D (Inform the patient that the record cannot be located despite a reasonable search.): When a genuine, reasonable search fails to locate a requested record, most provincial access regimes still require the custodian to respond transparently to the patient, explaining that the record cannot be located and outlining the patient's right to pursue a complaint with the relevant oversight body if they believe the search was inadequate. Simply closing the file silently or fabricating information would be a serious breach of both legal and ethical obligations. This situation also often triggers an internal review of records management practices to understand how the record was lost. Honest, documented communication protects both the patient's rights and the custodian's own accountability.
  116. The correct answer is B (Comprehensiveness, meaning coverage of all medically necessary hospital and physician services.): Comprehensiveness explicitly extends insured service coverage to certain surgical-dental services that must be performed in a hospital, in addition to hospital and physician services generally. This reflects that comprehensiveness is defined by the type and setting of the service rather than the specific title of the provider delivering it. Most dental care outside a hospital setting remains outside the scope of Canada Health Act insured services.
  117. The correct answer is D (Accessibility, meaning reasonable access to insured services free of financial barriers.): Accessibility is directly threatened when patients are charged an additional facility fee for an insured diagnostic service, since this constitutes extra billing that creates a financial barrier to care already covered under the public plan. The Canada Health Act's enforcement mechanism reduces federal transfers to provinces that permit such charges dollar for dollar. This scenario does not concern who administers the plan or whether coverage varies by residency status, so public administration and universality are less directly implicated.
  118. The correct answer is B (Health care delivery falls primarily under provincial jurisdiction.): Canada's Constitution assigns responsibility for the organization and delivery of health care services to the provinces and territories, which is why the federal government influences the system mainly through funding conditions in the Canada Health Act rather than by directly operating hospitals or employing physicians. The federal government retains direct responsibility for certain specific populations and areas, such as First Nations on reserve, federal inmates, and the Canadian Armed Forces. Understanding this division is essential to correctly answering questions about who is accountable for what in the Canadian health system.
  119. The correct answer is D (Universality, meaning uniform entitlement to insured services for every eligible resident.): Universality requires that insured services be provided to all eligible residents on uniform terms and conditions, so applying a different reimbursement rate based solely on where within the province a person lives would create unequal entitlement among residents. This is a geography-based inequity in entitlement rather than a question about whether the service itself is covered, which would instead implicate comprehensiveness. Uniform application of coverage terms is central to how universality is tested on certification exams.
  120. The correct answer is C (A province must satisfy all five principles to receive its full federal cash contribution.): the Canada Health Act ties full receipt of the federal cash contribution to compliance with all five principles together, and non-compliance, such as permitting extra billing, can result in dollar-for-dollar deductions from that transfer. This gives the federal government a financial mechanism to encourage adherence to national standards despite provinces holding constitutional responsibility for delivery. No single principle alone satisfies the conditions required for full funding.
  121. The correct answer is C (Insured persons must have reasonable access to insured services unimpeded by financial or other barriers.): accessibility requires that reasonable access to insured health services not be impeded by financial charges, such as extra billing and user fees, or by unreasonable barriers such as excessive distance where alternatives are not reasonably available. It also requires that reasonable compensation be provided to physicians and other practitioners for insured services. This principle is frequently the one implicated in extra-billing and user-charge scenarios on the exam.
  122. The correct answer is A (Accessibility, meaning reasonable access to insured services free of financial barriers.): A co-payment charged directly to patients at the point of an insured physician visit is a user charge, which the Canada Health Act discourages because it creates a financial barrier to obtaining insured services, directly implicating accessibility. Such user charges trigger mandatory dollar-for-dollar deductions from a province's federal cash transfer under the Act's enforcement provisions. This scenario is a textbook example used to test recognition of accessibility violations distinct from coverage-scope issues.
  123. The correct answer is C (The federal government sets national conditions and funds care, while provinces organize and deliver it.): under the constitutional division of powers, provinces and territories hold primary responsibility for planning, financing within their own budgets, and delivering health services, while the federal government uses its spending power to attach national conditions, such as the five Canada Health Act principles, to transfer payments like the Canada Health Transfer. This arrangement produces a system that is often described as decentralized but nationally conditioned. CHIM professionals need to understand this split because it affects how funding, data reporting, and policy decisions flow through the system.
  124. The correct answer is B (The Canada Health Transfer, the main federal cash payment supporting provincial health budgets.): the Canada Health Transfer is the primary federal transfer payment that provides ongoing funding support to provinces and territories for health care, calculated on a per capita cash basis and subject to compliance with Canada Health Act conditions. It replaced the combined Canada Health and Social Transfer in 2004 when health and social funding were split into separate transfers. Understanding the CHT is essential for CHIM professionals working with health system funding data.
  125. The correct answer is C (First Nations on reserve, federal inmates, and members of the Canadian Armed Forces.): the federal government retains direct responsibility for delivering or funding health services to specific populations, including First Nations people living on reserve, inmates in federal penitentiaries, active members of the Canadian Armed Forces, and some groups such as refugee claimants and veterans, alongside its broader Canada Health Act funding role. This direct-delivery role is narrower than the provincial role, which covers the general population. Recognizing these federal populations helps distinguish federal versus provincial jurisdiction on the exam.
  126. The correct answer is C (The provincial government, which holds constitutional authority over health service delivery.): because delivery and organization of health services fall under provincial jurisdiction, provincial governments, typically through their ministries of health, make the operational decisions about how to allocate their health budgets across hospitals, community programs, and other services.
