The federal government's role is limited to setting national conditions through the Canada Health Act and providing transfer funding, not dictating specific provincial spending allocations. CIHI and PHAC play data and public health roles rather than budget allocation roles.
  127. The correct answer is D (An equal per capita cash basis, calculated according to each jurisdiction's population share.): the Canada Health Transfer is distributed to provinces and territories on an equal per capita cash basis, meaning funding is calculated according to each jurisdiction's population share. This differs from older cost-sharing arrangements that reimbursed provinces for a share of actual program spending. CHIM professionals should understand this funding mechanism because it underlies discussions of health system sustainability and interprovincial funding equity.
  128. The correct answer is A (A dollar-for-dollar reduction in its Canada Health Transfer cash contribution.): the Canada Health Act empowers the federal Minister of Health to withhold an amount equal to the extra billing or user charges permitted in a province from that province's Canada Health Transfer cash contribution. This financial penalty is the primary enforcement tool available to the federal government, since it cannot directly compel provincial delivery decisions. The mechanism preserves provincial jurisdiction while still incentivizing compliance with national principles.
  129. The correct answer is C (The provincial and territorial role in organizing and delivering health care.): licensing hospitals, setting regional health authority budgets, and negotiating physician fee schedules are all operational delivery and organization functions that fall to provincial and territorial ministries of health under Canada's constitutional division of powers. The federal role, by contrast, is centered on setting national funding conditions and delivering care to specific federal populations. This distinction is a foundational concept tested throughout the health care system domain.
  130. The correct answer is D (To improve transparency and accountability for how much funding was specifically directed to health care.): the combined Canada Health and Social Transfer made it difficult to track exactly how much federal funding provinces were directing toward health care versus social programs like education and social assistance, so the transfer was split in 2004 into the Canada Health Transfer and the Canada Social Transfer to improve visibility and accountability. This change allowed clearer public reporting on health-specific federal contributions. It did not change the constitutional division of delivery responsibility between the two levels of government.
  131. The correct answer is B (The Act sets minimum national conditions, but provinces retain authority over delivery details and fee schedules.): because the Canada Health Act establishes floor conditions rather than a uniform national delivery model, provinces retain significant discretion over physician fee schedules, wait time management, additional benefits beyond the required minimum, and how services are organized. This is why coverage for items like physiotherapy, optometry, or ambulance services can vary considerably between provinces. Understanding this discretion helps explain the variability CHIM professionals encounter across provincial health information systems.
  132. The correct answer is C (The federal government's ability to attach conditions to transfer payments even in areas of provincial jurisdiction.): federal spending power refers to the federal government's capacity to use its funding, such as the Canada Health Transfer, to attach conditions like the Canada Health Act principles, even though health care delivery itself is constitutionally a provincial responsibility. This is the mechanism that allows the federal government to maintain national standards without directly legislating delivery. It is a key concept for understanding how national consistency is achieved in a decentralized system.
  133. The correct answer is A (Health services for First Nations communities on reserve.): the federal government, through Indigenous Services Canada, holds direct responsibility for funding and, in many communities, delivering primary health care and public health services to First Nations people living on reserve, alongside some Inuit communities, reflecting treaty and historical obligations distinct from the provincial delivery model used for the general population. This is a notable exception to the general rule that provinces deliver care. Recognizing this exception is commonly tested in the federal versus provincial jurisdiction domain.
  134. The correct answer is B (Pursue lower cost or free continuing education options independently to meet her renewal requirement.): Maintaining certification and competence is a personal professional obligation that does not disappear when an employer declines to fund it, and many low cost or free continuing education resources are typically available through professional associations. Allowing certification to lapse or having someone else complete education under her name both fail to resolve the underlying obligation honestly. Taking independent initiative reflects the accountability expected of a certified professional.
  135. The correct answer is D (Insist that coding follow only what the clinical documentation actually supports, regardless of funding impact.): Coding integrity requires that codes reflect only what is clearly documented in the health record, and financial pressure to inflate acuity for higher reimbursement is a form of upcoding that violates professional ethics and often the law. Applying the higher code to a sample or leaving the decision inconsistent across coders does not resolve the underlying integrity violation. The coding manager must resist pressure that conflicts with documentation based coding.
  136. The correct answer is C (Immediately deactivate the account.): Timely deactivation of access upon termination is a fundamental safeguard, and discovering a lapse requires both immediate correction and a review of activity logs to rule out unauthorized access during the exposure window. Leaving the account active in any form defeats the purpose of access control entirely. Waiting for a scheduled review cycle is inappropriate once a known vulnerability has already been identified.
  137. The correct answer is C (Request formal training and be transparent with her supervisor about needing additional support to build competence.): Maintaining competence is an ongoing professional obligation, and transparency about a skills gap allows the organization to provide appropriate support before errors occur. Avoiding the system or having someone else perform the work conceals the gap rather than closing it and creates accountability and accuracy risks. Waiting to learn informally leaves patient information at risk during the interim period.
  138. The correct answer is A (Assess the scope and cause of the misdirected messages and report findings to appropriate leadership promptly.): A potential systemic misdirection of even seemingly minor information like appointment reminders still constitutes an unintended disclosure of personal health information, so the appropriate first step is a prompt assessment of scope and cause followed by reporting to leadership so corrective action and any needed patient notification can occur. Waiting for complaints is reactive rather than proactive given a known potential issue. Assuming the issue is minor without investigation risks underestimating a genuine privacy exposure.
  139. The correct answer is B (Investigate and resolve the underlying technical cause before the report is used for decisions.): A report producing inconsistent results without a clear explanation indicates an underlying technical or data integrity issue that should be investigated and resolved before the report is relied upon for any decision making, since undiagnosed inconsistency undermines confidence in the data's accuracy. Assuming variation is simply normal without investigation risks continued use of unreliable data. Averaging results or selecting the most favourable count both paper over a genuine unresolved data quality problem instead of fixing it.
  140. The correct answer is D (Vague consequences can undermine consistent, fair enforcement and reduce accountability across the organization.): Clear, consistently applied consequences for ethical violations support fair and predictable accountability, while excessive vagueness can lead to inconsistent enforcement that appears arbitrary or influenced by favouritism. While individual circumstances do vary, a reasonable structured framework can still accommodate that variation more fairly than leaving consequences entirely undefined. This concern applies regardless of organizational size.
  141. The correct answer is A (Report the issue according to established timelines and requirements regardless of the survey schedule.): Accountability and integrity require adhering to reporting obligations regardless of how the timing might affect an unrelated organizational event, since delaying a known accuracy issue prioritizes institutional image over transparency and patient data integrity. Any form of delay, partial disclosure, or vague reporting still misrepresents the true state of the issue at the time it should be reported. The director's obligation is to report honestly and on time.
  142. The correct answer is B (Raise the testing and accuracy concerns clearly with the committee before supporting deployment of the tool.): HIM professionals bring important expertise about data accuracy and its downstream effects on patient care and funding, and ethical participation on such committees includes raising legitimate concerns about inadequate testing before deployment. Deferring entirely to technical staff ignores this relevant expertise, and staying silent to avoid appearing resistant does not serve the goal of accurate, safe data practices. A private personal workaround does not address the risk to other users or patients.
  143. The correct answer is D (Participate honestly and objectively, disclosing any personal closeness that could affect her impartiality.): Professional integrity requires participating honestly in a legitimate integrity investigation while transparently disclosing any relationships that could affect impartiality, allowing investigators to determine whether additional safeguards or a different reviewer are needed. Declining entirely due to discomfort avoids a genuine professional responsibility. Intentionally narrowing the review's scope or concealing personal relationships both compromise the integrity of the investigation itself.
  144. The correct answer is C (Reporting adjusted rather than actual figures misrepresents the department's true coding practices to the study.): Reporting adjusted figures rather than the department's genuine coding practice data misrepresents reality to a study intended to produce accurate comparative insight, undermining the value of the benchmarking exercise for every participating facility. Approximation inherent to benchmarking does not excuse deliberately altering figures. Keeping internal records accurate while reporting different external figures does not resolve the misrepresentation to the study itself.
  145. The correct answer is C (Whether the arrangement includes adequate privacy safeguards and complies with cross border data protection rules.): A cross border arrangement involving identifiable patient information requires careful evaluation of contractual privacy safeguards and compliance with applicable data protection requirements across jurisdictions, since the location and legal environment of the transcriptionist can significantly affect how well patient information is actually protected. Price, turnaround time, and staff convenience are legitimate operational factors but do not address the core privacy and legal compliance question that must be resolved first.
  146. The correct answer is B (The diagnosis that most accurately reflects the primary reason for the admission based on the documentation.): Ethical coding practice requires selecting the principal diagnosis based on official coding guidelines and clinical documentation accuracy, never based on which technically defensible option yields the highest reimbursement. Choosing the highest paying option among multiple defensible choices is a subtle form of upcoding that compromises data integrity. Precedent from unrelated cases or convenience are also not appropriate criteria for this clinical judgment.
  147. The correct answer is D (Disclose the personal relationship to her supervisor and step back from directly conducting the investigation.): A close personal relationship with the subject of a privacy investigation creates a conflict of interest that should be disclosed so an appropriately independent person can conduct the review, regardless of the individual's confidence in her own objectivity. Conducting the investigation less thoroughly compromises its integrity and the patient's interest in a genuine investigation. Warning the friend in advance would improperly interfere with a legitimate investigative process.
  148. The correct answer is A (Report the findings accurately and completely regardless of any personal relationship with that department.): Integrity in analytical work requires reporting findings honestly and completely so that leadership can make informed decisions, regardless of personal relationships with those affected by the results. Softening language, omitting identifying details, or delaying disclosure all compromise the accuracy and usefulness of the report for its intended decision making purpose. Personal relationships must not influence professional analytical reporting.
  149. The correct answer is D (Raise the concern with the colleague and encourage use of the approved secure communication channel instead.): Using unsecured personal messaging applications to transmit patient information bypasses approved security safeguards and creates real privacy risk, regardless of the good intention of communicating quickly. Staying silent or adopting the same practice allows an unsafe habit to spread further within the department. Waiting for an actual harm to occur before addressing a known unsafe practice is not a proactive or protective approach to patient privacy.
  150. The correct answer is C (Request appropriate legal documentation establishing authority before releasing any portion of the record.): Confidentiality obligations continue after a patient's death, and release to a family member requires appropriate legal authority, such as documented status as the estate's executor or another legally authorized representative, not simply a family relationship on its own. Releasing a partial summary without proper authority still constitutes an unauthorized disclosure. A permanent blanket refusal is also incorrect since a properly authorized request can and should be honoured once appropriate documentation is provided.
  151. The correct answer is A (suspend the data flow and work with appropriate staff to renew or properly terminate the agreement.): An expired data sharing agreement means the legal and ethical basis for continued data transfer no longer exists, so the appropriate response is to immediately suspend the flow and then determine whether renewal is appropriate or termination is needed. Allowing the flow to continue informally relies on outdated terms that are no longer valid. Waiting for the external partner to raise the issue abdicates the organization's own responsibility to manage its data sharing obligations.
  152. The correct answer is B (Advocate for access controls limiting viewing to those with a genuine care relationship with the patient.): HIM professionals have an ethical responsibility to advocate for privacy protective design even within legitimate system wide improvements, ensuring that broader interoperability does not come at the cost of unnecessary exposure of patient information. Supporting unlimited broad access ignores real privacy risk, while opposing the entire project ignores genuine continuity of care benefits. Advocating for appropriately scoped access balances both goals.
  153. The correct answer is A (Honour the patient's specific restriction request wherever legally permitted while documenting any resulting care coordination limitations.): Patients generally have a right to request reasonable restrictions on sharing certain sensitive information where legally permitted, and an ethical policy should honour such requests within legal limits while transparently documenting any resulting care coordination considerations so the care team understands the limitation exists. An absolute denial ignores this legitimate patient right. Granting restrictions without any documentation of the resulting limitation could create confusion or safety gaps for future providers unaware of the restriction.
  154. The correct answer is D (Establish a single authoritative source for allergy information and reconcile the discrepancy promptly for patient safety.): Allergy information discrepancies carry direct patient safety implications, so establishing a single authoritative source and promptly reconciling any inconsistency is an urgent data integrity priority rather than something to defer to a future audit. Allowing both versions to persist simply because it has historically been the practice ignores the real safety risk. While patient input can help clarify a discrepancy, the department itself bears responsibility for maintaining consistent, accurate clinical information promptly.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (They sweep trapped mucus and debris upward and out of the airway.): ciliated cells lining the respiratory tract move in coordinated waves to clear mucus and trapped particles toward the pharynx for clearance. Option A describes a mechanical function of the diaphragm and chest wall, not cilia. Option B describes a function of type II alveolar cells, not ciliated epithelium. Option D describes autonomic nervous system regulation, unrelated to ciliary action.
  2. The correct answer is A (Helicobacter pylori, a bacterium colonizing the gastric mucosa.): this organism disrupts the protective mucous layer of the stomach and duodenum, contributing significantly to peptic ulcer formation. Option B describes a bacterium primarily associated with urinary infections, not ulcers. Option C describes a bacterium associated with colitis, a different gastrointestinal condition. Option D describes a fungal organism unrelated to typical peptic ulcer pathogenesis.
  3. The correct answer is B (The gallbladder, a small saclike organ located beneath the liver that stores bile.): the gallbladder stores and concentrates bile produced by the liver, releasing it into the duodenum in response to fatty meals. Option A describes the pancreas, which produces enzymes and hormones but does not store bile. Option C describes the spleen, an organ involved in blood filtration rather than digestion. Option D describes the appendix, a structure with no role in bile storage.
  4. The correct answer is D (Diffuse fibrosis and nodular regeneration replacing normal liver architecture.): cirrhosis represents the end stage of chronic liver injury, where normal tissue is replaced by scar tissue and regenerative nodules, impairing liver function. Option B describes a discrete benign lesion, not the diffuse process of cirrhosis. Option C describes an acute, reversible process, the opposite of chronic cirrhosis. Option A describes simple fatty liver alone without the fibrosis required to define cirrhosis.
  5. The correct answer is C (The lower esophageal sphincter, located between the esophagus and stomach.): when this sphincter fails to close properly, gastric contents can reflux backward into the esophagus, producing the symptoms characteristic of this disease. Option A describes a sphincter located further along the digestive tract, unrelated to reflux. Option B describes a valve between two intestinal segments, not involved in reflux. Option D describes a sphincter at the opposite end of the digestive tract entirely.
  6. The correct answer is A (Small outpouchings that herniate through weakened areas of the bowel wall.): diverticula form when mucosa and submucosa protrude through weak points in the muscular wall, most commonly in the sigmoid colon. Option B describes a solid neoplastic growth, a distinct pathology from diverticula. Option C describes perforation, a possible complication rather than the diverticula themselves. Option D describes a stricture, an unrelated structural abnormality.
  7. The correct answer is C (Digestive enzymes, normally activated only after reaching the duodenum.): pancreatitis occurs when digestive enzymes such as trypsin become prematurely activated within the pancreas itself, causing autodigestion and inflammation of pancreatic tissue. Option A describes bile acids, which originate from the liver, not the pancreas. Option B describes pancreatic hormones, which are unrelated to the enzymatic autodigestion process. Option D describes gastric acid, produced in the stomach rather than the pancreas.
  8. The correct answer is B (The cecum, the first portion of the large intestine.): the appendix is a small tubular structure attached to the cecum, and appendicitis develops when this structure becomes obstructed and inflamed. Option A describes a distal segment of the large intestine, not where the appendix attaches. Option C describes a small intestinal structure unrelated to appendiceal anatomy. Option D describes another colonic segment unrelated to the appendix's actual attachment point.
  9. The correct answer is D (Dietary fats, which require pancreatic lipase for proper digestion.): pancreatic lipase breaks down dietary triglycerides into absorbable fatty acids, so its deficiency leads to fat malabsorption and steatorrhea. Option A oversimplifies protein digestion, which also requires pancreatic enzymes beyond pepsin. Option B oversimplifies carbohydrate digestion, which also depends heavily on pancreatic amylase. Option C describes electrolytes, which are absorbed without the enzymatic step that is impaired here.
  10. The correct answer is A (Stones located specifically within the gallbladder rather than another digestive organ.): chole refers to bile or the gallbladder, lith refers to a stone, and iasis denotes a condition of presence, together describing gallstones. Option B describes pancreatitis, an entirely different condition and organ. Option C describes colonic polyps, unrelated to the biliary system. Option D describes a gastric ulcer, a separate digestive condition entirely.
  11. The correct answer is B (A benign adenomatous polyp undergoing progressive dysplastic change.): most colorectal cancers develop through the adenoma-carcinoma sequence, in which benign polyps accumulate genetic changes over time and become malignant. Option A ignores the well-established precursor lesion pathway. Option C describes a vascular anorectal structure unrelated to malignant transformation. Option D describes diverticular disease, a separate and generally unrelated condition.
  12. The correct answer is C (The nephron, composed of a glomerulus and renal tubule.): each kidney contains roughly one million nephrons, and each nephron filters blood plasma and processes the filtrate into urine through its tubular segments. Option B describes a transport structure carrying urine, not the filtering unit itself. Option A describes a collecting structure within the kidney, not the functional filtering unit. Option D describes an endocrine gland located near the kidney but structurally separate from it.
  13. The correct answer is D (Both are waste products normally cleared by glomerular filtration.): urea and creatinine are metabolic waste products that accumulate in the blood when the glomeruli filter less effectively, making them useful markers of kidney function. Option B misclassifies these substances as hormones rather than waste products. Option C misclassifies them as electrolytes, which they are not. Option A misclassifies them as proteins synthesized by the kidney rather than filtered waste products.
  14. The correct answer is B (The urethra, carrying urine from the bladder to outside the body.): the prostate gland surrounds the proximal urethra, so its enlargement can compress this structure and obstruct urine flow. Option A describes a structure located well above the prostate, unaffected by its enlargement. Option C describes a reproductive duct not responsible for urinary symptoms. Option D describes a structure involved in sperm maturation, unrelated to urinary obstruction.
  15. The correct answer is A (Serum creatinine, used to estimate glomerular filtration rate.): creatinine clearance calculations derived from serum creatinine are the standard method for estimating GFR and staging chronic kidney disease. Option B describes an electrolyte unrelated to GFR staging. Option C describes a diabetes monitoring test, not a kidney function stage marker. Option D describes an infection or inflammation marker, not a renal function measurement.
  16. The correct answer is D (The glomerular capillary filtration barrier, which normally excludes large plasma proteins from filtrate.): damage to the glomerular basement membrane and podocytes allows large plasma proteins to leak into the filtrate, producing significant proteinuria. Option B describes a distal segment responsible for concentrating urine, not protein filtration. Option C describes a segment involved in the countercurrent mechanism, not protein barrier function. Option A describes a distal tubular segment unrelated to the glomerular filtration barrier.
  17. The correct answer is C (Obstruction of urine outflow causing backpressure within the kidney.): when a stone blocks the ureter, urine backs up into the renal pelvis and calyces, causing them to dilate under increased pressure. Option B describes a benign physiological response unrelated to obstruction. Option A describes an immune process seen in glomerulonephritis, not obstruction. Option D describes pyelonephritis, a different pathologic process from mechanical obstruction.
  18. The correct answer is B (The collecting duct, the nephron segment that concentrates the final urine before excretion.): antidiuretic hormone increases the permeability of the collecting duct to water, allowing more water to be reabsorbed and concentrating the final urine. Option A describes a segment where most reabsorption occurs but is not the primary site of ADH action. Option C describes the filtration site, not a hormone-responsive reabsorptive segment. Option D describes a vessel regulating blood flow into the glomerulus, not water reabsorption.
  19. The correct answer is B (Comparability, meaning whether data can be consistently compared across different sources and time.): Applying coding standards consistently across different charts and physicians supports comparability, since it helps ensure that coded data means the same thing regardless of its source, which is essential for meaningful comparison across facilities, time periods, and providers. Legibility relates to how clearly information is written, confidentiality relates to protecting information from inappropriate disclosure, and portability is not a standard recognized data quality dimension in this context.
  20. The correct answer is A (Timeliness, meaning how promptly data becomes available relative to when it is needed.): Timeliness refers to how promptly data becomes available relative to when it is needed, and consistently meeting or beating reporting deadlines directly supports this dimension by ensuring national data users have access to current information for analysis and decision making. Accuracy concerns correctness of content, completeness concerns whether all required data elements are present, and comparability concerns consistent application across sources, which are related but distinct dimensions.
  21. The correct answer is D (Completeness, meaning whether all relevant and required data elements have been captured.): Completeness refers to whether all relevant and required data elements have actually been captured, so missing diagnosis codes for conditions that were clearly documented represents a direct completeness gap in the coded data. Timeliness concerns how quickly data is made available, legibility concerns clarity of documentation, and confidentiality concerns protecting information from inappropriate access, none of which describe a gap in what was actually captured.
  22. The correct answer is C (To identify and correct coding errors, provide feedback, and support continuous improvement.): Internal coding quality review processes are designed to catch errors, provide constructive feedback, and support continuous improvement in coding accuracy and consistency across the department, ultimately protecting the quality of data submitted to bodies such as CIHI. They are not intended as an automatic basis for dismissal after a single error, do not replace the need to follow established national coding standards, and have no connection to an individual's personal tax matters.
  23. The correct answer is B (Comparisons and conclusions drawn from the data across those jurisdictions may be unreliable or misleading.): When coding conventions are applied inconsistently across jurisdictions, cross-jurisdictional comparisons built on that data risk being unreliable or misleading, since apparent differences in outcomes or resource use may actually reflect coding inconsistency rather than real clinical differences. This kind of problem does not resolve itself automatically without corrective action, and both funding formulas and research studies that rely on this data can be meaningfully affected by such inconsistencies.
  24. The correct answer is D (Contacting the attending physician to clarify an ambiguous or incomplete diagnosis before finalizing the code assignment.): A coding query process involves the coder formally contacting the responsible physician or clinician to seek clarification when documentation is ambiguous, conflicting, or incomplete, which supports both accuracy and appropriate physician accountability for the final diagnosis. Coders must never alter a physician's documentation directly, must not select codes based on convenience rather than accuracy, and patient approval of individual codes is not part of a legitimate coding query process.
  25. The correct answer is A (Under-coding, which reduces the accuracy and specificity of the resulting data.): Selecting a less specific code than the documentation actually supports is generally described as under-coding, and it undermines the accuracy and clinical specificity of the resulting data, even though each individual code chosen may be technically valid on its own. This is a genuine quality concern, not an encouraged shortcut, it is distinct from over-coding, which involves assigning codes not supported by documentation, and it is not an appropriate substitute for querying a physician when documentation is genuinely ambiguous.
  26. The correct answer is B (Relevance, meaning whether the data actually meets the needs of its intended users.): Relevance refers to the degree to which data actually meets the needs of its intended users, so evaluating whether coded data serves the practical needs of funding analysts, researchers, and planners is fundamentally an assessment of relevance. Legibility concerns the clarity of written or entered information, authentication concerns verifying the identity of a document's author, and retention concerns how long records are kept, none of which describe this particular evaluation focus.
  27. The correct answer is C (Clarify the discrepancy with the responsible physician through a formal query before assigning a code.): When documentation is inconsistent between sources, the coder's appropriate first step is to seek clarification from the responsible physician through a formal query, rather than guessing based on document order, coding both possibilities to hedge, or leaving the field blank indefinitely. This protects the accuracy of the final code and ensures the physician remains accountable for confirming the correct diagnosis.
  28. The correct answer is B (Classifications are periodically updated with new codes and revised conventions.): Because CIHI periodically updates ICD-10-CA and CCI with new codes, revised conventions, and clarified guidelines, coders must engage in ongoing continuing education to apply the current version of each classification accurately. Classifications are not static once a coder is certified, continuing education has no connection to tax requirements, and formal update communications are distributed through official CIHI and organizational channels, not informal bulletin boards.
  29. The correct answer is C (Provide targeted training and support to help coders correctly apply the updated classification content.): When a classification update coincides with a measurable drop in coding accuracy, the appropriate management response is to invest in targeted training and support so coders can correctly apply the new content, since this is a recognized and manageable transition period. Immediate discipline without investigation is inappropriate, updates do in fact commonly cause a temporary accuracy dip if unaddressed, and refusing to adopt current national classification versions would create its own serious compliance and comparability problems.
  30. The correct answer is C (Abstracting, a point relevant to how coded health data is used.): Abstracting is the process by which a health information professional reviews the full health record and extracts a standardized set of key clinical and administrative data elements, such as diagnoses, interventions, and dates, to be submitted to a database such as CIHI's Discharge Abstract Database. Authentication refers to verifying an author's identity on a document, de-identifying refers to removing identifying details from data, and triangulating is not a recognized abstracting term in this context.
  31. The correct answer is D (Administrative data, as distinct from coded clinical diagnosis or intervention data.): Length of stay is an administrative data element, calculated from dates recorded during the encounter, and it is distinct from the coded clinical information captured through ICD-10-CA diagnosis codes or CCI intervention codes. It is not itself a terminology concept, a CCI qualifying characteristic, or an etiology code, all of which describe different kinds of coded clinical detail rather than a calculated administrative field.
  32. The correct answer is C (The relevant Tabular List entry's instructional notes, such as a use additional code note.): The Tabular List entry for the diagnosis category is where instructional notes, such as a direction to use an additional code to identify a causative organism, are documented, and the coder should consult this before finalizing the code assignment. A consent form and a visitor policy are unrelated administrative documents, and randomly selecting a code from the chapter would not reflect proper, standards-based coding practice.
  33. The correct answer is B (They provide detailed, standardized guidance on how to interpret and apply the classifications consistently.): Canadian coding standards provide detailed guidance on how to apply ICD-10-CA and CCI consistently to real clinical scenarios, addressing situations the classifications alone may not fully resolve, such as sequencing rules or how to handle particular documentation patterns. These standards do not replace the Index or Tabular List, are not optional for coders following recognized national practice, and apply nationally rather than to a single province.
  34. The correct answer is B (Different information needs, direct clinical communication versus standardized aggregate reporting.): Direct clinical communication benefits from the nuance and detail of narrative documentation and clinical terminologies, while standardized statistical and administrative reporting benefits from the consistency and manageable structure of a classification system, so maintaining both serves different, complementary information needs. Narrative documentation remains a legally valid and retained part of the record, classification coding occurs after the clinical encounter and its documentation, and narrative documentation is used across all clinical specialties, not psychiatry alone.
  35. The correct answer is A (The most specific clinical term documented, since it best reflects the condition's true nature.): When both a specific clinical term and a more general phrase describe the same documented condition, the coder should generally search the Index using the most specific term available, since this best reflects the true nature of the diagnosis and supports selecting the most accurate, specific code. Choosing a term based on alphabetical order in the sentence, typing convenience, or ignoring the more specific term in favour of a vaguer one would all undermine coding accuracy.
  36. The correct answer is D (Sections organize the classification logically by broad category of intervention.): CCI's sections organize interventions into logical broad categories, which supports consistent navigation of the classification and helps ensure coders can reliably locate and apply the appropriate code for a given type of intervention. Sections are not tied to which hospital department manages billing, are not assigned randomly, and apply across all patient populations rather than being limited to pediatric care.
  37. The correct answer is D (Consult the applicable coding standards and instructional notes.): When documentation genuinely supports more than one possible category, the coder should first consult applicable coding standards and instructional notes for direction, and escalate to a physician query if the ambiguity cannot be resolved through those resources. Automatically assigning both codes, choosing based on a code's numeric value, or leaving a legitimately documented diagnosis uncoded indefinitely are not appropriate responses to genuine coding ambiguity.
  38. The correct answer is D (Coded data can only be as accurate and complete as the underlying clinical documentation it is based on.): Because coders assign codes based on what has actually been documented in the health record, the accuracy and completeness of coded data is fundamentally dependent on the quality of the underlying clinical documentation, which is why documentation improvement initiatives are closely tied to coding quality efforts. Coding accuracy is not independent of documentation, coders are not permitted to fill documentation gaps with their own assumptions, and documentation quality matters across all care settings, not inpatient care alone.
  39. The correct answer is C (More specific documentation allows coders to assign more accurate and specific ICD-10-CA and CCI codes.): Clinical documentation improvement programs work by helping physicians document conditions and treatments with greater specificity and clarity, which in turn allows coders to assign more accurate and specific codes, directly improving overall data quality. Such programs do not remove the coder's responsibility to verify codes in the Tabular List, do not exempt a hospital from CIHI reporting obligations, and do not replace a physician's own authentication responsibilities.
  40. The correct answer is A (A terminology can represent very fine-grained clinical detail, while a classification aggregates that detail into broader categories.): Granularity in this context refers to the level of detail a system can represent, and a clinical terminology is generally capable of far finer-grained representation of clinical concepts than a statistical classification, which intentionally aggregates related concepts into broader, standardized categories for reporting purposes. Granularity has nothing to do with a database's file size or a manual's page count, and the two system types are specifically designed to differ in their level of detail.
  41. The correct answer is A (Established Canadian coding standards for handling qualified or uncertain diagnoses in the applicable care setting.): How a qualified or uncertain diagnosis, such as one documented as 'suspected,' should be coded is governed by established Canadian coding standards specific to the care setting involved, since these standards provide consistent direction for handling this kind of documentation. This determination has nothing to do with a patient's own wording preference, the colour of ink used, or the physical order of pages in the chart, none of which are recognized coding considerations.
  42. The correct answer is A (Follow the organization's documentation and coding policy regarding provider attribution.): Health information management departments typically have policies addressing how documentation from different types of providers, including residents, should be handled for coding purposes, and a coder should follow that policy, querying the attending physician when confirmation is genuinely needed. Automatically disregarding a resident's documentation, coding without any consideration of provider attribution, or waiting for a future readmission are not appropriate or standard responses to this situation.
  43. The correct answer is C (Relevant diagnoses and interventions may be documented throughout the record, not only the summary.): Because clinically significant diagnoses, complications, and interventions can be documented in various parts of the record, such as progress notes, operative reports, and consultation notes, and may not all be captured in the discharge summary, a thorough review of the full record supports complete and accurate coding. The discharge summary is in fact typically authored or authenticated by a physician, full-record review is expected regardless of length of stay, and it does not eliminate the occasional need to query a physician for clarification.
  44. The correct answer is D (The terminology may offer several closely related, highly specific terms.): A clinical terminology is generally built to represent a procedure with a high degree of specificity across many related terms, while CCI, as a classification, represents the same procedure using a smaller, standardized set of codes built from defined components such as body system, intervention type, and attributes. CCI does not contain more entries than a comprehensive clinical terminology, the two systems are not interchangeable, and surgical procedures are in fact a core part of what CCI is designed to classify.
  45. The correct answer is C (They provide the common structure needed to collect, compare, and report health data consistently across the country.): National health data standards, including ICD-10-CA and CCI, function as essential infrastructure because they establish a common structure that allows health information to be collected, compared, and used consistently by many different organizations and jurisdictions across the country, supporting planning, funding, and research. They are not limited to facilities in a capital city, they serve a genuine functional purpose rather than existing simply to create paperwork, and they apply across the full range of publicly funded facilities, not private for-profit ones alone.
  46. The correct answer is C (When no definitive diagnosis has been established by the end of the encounter or stay in question.): A sign or symptom code is generally appropriate when no definitive diagnosis has been established by the conclusion of the relevant encounter or stay, since coding a symptom in that situation still captures clinically meaningful information about the reason for care. If a definitive diagnosis was in fact established, that diagnosis, not merely the presenting symptom, should typically be coded. This principle applies regardless of patient age or the day of the week the care occurred.
  47. The correct answer is A (The coder should select the code representing the specific subtype.): When documentation clearly supports a specific subtype of a broader condition, standard coding practice is to select the code that reflects that greatest available level of clinical specificity, rather than defaulting to a more general category code. Assigning both as unrelated, separate diagnoses misrepresents the clinical picture, and patients do not select which diagnosis code is used, since that determination is based on documented clinical fact rather than personal preference.
  48. The correct answer is C (A sequencing or 'code first' type restriction guiding how the code may be used in combination.): Certain ICD-10-CA categories carry sequencing-related instructional notes, sometimes phrased as a 'code first' direction, restricting how that code may be used relative to another, often more specific or causally primary, code. This is a legitimate structural convention rather than an indication of deletion, and it is not limited to outpatient laboratory settings or restricted to use by a single named facility.
  49. The correct answer is C (Understanding the underlying logic and conventions of a classification allows a coder to apply it correctly.): Understanding the underlying conventions and logic of a classification, rather than relying purely on rote lookup, allows a coder to handle documentation scenarios that a simple index search alone cannot resolve, supporting more accurate and consistent code assignment overall. This theoretical understanding remains practically relevant well beyond simple memorization, is expected of front-line coders as well as supervisors, and applies equally to diagnosis and procedure classifications.
  50. The correct answer is C (In a proportion the numerator is always a subset of the denominator, while in a ratio the numerator need not be part of the denominator.): A proportion is a special type of ratio in which the numerator is included within the denominator, such as female patients over total patients, so the result always falls between 0 and 100 percent. A general ratio, such as the ratio of registered nurses to physicians on staff, compares two quantities that are not subsets of one another and can exceed 100 percent.
  51. The correct answer is B (A rate always incorporates a specific time period during which the events being measured occurred.): A rate measures the frequency of an event occurring over a defined period of time relative to a population at risk during that same period, which is why rates are always tied to a stated timeframe such as a month, quarter, or year. A proportion, by contrast, simply describes what fraction of a whole a part represents without necessarily involving the passage of time.
  52. The correct answer is A (Incidence rate.): Incidence specifically counts new cases that arise during a defined period among a population at risk, making it the appropriate measure for tracking how many people are newly developing a disease. Prevalence, in contrast, counts every existing case, whether newly diagnosed or long-standing, at one point or over one period, and therefore answers a different clinical and administrative question.
  53. The correct answer is B (Prevalence.): Prevalence measures the total number of existing cases, both new and pre-existing, divided by the total population at a defined point or period, which makes it the correct choice for describing the overall burden of a chronic condition in a community. Incidence would understate the true burden because it only reflects cases that are newly diagnosed within the specific reporting window.
  54. The correct answer is C (Improved survival and longer duration of illness allow existing cases to accumulate in the population over time.): Prevalence depends on both how many new cases occur (incidence) and how long each case remains in the population, whether due to chronic illness, delayed mortality, or improved treatment that extends survival. If incidence stays constant but people live longer with the condition rather than dying or being cured quickly, existing cases accumulate and prevalence rises even without any change in new diagnoses.
  55. The correct answer is D (All discharges for the period.): The gross death rate uses total discharges, including deaths, as its denominator, without excluding any category of patient. This differs from the net death rate, which excludes deaths occurring within 48 hours of admission from both the numerator and denominator on the reasoning that such early deaths are less likely to reflect the quality of care delivered during the stay.
  56. The correct answer is B (Because a death so soon after admission is considered unlikely to reflect the quality of inpatient care provided.): The net death rate is designed to more fairly evaluate the quality of care delivered during a hospital stay, and a patient who dies within 48 hours of admission is generally considered to have arrived already too critically ill for hospital care to meaningfully affect the outcome. Excluding these early deaths from both the numerator and denominator produces a rate that better isolates deaths potentially related to care delivered over a longer stay.
  57. The correct answer is C (The net autopsy rate removes coroner or medical examiner cases from the denominator, while the gross rate includes them.): The gross autopsy rate divides total hospital autopsies by total inpatient deaths, without adjustment, while the net autopsy rate removes coroner or medical examiner cases from the denominator because the hospital had no legal authority to perform an autopsy on those cases. As a result, the net autopsy rate is typically higher than the gross rate calculated from the same underlying data.
  58. The correct answer is A (Bed turnover rate.): Bed turnover rate measures how many times, on average, each available bed was used by a new patient during the reporting period, calculated as total discharges divided by the average number of beds. This indicator, together with average length of stay and bed occupancy rate, forms the core set of hospital utilization statistics reviewed in capacity and resource planning.
  59. The correct answer is B (Average length of stay and bed occupancy rate.): Average length of stay shows how long patients typically remain in a bed, while bed occupancy rate shows what proportion of total bed capacity is filled at any given time; reviewed together, these two statistics reveal whether beds are being used efficiently or whether excess capacity or overcrowding exists. A short average length of stay paired with a low occupancy rate, for example, may indicate underused capacity relative to demand.
  60. The correct answer is D (The number of admissions increased proportionally, so beds were refilled more quickly and occupancy stayed the same.): If patients are discharged sooner, freeing beds faster, but the hospital admits new patients at a correspondingly higher rate to fill those beds, bed turnover increases while occupancy rate can remain stable because the beds are simply being refilled more quickly rather than sitting empty. This illustrates why utilization statistics must be interpreted together rather than assuming one indicator alone tells the full story.
  61. The correct answer is B (Data values correctly reflect the true clinical or administrative facts they are intended to represent.): Accuracy refers to whether a data element is correct, meaning it truly reflects the real clinical event, diagnosis, or administrative fact it is meant to capture, such as a coded diagnosis matching what the physician actually documented. Accuracy is distinct from consistency, which concerns whether the same definitions and collection methods are applied uniformly, and from timeliness, which concerns how current the data are.
  62. The correct answer is B (Accuracy.): Accuracy is compromised whenever a recorded data value fails to correctly represent the true underlying fact, and in this scenario the assigned code does not match the confirmed final diagnosis. While premature coding practices can also raise timeliness or process concerns, the direct data quality problem created here is that the recorded value itself is now incorrect.
  63. The correct answer is C (Consistency.): Consistency is the data quality dimension concerned with applying the same definitions, codes, and collection methods uniformly across departments, facilities, and time periods, so that a value such as a discharge status code means the same thing everywhere it is used. Without consistency, comparisons between units, facilities, or time periods become unreliable even if each individual data element is otherwise accurate.
  64. The correct answer is A (Consistency.): This scenario describes a breakdown in consistency, since the problem is not that the individual data values were wrong but that the two facilities were not applying a shared, standardized definition when classifying cases, making the combined dataset unreliable for comparison. Data governance standards and shared data dictionaries exist specifically to prevent this type of definitional drift between reporting organizations.
  65. The correct answer is A (Granularity.): Granularity refers to the level of detail captured in a data element, such as recording a specific ICD code rather than only a broad diagnostic category, and this level of detail must match what the intended use of the data requires. A registry tracking treatment outcomes for a specific cancer subtype, for example, needs finer granularity than a report simply counting total cancer admissions.
  66. The correct answer is B (Comprehensiveness.): Comprehensiveness refers to whether all data elements required for the record's intended purpose are present, and a missing required field such as the attending physician's identification number represents a direct gap in comprehensiveness. This differs from accuracy, which concerns whether the values that ARE present are correct, since the problem here is absence rather than incorrectness.
  67. The correct answer is C (Data are recorded and made available within a period that still supports their intended use.): Timeliness concerns whether data are captured and made available quickly enough to be useful for their intended purpose, such as a lab result being entered into the record before a clinician needs to act on it. Data that are perfectly accurate but delivered too late to inform a decision fail the timeliness dimension even though every other data quality dimension may be satisfied.
  68. The correct answer is A (A histogram.): A histogram groups continuous numeric data into intervals, or bins, and displays the frequency of observations falling into each interval using adjacent bars, which makes it well suited to showing the shape and spread of a distribution such as patient age or length of stay. This differs from a bar chart, which is used to compare discrete, unrelated categories rather than intervals of a single continuous variable.
  69. The correct answer is A (A line graph.): A line graph is the standard choice for displaying a trend over time because it connects data points in sequential order along a continuous time axis, allowing the viewer to easily see increases, decreases, and patterns such as seasonality. A pie chart, by contrast, is intended to show how parts contribute to a single whole at one point in time and is poorly suited to displaying change across multiple periods.
  70. The correct answer is D (A pie chart.): A pie chart divides a circle into slices proportional to each category's share of the total, which makes it appropriate when the goal is to show how parts, such as five discharge disposition categories, contribute to a single whole at one point in time. This differs from a bar chart, which can also compare categories but does not inherently emphasize their relationship to a combined total the way a pie chart does.
  71. The correct answer is C (A control chart.): A control chart plots a process measure, such as an infection rate, over successive time periods against statistically calculated upper and lower control limits, allowing a quality improvement team to distinguish normal variation from a true special-cause signal that requires investigation. This makes it the standard tool in continuous quality monitoring, distinct from charts designed simply to compare categories or display proportions.
  72. The correct answer is B (Standardized codes allow results to be consistently interpreted and exchanged across different systems and organizations.): Coded laboratory results, such as those using LOINC, carry a fixed, unambiguous meaning that a receiving system can parse and display correctly without relying on human interpretation of free text wording. Free text is prone to spelling variation, abbreviation, and local phrasing, which makes automated processing, trending, and cross-system exchange unreliable. Standardization is not primarily about billing (option D) or restricting bedside readability (option A), and no Canadian law mandates free text reporting (option C). The core justification is consistency of meaning across the systems that consume the result.
  73. The correct answer is D (The picture archiving and communication system access log.): A chest CT scan is diagnostic imaging data, which is stored, transmitted, and accessed through a picture archiving and communication system (PACS), so any audit of who viewed those specific images must examine the PACS access log. A pharmacy dispensing log (A) or a laboratory specimen log (B) would not capture image viewing activity at all, and a general building entry log (C) tracks physical access, not electronic chart access. HIM privacy auditors routinely pull PACS audit trails specifically because they timestamp every user who opened a given imaging study.
  74. The correct answer is B (A networked picture archiving and communication system capable of transmitting images between sites.): Teleradiology depends on a PACS that is networked between the originating hospital and the reporting radiologist's site, allowing diagnostic images to be transmitted electronically and viewed remotely for interpretation. A pharmacy information system (A) and laboratory information system (C) handle entirely different data types, medications and specimen results respectively, not images. A paper based courier process (D) is the opposite of what makes teleradiology possible, since it removes the electronic transmission that defines the practice.
  75. The correct answer is C (Integration allows a physician's electronic medication order to flow directly to the pharmacist for verification.): Integrating the clinical information system with the pharmacy information system creates a seamless electronic pathway so that an order entered by a physician reaches pharmacy without manual re-transcription, reducing transcription errors and delay. Keeping the systems separate, as options B and D suggest, would actually increase risk by requiring duplicate data entry and removing the checks that integration provides. Option A misidentifies the purpose as an energy consumption concern, which is not a recognized rationale for clinical system integration.
  76. The correct answer is C (The clinical information system, which reflects standard practice in Canadian health information management.): Nursing documentation such as vital signs, assessments, and care plans during an inpatient stay is entered and maintained within the clinical information system, which is the core system supporting bedside clinical workflow. The picture archiving and communication system (A) handles diagnostic images, the pharmacy information system (B) manages medication orders and dispensing, and a health information exchange (D) connects separate external organizations rather than supporting day-to-day inpatient charting. Of the four, only the clinical information system is designed around ongoing nursing care documentation.
  77. The correct answer is D (The systems do not use a shared data standard or terminology to structure and code the information consistently.): Even when both systems are fully digital, laboratory results cannot be exchanged automatically unless both sides use a common coding and messaging standard, such as LOINC for the test identifiers and HL7 or FHIR for the transmission format, so the receiving system can interpret the data correctly. Physical location (A) has no bearing on electronic exchange once network connectivity exists, and system age (C) alone does not prevent interoperability, since older systems can still be mapped to a standard. The determining factor is whether both systems structure and code the data the same way.
  78. The correct answer is C (Interoperability, used to support day to day health information management practice.): Interoperability is the specific term for the ability of different health information systems to exchange, interpret, and meaningfully use shared data, which is exactly the technical standards question being described. Deduplication (A) refers to resolving duplicate patient records, overlay correction (B) refers to fixing incorrectly merged patient identities, and record linkage weighting (D) refers to the scoring used in probabilistic patient matching, none of which describe cross-system data exchange capability.
  79. The correct answer is B (A set of international standards that define how clinical and administrative data should be formatted and exchanged between systems.): HL7 (Health Level Seven) is an internationally recognized family of standards, not a single vendor product, that specifies how clinical and administrative information should be structured, coded, and transmitted so that different systems can interpret it consistently. It is not a government licensing body (option C) and is not limited to laboratory billing codes (option D), and because it is a standard rather than a piece of software, option A's description of it as one proprietary application is also incorrect. Its value in Canadian health information management lies precisely in enabling meaningful exchange between otherwise unrelated systems.
  80. The correct answer is A (A modern HL7 standard that uses web based technology to make structured health data easier to exchange between different systems.): FHIR, Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources, is the newest generation HL7 standard, built on modern web technologies such as RESTful APIs and JSON or XML resources, specifically designed to make structured health data simpler to access and exchange than earlier HL7 versions. It is not a privacy law replacement (B), not a vendor-specific PACS product (C), and not a billing classification system (D). FHIR's defining feature is its use of familiar internet based technology to lower the technical barrier to interoperability.
  81. The correct answer is C (A comprehensive, standardized clinical terminology used to code diagnoses, findings, and procedures for consistent meaning across systems.): SNOMED CT is a large, structured clinical terminology that assigns standardized codes to diagnoses, findings, procedures, and other clinical concepts so that the same concept is represented identically no matter which system records it. It is not hardware infrastructure (A), a provincial billing schedule (B), or a single vendor's branded EMR product (D). Its role in Canadian health information systems is specifically to provide a common clinical vocabulary that supports accurate coding and cross-system interoperability.
  82. The correct answer is B (A standardized coding system primarily used to identify laboratory and clinical observations such as specific test results.): LOINC (Logical Observation Identifiers Names and Codes) exists specifically to give each distinct laboratory test or clinical observation a unique, consistent code, which allows results from different laboratories and systems to be compared and exchanged reliably. It is not a network transmission protocol (A), a government issued patient identifier (C), or a physician billing code set (D). LOINC's specific domain is naming and identifying observations, which distinguishes it from a broader clinical terminology like SNOMED CT.
  83. The correct answer is B (Results may not map cleanly between the two systems, creating interoperability challenges that require mapping or translation.): When one system uses LOINC to identify a laboratory test and the other expects SNOMED CT for the same data element, the two coding systems serve related but structurally different purposes, so a direct one-to-one match cannot be assumed and a mapping or translation layer is typically required to reconcile them. Option A understates the real practical difficulty these mismatches create, and options C and D are both incorrect since no automatic national resolution exists and a coding mismatch does not itself stop a laboratory from performing a test. This scenario is a textbook example of why terminology mapping is a real, ongoing interoperability task.
  84. The correct answer is A (A shared terminology ensures a diagnosis or finding is coded and interpreted consistently regardless of which system or clinician records it.): When every clinician and system in a region uses the same terminology, such as SNOMED CT, a given diagnosis or finding is represented the same way everywhere, which reduces the risk of miscommunication or misinterpretation when a patient's record moves between providers. This directly supports patient safety, unlike options B and C, which overstate the effect by claiming a terminology can guarantee agreement on treatment or eliminate clinical judgement, something no coding system can do. Option D is also incorrect because standardized terminology has direct clinical, not merely billing, significance.
  85. The correct answer is B (HL7 standards define structured formats so that clinical and administrative information can be reliably sent between different systems.): HL7 messaging standards exist to define consistent, structured message formats so that clinical and administrative data, such as admissions, results, and orders, can be transmitted reliably between different health information systems. Option A is wrong because HL7 covers far more than financial transactions, option C incorrectly claims a single vendor requirement which HL7 does not impose, and option D wrongly limits HL7 to imaging and radiology when it spans many clinical domains. The defining feature of HL7 is its role in structuring data for reliable cross-system transmission.
  86. The correct answer is C (FHIR uses modern web based application programming interfaces.): A key advantage of FHIR over older HL7 messaging standards, such as HL7 v2, is that FHIR is built on modern, widely used web technologies like RESTful APIs, making it easier and faster to implement, including in mobile and web applications. Option A is incorrect because FHIR still relies on a defined data standard rather than eliminating one, option B wrongly limits FHIR to imaging data when it represents many data types including labs and medications, and option D incorrectly restricts FHIR to billing only. FHIR's practical advantage is specifically its accessible, web native technical approach.
  87. The correct answer is B (Interoperability enabled by shared data standards between the two systems.): For a referral to automatically populate structured fields in a specialist's intake system rather than arriving as unstructured free text, both systems need to use compatible, shared data standards, which is precisely what interoperability provides. A standalone personal health record with no system connection (A) could not populate another system's fields automatically, a PACS (C) is unrelated to referral text, and a manual fax re-entry process (D) is the opposite of the automated, structured behavior described. The scenario is a direct illustration of interoperability enabling structured data flow between systems.
  88. The correct answer is C (LOINC is primarily used to identify laboratory and clinical observations.): LOINC's defining role is assigning standardized identifiers to laboratory tests and clinical observations, which correctly pairs the standard with its primary domain of use. Option A incorrectly assigns LOINC to imaging and SNOMED CT to pharmacy billing, neither of which reflects their actual purposes, option B incorrectly labels HL7 and FHIR as billing code sets when they are data exchange standards, and option D reverses the roles of SNOMED CT and LOINC, mischaracterizing SNOMED CT as a messaging protocol and denying LOINC any coding function. Only option C correctly matches a standard with the domain it actually serves.
  89. The correct answer is A (Interoperability depends on shared data standards and consistent implementation, not merely on using the same software vendor.): Even hospitals running identical EHR vendor software can face interoperability challenges with an outside, unrelated organization because true data exchange depends on both organizations implementing shared standards and consistent configuration, not simply on which vendor's product each side happens to run. Option B incorrectly assumes shared vendor status automatically guarantees interoperability, option C wrongly claims challenges occur mainly between different vendors while ignoring same-vendor implementation differences, and option D incorrectly dismisses HL7 and FHIR as irrelevant once a vendor is chosen. The underlying issue is standardized, consistent implementation, which vendor identity alone does not ensure.
  90. The correct answer is B (FHIR was specifically designed to support modern, web based data exchange including mobile and application based.): FHIR was purpose built using RESTful web APIs and common formats like JSON, which makes it well suited for a mobile app to securely pull structured lab results directly from a hospital system with the patient's consent. Option A incorrectly claims FHIR cannot support consumer facing mobile applications, when that is actually one of its core intended use cases, option C wrongly frames FHIR as a billing standard incapable of representing lab data, and option D incorrectly requires paper exchange before any digital transmission, which contradicts FHIR's entirely digital design. FHIR's web native architecture is exactly why it fits this mobile use case well.
  91. The correct answer is D (Consistent coding allows data from many different sources to be aggregated and analyzed meaningfully.): Standardized coding systems like SNOMED CT and LOINC ensure that the same clinical concept or test is represented identically across many different organizations, which is essential for secondary uses such as research and public health surveillance where data from numerous sources must be aggregated and compared reliably. Option A incorrectly limits these coding systems to primary care with no research role, option B incorrectly claims secondary uses ignore original recording methods, and option C wrongly reduces standardized coding to a purely billing function with no analytic value. The real value for research and surveillance lies in enabling meaningful large scale data aggregation.
  92. The correct answer is C (Both systems must use compatible data standards and structured formats for medication information.): Exchanging medication data between a clinic system and a hospital pharmacy information system without manual re-entry requires both systems to use compatible data standards and structured formats, such as coded drug identifiers and a shared messaging format like HL7, so the receiving system can interpret the data automatically. Options A and B describe irrelevant factors, geographic origin of staff and interface colour scheme, that have no bearing on data exchange capability, and option D incorrectly suggests limiting the systems to imaging data only, which is unrelated to the medication exchange goal. The essential requirement is data standard compatibility, not any of these unrelated factors.
  93. The correct answer is B (FHIR is a newer HL7 standard built on modern web technologies, developed to make health data easier to exchange between systems.): FHIR was developed by HL7 as its newest generation standard, built specifically on modern web technologies to simplify and modernize the exchange of health data compared with earlier HL7 versions. Option A incorrectly claims FHIR and HL7 come from largely separate groups, when FHIR is in fact an HL7 product, option C incorrectly states HL7 replaced FHIR and that FHIR is rarely used, which reverses the actual relationship since FHIR is the newer, increasingly adopted standard, and option D incorrectly frames FHIR as primarily a hardware specification. The accurate relationship is that FHIR is HL7's modern, web based evolution.
  94. The correct answer is B (A request by the patient for a copy of their own diagnostic imaging reports and clinical notes.): A request by a patient for their own diagnostic imaging reports and clinical notes is squarely a personal health information access request, since it concerns identifiable information specifically about the requester. Requests for institutional budgets, aggregate wait time statistics, or general policy manuals concern records about the organization's operations rather than an individual's own health information, and would typically be processed, if at all, under general freedom of information or access-to-information legislation applicable to public bodies. Recognizing this distinction helps HIM staff route requests to the correct process and apply the right set of exceptions. Mixing up the two frameworks can lead to improper delays or improper disclosures.
  95. The correct answer is A (Many provincial statutes recognize a specific exception allowing this type of confidentially provided third-party information.): Several provincial health privacy statutes include a specific, narrow exception recognizing that certain information provided in confidence by a third party, such as a person reporting a safety concern, may be withheld from the individual the information concerns, in order to protect the safety and willingness of others to provide important information to health and social service providers. This exception is applied carefully and is not a general basis to withhold all third-party references, only genuinely confidential reports meeting the statutory criteria. The custodian should still disclose the remainder of the record not implicated by this exception. Misapplying this exception too broadly can improperly deny a patient legitimate access to their own information.
  96. The correct answer is D (Sever or redact only the specific exempt portion and disclose the remainder of the record.): The general principle across most provincial access regimes is that exceptions should be applied as narrowly as possible, meaning a custodian should sever or redact only the specific portion of a record that genuinely falls within a recognized exception, while still disclosing the remainder of the record to the patient. Denying an entire request simply because one exception applies to a small portion would be inconsistent with the purpose of the access right. This severance approach requires careful, page-by-page or entry-by-entry review rather than a blanket decision. HIM staff processing complex requests must be trained to apply this principle consistently and defensibly.
  97. The correct answer is A (A reasonable identity verification process before releasing personal health information in response to an access request.): A reasonable identity verification process, such as confirming government-issued identification, matching demographic details already on file, or using a secure authenticated patient portal, is specifically intended to reduce the risk that personal health information is released to someone fraudulently impersonating the patient. Relying solely on a stated name with no further check creates significant risk of improper disclosure to the wrong individual. This verification step is a core operational safeguard that HIM departments must build into their access request procedures. Failing to verify identity adequately can itself constitute a privacy breach if information is released to the wrong person.
  98. The correct answer is D (A privacy breach requiring assessment and, where required, notification.): Accessing a patient's record without a legitimate purpose connected to their care, sometimes called snooping, is a clear example of unauthorized access that most provincial health privacy statutes treat as a privacy breach. The custodian is generally required to assess the breach, take steps to contain it, and determine whether notification to the affected patient and, depending on severity, the applicable oversight body is required. This type of breach also frequently triggers disciplinary action against the employee under the custodian's own policies and applicable professional or employment standards. Robust audit logging is the primary tool used to detect this kind of unauthorized access.
  99. The correct answer is B (Immediately contain the breach, such as attempting remote wipe capability if available.): When a device containing personal health information is lost or stolen, the immediate priority is containment, which may include remote wipe or disabling access if the device supports it, followed promptly by an assessment of what information was on the device, how many patients are affected, and the level of risk created by the loss. This rapid response can meaningfully limit the harm from the incident. Delaying any action for an extended period would allow potential harm to continue unaddressed and is inconsistent with breach management obligations. The organization should also review whether encryption or other safeguards should have been in place on the device in the first instance.
  100. The correct answer is D (The sensitivity of the information involved, the number of individuals affected.): Assessing the real risk of significant harm from a privacy breach generally involves considering factors such as how sensitive the exposed information is, how many individuals are affected, who gained unauthorized access and what they might do with the information, and whether reasonable mitigation such as encryption reduces the practical risk of misuse. These substantive factors drive whether notification obligations are triggered under most provincial breach notification frameworks. Superficial or irrelevant details, such as the physical distance to a patient's home, have no bearing on this risk assessment. A documented, consistent risk assessment process helps custodians make defensible notification decisions.
  101.
