The correct answer is B (A description of what happened, what information was involved, what steps are being taken.): Effective and legally sound breach notification generally provides affected individuals with meaningful information, including a clear description of what happened, the specific types of information involved, the steps the custodian is taking to contain and remediate the breach, and how the patient can seek further information or lodge a complaint with the applicable oversight body. This transparency allows patients to take their own protective steps, such as monitoring for identity misuse where relevant. Requesting sensitive financial information as part of a notification would itself raise serious security concerns and should never be part of a legitimate notice. Concealing an obligation to notify an oversight body would be inconsistent with statutory transparency requirements.
  102. The correct answer is D (Yes, unauthorized access or loss of availability still warrants a documented assessment.): A ransomware incident represents unauthorized access to, and interference with, personal health information, and most provincial breach frameworks expect the custodian to conduct and document a risk assessment even where it cannot be conclusively confirmed that data was actually viewed or exfiltrated by the attacker. The assessment considers the nature of the attacker's access and the plausibility that data was compromised, not merely whether viewing can be proven with certainty. Practice size is not generally a threshold for whether an assessment is required, though it may affect the scale of the response. Restoring from backup addresses availability but does not itself resolve the separate question of whether confidentiality was compromised.
  103. The correct answer is C (Attempt to contact the unintended recipient to request destruction or return of the fax.): Upon discovering a misdirected fax, the custodian should promptly attempt to contain the breach by contacting the unintended recipient to request that the fax be destroyed or returned and not further disclosed, while also assessing the sensitivity of the information and whether the affected patient should be notified. This kind of misdirected communication is one of the most common categories of health information privacy breaches and is generally preventable through verified fax or email address protocols. Immediate termination without investigation would be premature and inconsistent with a fair, documented response process. The incident should also be logged to identify whether a systemic fix, such as pre-programmed verified numbers, is needed.
  104. The correct answer is D (To ensure affected individuals are informed of incidents that pose a real risk of significant harm.): Mandatory breach notification provisions exist to ensure that individuals whose personal health information has been compromised in a way that creates a real risk of significant harm are informed promptly, so they can take protective steps such as monitoring for misuse, and to promote broader organizational accountability and continuous improvement in privacy practices. These provisions are generally calibrated to incidents meeting a defined risk threshold, rather than requiring notification for every trivial administrative slip with no meaningful risk. Notification is one part of a broader privacy protection framework and does not replace the need for preventive safeguards. Oversight bodies often also collect breach reports to identify systemic issues across the health sector.
  105. The correct answer is D (Containment of the breach to stop ongoing unauthorized access or disclosure.): A sound breach response process generally begins with containment, meaning immediate steps to stop further unauthorized access, use, or disclosure of the affected information, such as disabling compromised credentials or recovering a misdirected disclosure. Containment is typically followed by evaluation of the cause and scope, notification where warranted, and remediation to prevent recurrence. Public media announcement is not always necessary or appropriate and would not logically precede containment in any event. Deleting evidence of the incident would be inappropriate and could itself constitute a serious compliance failure, since documentation supports both the required assessment and any regulatory reporting.
  106. The correct answer is A (Breaches meeting the applicable statutory threshold, generally involving a real risk of significant harm.): Many provincial breach notification frameworks set a defined statutory threshold, often tied to a real risk of significant harm, above which reporting to the applicable oversight body becomes mandatory, while lower-risk incidents that do not meet this threshold may still need to be logged internally for ongoing monitoring and pattern analysis without necessarily triggering external reporting. This tiered approach focuses regulatory attention on the most consequential incidents. The specific threshold and reporting mechanics vary by province and by the custodian's particular governing legislation. Custodians should still maintain a complete internal breach log regardless of whether external reporting is ultimately required for a specific incident.
  107. The correct answer is D (Electronic health record audit logs tracking user access to specific patient charts over time.): Audit logs that track which user accessed which patient's record, when, and from where are the primary tool used to investigate suspected unauthorized access, allowing a privacy officer to reconstruct the pattern and scope of an employee's activity over the relevant time period. This kind of log review is a core, everyday HIM function supporting both proactive monitoring and reactive breach investigation. Unrelated administrative records, such as parking assignments or menus, have no relevance to this type of investigation. Regular, even automated, audit log review can also help detect this kind of pattern before it is reported by a patient or another source.
  108. The correct answer is D (Treat it as a potential privacy and security incident warranting investigation.): A report of services the patient never received can indicate identity theft or misuse of the patient's health record and should be treated as a potential privacy and security incident warranting prompt investigation, not simply dismissed as an unrelated billing error. This type of report may uncover fraudulent record creation, unauthorized access, or a data integrity issue requiring correction across the affected record. Investigating promptly also helps protect the clinic from broader systemic exposure if the underlying cause affects other patients. Patients should never be required to provide onerous documentation, such as notarization, simply to have a legitimate privacy concern taken seriously.
  109. The correct answer is B (An employee viewing a patient's chart with a legitimate, clinically justified reason connected to the patient's care.): An employee viewing a patient's chart for a legitimate, clinically justified reason connected to the patient's current care is properly authorized access falling within the circle of care and is not a privacy breach at all, in contrast to the other three examples, each of which involves information reaching someone without proper authorization or adequate safeguards. Recognizing this distinction matters because it prevents ordinary, appropriate clinical access from being mischaracterized as an incident requiring the resource-intensive breach response process. Confirming that access was in fact legitimate typically involves reviewing the documented clinical justification. Only access, use, or disclosure lacking proper authorization triggers breach assessment and response obligations.
  110. The correct answer is B (To identify the systemic or procedural cause so corrective action can prevent similar future incidents.): A root cause analysis is intended to move beyond simply identifying that a breach occurred to understanding the underlying systemic, procedural, or technical factors that allowed it to happen, so the organization can implement corrective action, such as improved training, technical controls, or process changes, to reduce the likelihood of similar incidents. While individual accountability may sometimes be a relevant finding, the analysis should not be reduced to simple blame assignment disconnected from the systemic factors involved. Notification obligations to affected individuals or an oversight body generally proceed on their own statutory timeline and should not be delayed pending completion of a root cause analysis. This forward-looking, corrective focus distinguishes root cause analysis from a purely disciplinary process.
  111. The correct answer is B (No, where the statutory threshold for notification is met.): Where a breach genuinely meets the applicable statutory threshold for individual notification, a custodian's discomfort about potentially causing the patient distress is not generally recognized as a valid legal basis to withhold that notification altogether, since the patient's right to know about a risk affecting their own information takes priority. The custodian can and should deliver the notification thoughtfully and sensitively, offering appropriate support resources or contact points for questions, but this does not permit simply skipping the obligation. Provincial breach thresholds are not generally tied to a minimum numerical count of affected patients in the way suggested. Genuine legal exceptions to notification are narrow and specific, not based on a general preference to avoid difficult conversations.
  112. The correct answer is D (Logged and reviewed as a security event, even though it did not meet the threshold.): A blocked attack that did not result in actual unauthorized access to patient data generally does not meet the threshold for individual patient notification, since no personal health information was actually compromised. However, sound security practice still calls for logging and reviewing the attempted intrusion as a security event, since it may reveal a vulnerability, such as an unpatched system, that should be remediated to prevent a future, potentially successful attack. Treating a blocked attempt identically to a confirmed breach would misallocate response resources and could cause unnecessary alarm. This distinction between a successful breach and a blocked attempt is an important part of proportionate incident management.
  113. The correct answer is A (The brief duration of exposure and lack of evidence of actual unauthorized viewing.): In assessing risk from an incident like temporarily unattended paper records, relevant factors include how long the exposure lasted, whether there is any evidence the information was actually viewed or copied by an unauthorized person, and how sensitive the specific information was, all weighed together to determine whether a real risk of significant harm exists. A brief exposure with no evidence of actual unauthorized viewing generally supports a lower risk assessment than a longer exposure or confirmed viewing, though the custodian should still document the incident and the reasoning behind its risk determination. Irrelevant factors, such as the physical characteristics of the paper or unrelated institutional history, have no bearing on this assessment. Even lower-risk incidents like this one should still be logged to track patterns that might indicate a need for improved workstation practices.
  114. The correct answer is D (Conducting regular staff privacy training and periodic audit log reviews intended to detect and prevent breaches.): Proactive breach management focuses on preventing breaches or catching them as early as possible through measures such as regular staff privacy training, periodic and even automated review of audit logs to detect unusual access patterns, and ongoing risk assessments of systems and processes. Reactive measures, such as notification letters or post-incident investigation, remain necessary but only come into play after a breach has already occurred. Waiting passively for a patient complaint before ever reviewing access activity leaves an organization vulnerable to breaches going undetected for extended periods. A mature privacy program combines strong proactive measures with an effective, well-practiced reactive response plan for when incidents nonetheless occur.
  115. The correct answer is C (Investigate the underlying cause of the recurring pattern, such as an outdated speed-dial entry.): When a breach log reveals a recurring pattern rather than isolated, unrelated incidents, sound privacy management calls for investigating the underlying systemic cause, such as an outdated or incorrectly programmed speed-dial entry, and implementing a corrective, systemic fix, such as requiring verification of fax numbers before transmission or transitioning to more secure communication methods. Continuing to respond only to individual instances without addressing the root cause allows the pattern, and the associated risk, to persist indefinitely. An abrupt, unplanned discontinuation of an entire communication method could itself disrupt patient care without adequate transition planning. This kind of trend analysis across the breach log is an important, ongoing HIM and privacy officer responsibility.
  116. The correct answer is C (Federal inmates receive health services funded and organized directly by the federal correctional system.): the Correctional Service of Canada is responsible for providing health care services to individuals incarcerated in federal penitentiaries, funding and organizing that care directly rather than routing it through provincial health insurance billing. This is one of the specific federal populations excluded from the standard provincial delivery model. Provincial inmates, by contrast, are generally covered under provincial correctional health arrangements.
  117. The correct answer is B (Provincial legislation establishes and operates the plan, while the Act sets national funding conditions.): each province and territory enacts its own health insurance legislation to establish, administer, and operate its plan, while the Canada Health Act operates as a federal condition on transfer funding rather than legislation that directly creates or runs the plans themselves. This layered relationship is why terminology and specific benefits can differ by province even though the same five national principles apply everywhere. CHIM professionals need to be comfortable navigating both levels of legislation.
  118. The correct answer is A (Ongoing federal-provincial-territorial negotiation over health funding levels and terms.): the level and growth rate of the Canada Health Transfer, and at times additional targeted funding for areas like mental health or home care, are subjects of periodic federal-provincial-territorial negotiation, reflecting the shared but distinct roles each level of government plays in funding and delivering health care. These negotiations can be contentious because provinces bear the delivery costs while federal transfers cover only a portion of total health spending. This dynamic is a recurring feature of Canadian health policy discussions.
  119. The correct answer is C (They are responsible for policy direction, regulation, funding allocation, and system oversight in their jurisdiction.): provincial and territorial ministries of health set health policy, regulate providers and facilities, allocate funding to regional health authorities or equivalent bodies, and oversee overall system performance within their jurisdiction, operating independently of federal direction subject to Canada Health Act conditions on transfer funding. Every province and territory maintains its own ministry or department of health regardless of population size. This provincial role is central to understanding where health information management functions are typically situated administratively.
  120. The correct answer is B (Outpatient prescription drugs are not required insured services under the comprehensiveness principle.): comprehensiveness requires coverage of medically necessary hospital and physician services, but outpatient prescription drug coverage, often called pharmacare, is not a required insured service under the Act, which is why provinces have historically designed their own distinct drug benefit programs with varying eligibility and coverage. This is a frequently tested distinction because many candidates assume all health-related costs fall under Canada Health Act protection. Recent national pharmacare policy discussions have proposed changing this landscape, but the core CHA requirement remains focused on hospital and physician services.
  121. The correct answer is C (Canada uses thirteen separate provincial and territorial plans operating under shared national conditions.): Canada's system, often summarized as 'Medicare,' actually consists of thirteen distinct provincial and territorial health insurance plans, each independently administered but bound by the same five national conditions set out in the Canada Health Act. This structure differs from a unified single-payer national health service model where one central government body directly administers the entire system. Recognizing this structural distinction helps explain interprovincial variation in coverage details.
  122. The correct answer is C (A transfer of federal tax room to provinces, letting them raise equivalent tax revenue.): a tax point transfer occurs when the federal government reduces its own tax rates and allows provinces to raise their tax rates by an equivalent amount, effectively transferring taxing room rather than cash, which supplements the cash portion of federal health transfers. This arrangement dates back to earlier federal-provincial funding agreements and continues to factor into total federal health support calculations. CHIM professionals working with health system financing data should be able to distinguish cash transfers from tax point transfers.
  123. The correct answer is A (A senior non-elected official who leads the ministry and implements the minister's policy.): the deputy minister of health is typically the most senior public servant within a provincial ministry of health, responsible for leading the ministry's day-to-day administration, advising the elected minister, and overseeing implementation of government health policy. The minister of health, by contrast, is an elected official who sets political direction and is accountable to the legislature. This distinction between political and administrative leadership is a common governance concept in health system structure questions.
  124. The correct answer is B (It affects which funding rules and legislative obligations apply to the data managed.): because provincial legislation governs the actual delivery, funding, and reporting requirements for most health services, while federal conditions and national data standards set through bodies like CIHI shape reporting expectations, a HIM professional must understand both layers to correctly manage records, funding submissions, and privacy compliance. This dual awareness underlies much of the day-to-day work of health information management in hospitals and regional health authorities. Ignoring this division can lead to incorrect assumptions about which rules govern a given data or funding question.
  125. The correct answer is D (Primary care, usually the first point of contact for most patients.): primary care is typically the first point of contact with the health system and includes services such as routine checkups, chronic disease management, and preventive care delivered by family physicians, nurse practitioners, and other primary care providers, often without requiring referral to a specialist or hospital. It serves as the coordinating hub that connects patients to more specialized services when needed. Recognizing which continuum level a scenario describes is a core skill for HIM professionals coding and classifying encounters.
  126. The correct answer is B (Acute care, short term intensive treatment typically delivered inside a hospital.): acute care refers to short-term, intensive treatment for a severe illness, injury, or episode of urgent medical need, typically delivered in a hospital setting with the expectation of stabilization and eventual discharge rather than indefinite residence. This distinguishes it from long-term care, which serves individuals with ongoing chronic conditions requiring extended support. Hospitals are the primary setting associated with acute care within the continuum.
  127. The correct answer is A (Long-term care, ongoing residential support for people unable to live independently.): long-term care facilities provide ongoing, typically permanent residential care with 24-hour nursing supervision and assistance with activities of daily living for individuals, often older adults, who can no longer live independently due to chronic illness, disability, or cognitive decline. This differs from acute care's short-term, treatment-focused hospital stays. Long-term care is a growing area of focus in Canadian health system planning due to an aging population.
  128. The correct answer is A (Home and community care, services delivered inside a person's home or community.): home and community care encompasses health and support services delivered in a person's own residence or local community setting, such as nursing visits, personal support, and rehabilitation, intended to help individuals recover, manage chronic conditions, or remain independent outside institutional settings. This part of the continuum has expanded in importance as health systems seek to reduce unnecessary hospital admissions and readmissions. It bridges the gap between acute hospital care and independent living.
  129. The correct answer is A (Public health, population level prevention, surveillance, and health protection activities.): public health focuses on population-level health promotion, disease prevention, surveillance, and protection activities, such as immunization campaigns and outbreak monitoring, rather than treating individual patients on a case-by-case basis. This distinguishes public health from clinical care delivered directly to individual patients across the other continuum categories. Public health units typically operate under provincial or regional public health legislation with support from national bodies like PHAC.
  130. The correct answer is D (A patient moving from primary care, to acute care, to home and long-term care.): the continuum of care describes the coordinated range of health services, from health promotion and primary care through acute treatment, rehabilitation, home and community support, and long-term or palliative care, designed to meet a person's evolving health needs over their lifetime. Effective coordination and information flow across these levels is a central concern of health information management. Gaps or poor transitions between continuum levels can lead to adverse outcomes and unnecessary hospital readmissions.
  131. The correct answer is C (Rehabilitation care, services that help patients regain function after an illness or injury.): rehabilitation care focuses on helping patients regain function, mobility, and independence following an acute illness, injury, or surgery, often serving as a transitional step between acute hospital treatment and return to home or community living. This differs from palliative care, which focuses on comfort for patients with a life-limiting illness rather than restoring function. Rehabilitation is a recognized and distinct segment within the broader continuum of care.
  132. The correct answer is A (Symptom management and comfort-focused support for a patient with a terminal illness.): palliative care focuses on relieving pain and other distressing symptoms and providing psychosocial and spiritual support for patients with serious, life-limiting illness, prioritizing quality of life over curative treatment. It can be delivered in hospital, hospice, home, or long-term care settings depending on the patient's needs and preferences. This distinguishes palliative care from acute care's curative and stabilizing focus.
  133. The correct answer is D (Coordination across the continuum of care to support successful transitions between care settings.): this scenario demonstrates how gaps in coordination between acute care and home and community care can directly affect patient outcomes such as readmission rates, underscoring why integrated information flow and discharge planning across continuum levels matters. Health information management plays a key role in ensuring accurate, timely information transfer supports these transitions. This is a practical application of continuum of care concepts to quality improvement work.
  134. The correct answer is C (Politely ask the colleagues to stop and remind them it is not an appropriate setting.): Confidentiality obligations require HIM professionals to actively prevent disclosures of personal health information in public spaces, not merely avoid causing them personally. Speaking up immediately protects the patient and models professional accountability for peers. Waiting or staying silent allows the breach to continue and fails the duty to safeguard information at every opportunity. Omitting a full name does not remove identifying details like diagnosis, unit, and timing that together can identify the patient.
  135. The correct answer is B (Skipping identity verification for any requester, regardless of familiarity, increases the risk of unauthorized disclosure.): Identity verification exists precisely because familiarity or the appearance of legitimacy does not reliably confirm a caller's actual identity or authority to receive information, so skipping this step for any category of caller introduces real risk of unauthorized disclosure. Framing the issue as pure efficiency or acceptable for a small group understates the genuine privacy risk involved. Verification procedures should apply consistently regardless of how familiar a requester seems.
  136. The correct answer is B (Access logs need to be retained long enough to support meaningful privacy investigations and audits.): Access logs are a critical tool for detecting and investigating unauthorized access, and a retention period that is too short can prevent a legitimate breach investigation from finding relevant historical activity, so this protective function should weigh most heavily against pure cost considerations. Treating cost reduction as the primary factor risks undermining a key privacy safeguard. Defaulting to the bare legal minimum without considering practical investigative needs may leave the organization without the log history it actually needs.
  137. The correct answer is D (Provide access only to the records of patients included in the review, not the extra ones.): Even a legitimate committee purpose does not justify accessing records beyond what the specific review actually requires, and the minimum necessary standard applies to internal quality activities just as it does to external requests. Denying the entire legitimate request is an overcorrection, and partially de-identifying the extra records does not address the fact that they should not be included at all. Scoping access precisely protects patients not relevant to the review.
  138. The correct answer is A (Report the records in the correct time period even though it makes her statistics less favourable.): Integrity requires accurate reporting of work data regardless of how the numbers reflect on individual performance, since inaccurate productivity data can distort staffing and resource decisions. Shifting records between periods, whether wholly or partially, misrepresents the facts to make personal statistics appear better. Seeking informal permission to misreport does not make the underlying misrepresentation acceptable.
  139. The correct answer is C (Raise the concern and advocate for updating the training materials to reflect current legal requirements.): Ensuring training materials reflect current legal and professional standards is important for maintaining organizational competence and compliance, and identifying a gap creates an obligation to raise it through appropriate channels even if updating materials is not formally her sole responsibility. Delivering outdated training as is, even with informal verbal additions, leaves an undocumented gap in staff knowledge. Refusing to train at all leaves new staff with no guidance while the materials remain unupdated.
  140. The correct answer is B (Correct the reference document immediately.): Discovering a long standing error in a shared coding reference requires both an immediate correction to prevent further inaccurate coding and an assessment of how many prior cases may need review and correction, since the scope of impact could be significant given how long the error has existed. Correcting only going forward ignores the substantial number of potentially affected prior cases. A purely personal correction without updating the shared resource leaves every other staff member still working from the same error.
  141. The correct answer is C (The pattern suggests systemic upcoding that compromises data accuracy and coding integrity across the department.): Consistently resolving ambiguous documentation toward the highest reimbursement code, rather than querying for clarification, is a hallmark of upcoding and represents a serious integrity failure that distorts case mix funding and clinical data. Framing the pattern as efficiency or normal variation ignores the systemic bias toward financial benefit. The supervisor has an obligation to investigate and correct the pattern through proper query processes.
  142. The correct answer is A (Establish independent coding audits and separate coding decisions clearly from any financial performance targets.): Managing this structural conflict of interest requires building safeguards, such as independent audits and clear separation of coding decisions from financial incentives, so that documentation, not funding pressure, drives every code assigned. Encouraging higher codes crosses into upcoding, while coding artificially conservatively is its own form of inaccurate reporting. The director's obligation is objective accuracy in both directions, supported by structural safeguards.
  143. The correct answer is D (Document the pattern and raise it with appropriate leadership or the vendor for further investigation.): Ethical use of health information technology requires actively identifying and escalating potential accuracy or equity concerns rather than passively accepting vendor claims or silently working around the problem. A silent individual workaround does not address the systemic risk to other users or patients affected by the tool. Dismissing the pattern as coincidental without investigation risks allowing a genuine bias to continue affecting patient care decisions.
  144. The correct answer is A (Escalate the ambiguous guideline for formal clarification so both coders can apply a single consistent interpretation.): Consistent, accurate coding requires resolving genuinely ambiguous internal guidelines through formal clarification rather than allowing continued inconsistent interpretation or relying on an informal compromise that may not reflect correct application of official coding rules. Deferring automatically to seniority does not necessarily produce the technically correct interpretation. A formally clarified guideline protects data consistency and accuracy across the entire department going forward.
  145. The correct answer is C (Report the error to a supervisor and correct the record through the appropriate resubmission process.): Integrity requires correcting known errors promptly regardless of when they are discovered or whether detection is likely, because inaccurate data undermines funding accuracy, research, and patient care decisions. Leaving the error unaddressed or offsetting it elsewhere compounds the original problem and adds new inaccuracies to the record. Timely, transparent correction through proper channels is the accountable response expected of a certified professional.
  146. The correct answer is D (Refuse to unbundle the codes and explain that coding must follow official bundling guidelines.): Unbundling services that are properly billed together under a comprehensive code is a recognized form of improper billing regardless of whether each component was technically performed, because official coding guidelines define how such services must be reported together. A partial compromise or deferring to non coding staff does not make unbundling appropriate. Coding decisions must remain governed by official classification rules, not administrative pressure.
  147. The correct answer is B (Refuse to use the shared material and report the situation to the certifying body if appropriate.): Professional integrity extends to the certification process itself, and using improperly obtained exam content undermines the credibility of the credential for the entire profession, regardless of how common the practice may be within a particular group. Using the material privately or simply leaving without reporting both allow a genuine exam security breach to continue affecting other candidates. Reporting to the certifying body addresses the issue at its source.
  148. The correct answer is C (Seek additional training or supervision before completing the assignment rather than proceeding alone.): Maintaining competence means recognizing the limits of one's current knowledge and taking proactive steps to close gaps before performing tasks that affect data accuracy and patient care. Proceeding alone and relying on a later audit risks inaccurate data entering the health record and clinical or funding decisions. A permanent refusal is an overreaction when targeted training would resolve the gap.
  149. The correct answer is A (Escalate the finding for formal review since the access pattern lacks any apparent legitimate purpose.): Audit findings showing access without an apparent clinical relationship warrant formal investigation to determine whether the access was legitimate, since assuming an explanation without verification allows a genuine privacy breach to go unaddressed. Undocumented informal restrictions undermine accountability and due process. Waiting for repetition before raising a concern delays addressing a risk that has already been identified through the audit.
  150. The correct answer is D (Accessing patient information through an unsecured personal device increases the risk of unauthorized exposure.): Using an unsecured personal device to access sensitive patient information lacks the organizational security controls, such as encryption and monitored access, that protect health information, creating real risk regardless of the employee's intentions. A verbal promise not to save files locally does not address vulnerabilities like an unsecured network or shared household device. The prevalence of remote work generally does not eliminate the need for a properly secured device before accessing patient records.
  151. The correct answer is B (Prioritizing speed over accuracy risks compromising coding integrity and the reliability of health record data.): Coding integrity requires resolving genuine documentation ambiguity through the query process rather than guessing to save time, because skipped queries can produce inaccurate codes that affect funding, research, and patient care. Whether the target itself is reasonable is a separate operational question from the ethical duty to code accurately. Physician queries are a required part of accurate coding, not an optional convenience.
  152. The correct answer is B (Notify her supervisor promptly and initiate the correct amendment process to fix the record.): Accountability requires prompt disclosure of one's own errors so they can be corrected through a proper, documented amendment process that maintains an audit trail. Quietly changing the record without oversight can itself create integrity concerns about undocumented alterations. Assuming an error is inconsequential or delaying disclosure risks the error propagating into clinical, legal, or funding decisions.
  153. The correct answer is D (Share only general professional knowledge and decline to disclose another client's proprietary or confidential details.): Professional integrity as a consultant requires respecting confidentiality obligations owed to each client, distinguishing between general professional expertise, which she may reasonably apply broadly, and another client's specific proprietary information, which she may not disclose. A confidentiality promise from the new client does not authorize the original disclosure in the first place. Omitting the competitor's name does not remove the underlying breach if the methodology itself remains identifiable and proprietary.
  154. The correct answer is C (Decline to confirm or deny that the neighbour was ever a patient at the facility.): Even confirming that an individual was or was not a patient is protected information, since it reveals a fact about that person's health encounters. Without a valid authorization, no admission detail may be shared, however minor it may seem. Asking the caller's motive does not change the legal and ethical requirement to withhold the information regardless of the stated reason.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Infection of the renal pelvis and surrounding kidney tissue.): pyelo refers to the renal pelvis and nephr refers to the kidney, so pyelonephritis describes an ascending infection that has reached the upper urinary tract. Option B describes urethritis, a much more limited infection site. Option C describes cystitis, a lower urinary tract infection distinct from pyelonephritis. Option D describes prostatitis, an unrelated infection site entirely.
  2.   19. The correct answer is A (The coded data reflects the clinical complexity and resource intensity of patients treated.): Because ICD-10-CA and CCI codes capture information about diagnoses and interventions that reflects the clinical complexity and resource intensity of the patients a facility treats, this coded data can be used within funding models to help allocate resources more equitably based on actual patient care activity. The codes are not used to determine individual staff bonuses, do not replace a hospital's own financial reporting obligations, and have no relationship to visitor parking fees.
  20. The correct answer is C (The diagnosis code explains why the patient needed care.): Together, the diagnosis code and the intervention code provide a complete picture of an episode of care: the diagnosis code documents the reason the patient required care, while the intervention code documents what was actually performed in response. These two codes do not simply restate the same fact in different words, both are genuinely used in national reporting rather than one being discarded, and intervention coding still requires independent coder review rather than being automatically derived from the diagnosis.

WHO guidance is not rigidly binding in a way that forbids country-specific enhancement, Canadian standards are developed in direct relation to ICD-10-CA and CCI rather than independently of them, and these adaptations are intentional rather than accidental.
  23. The correct answer is A (Accurate coding requires understanding classification structure, conventions, and standards together.): Real clinical documentation and coding scenarios are often more complex than a simple word-for-word match against a printed index can resolve, so accurate, consistent coding depends on understanding classification structure, its conventions, and the detailed national standards built around it. Word matching alone is not sufficient for competent coding, classification theory is directly relevant to front-line coders performing daily code assignment, and national standards are designed to support, not contradict, correct application of the Alphabetical Index.
  24. The correct answer is A (They apply classification knowledge and coding standards to translate clinical documentation into accurate codes.): Health information management professionals apply their knowledge of classification structure and national coding standards to translate clinical documentation accurately into standardized codes, forming the essential link between what happens in patient care and the reliability of the resulting national health statistics. They do not make clinical treatment decisions, do not set national health policy unilaterally, and their responsibilities extend well beyond simple physical filing to include direct accountability for coded data quality.

This process has no connection to patient complaint volumes or conference hosting decisions, and it complements rather than replaces a facility's own internal coding quality review activities.

This definition intentionally looks at the whole course of the admission rather than at how the record was written or ordered. Option A understates the purpose of these codes, which extends well beyond annual public health surveillance. This distinction is essential for accurate mortality reporting and quality indicator calculation at the national level. The correct answer is D (A structured review comparing coded abstract data against source documentation to identify errors and inconsistencies.): A data quality audit in the DAD abstracting context is a structured, systematic process in which coded data on the abstract is compared against the original source documentation in the health record to identify coding errors, omissions, and inconsistencies that could affect data accuracy and downstream reporting. Options A and C describe unrelated activities, a financial audit and a patient satisfaction survey, that have nothing to do with coding quality review. Option B trivializes the audit process to a single superficial check, when in practice a data quality audit examines diagnosis selection, typing, sequencing, and intervention coding comprehensively.

  49. The correct answer is D (It provides the clinical evidence coders rely on to assign codes according to coding standards.): Physician documentation is the foundation coders use to select, type, and sequence diagnoses accurately, since ICD-10-CA and CCI coding conventions require that codes be supported by clear clinical evidence recorded in the health record. Without adequate documentation, coders cannot appropriately determine MRDx, diagnosis type, or intervention codes. Option A is incorrect because coding relies on the full clinical picture documented by the physician, not laboratory results alone, option B understates documentation's central coding role by framing it as primarily legal, and option C is false since standardized classification systems like ICD-10-CA remain necessary regardless of documentation quality.
  50. The correct answer is D (One patient's paired values for length of stay and total charges.): In a scatter plot, each plotted point represents one observation, in this case one patient, positioned according to that patient's value on each of the two variables being compared, length of stay on one axis and total charges on the other. This allows a viewer to visually assess whether a relationship, or correlation, exists between the two variables across the full set of patients.
  51. The correct answer is D (To display data plotted in time order to help identify trends, shifts, or patterns before formal statistical control limits are calculated.): A run chart plots data points in the order they occurred over time, typically with a median line, and is used to visually detect trends, shifts, or unusual runs of points before a team invests in calculating formal statistical control limits for a control chart. It is a simpler, faster first step in quality improvement analysis that does not require the statistical calculations a control chart demands.
  52. The correct answer is B (An experimental design, specifically a randomized controlled trial.): Random assignment of participants to an intervention group and a comparison group, followed by measurement of outcomes, is the defining feature of an experimental design, and when applied to a health intervention this is specifically called a randomized controlled trial. This design allows researchers to more confidently attribute differences in outcome to the intervention itself, since random assignment helps balance other characteristics between groups.
  53. The correct answer is C (A case-control study.): A case-control study begins by identifying individuals who already have the outcome of interest, the cases, and a comparable group who do not, the controls, then looks backward in time to compare their prior exposures, making it a retrospective design. This differs from a cohort study, which instead begins with a group defined by exposure status and follows them forward in time to see who develops the outcome.
  54. The correct answer is D (A cohort study.): A cohort study defines a group based on exposure status at the start of the study and follows that group forward in time to observe who develops the outcome of interest, which allows the calculation of incidence and relative risk. A case-control study instead works backward from an already-observed outcome, while a cross-sectional study captures exposure and outcome at a single point in time without any forward or backward tracking.
  55. The correct answer is C (A cross-sectional study.): A cross-sectional study captures information about exposure and outcome simultaneously at one point in time, providing a snapshot of a population rather than tracking changes over time, which makes it useful for estimating prevalence but limited in its ability to establish which factor preceded the other. Because there is no follow-up period and no comparison group defined by prior exposure or outcome status, this design differs from both cohort and case-control studies.
  56. The correct answer is A (To review the necessity, appropriateness, and efficiency of healthcare services provided to patients.): Utilization management is the ongoing process of evaluating whether healthcare services being provided are medically necessary, delivered at the appropriate level of care, and provided efficiently, often through activities such as admission review, concurrent review, and discharge planning. It is distinct from credentialing, which governs physician privileges, and from records retention, which is a separate health information management function.
  57. The correct answer is B (Concurrent review.): Concurrent review takes place while a patient is still receiving care, evaluating on an ongoing basis whether continued admission and the current level of service remain medically necessary and appropriate. Prospective review instead occurs before admission to determine necessity in advance, and retrospective review occurs after discharge, typically as part of billing audits or quality analysis.
  58. The correct answer is D (Prospective review.): Prospective review evaluates medical necessity and appropriateness before a service is delivered, such as requiring prior authorization for a planned elective admission, allowing potential denials or alternative recommendations to be made before the patient incurs the cost of care. This differs from concurrent review, which happens during an active stay, and retrospective review, which happens only after the encounter has already concluded.
  59. The correct answer is B (To compare an organization's performance data against external standards or peer organizations to identify gaps and opportunities for improvement.): Benchmarking involves comparing an organization's own performance data, such as infection rates or average length of stay, against external standards, best practices, or peer organizations, in order to identify performance gaps and set meaningful improvement targets. Without an external or standardized comparison point, an organization has no reliable way to judge whether its own results represent strong, average, or poor performance.
  60. The correct answer is A (Continuous quality improvement.): Continuous quality improvement is characterized by ongoing measurement of a process or outcome over time combined with structured, iterative testing of changes, commonly through a Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle, to determine whether an intervention produces a meaningful and sustained improvement. This differs fundamentally from a one-time research study or a utilization review determination, both of which serve different, more narrowly defined purposes.
  61. The correct answer is A (The median.): The median is the middle value in an ordered dataset and is determined by position rather than magnitude, so a small number of extreme outliers has little to no effect on its value. The mean, by contrast, is calculated using the sum of all values, so even one or two very large outliers can pull the mean substantially higher than what most of the data actually show.
  62. The correct answer is C (The mode.): The mode is simply the most frequently occurring value in a dataset and does not require the data to have a meaningful numeric order or be suitable for arithmetic operations, which makes it the only measure of central tendency that can be meaningfully applied to categorical data such as discharge disposition codes. The mean and median both require data that can be ordered or summed, which categorical labels generally cannot support.
  63. The correct answer is C (All three measures should be approximately equal to one another.): In a symmetric, unimodal distribution with no skew or outliers, the mean, median, and mode converge on approximately the same central value, since the data are balanced evenly around the centre of the distribution. When a distribution is skewed, however, the mean shifts in the direction of the skew more strongly than the median, which is one reason analysts compare all three measures rather than relying on a single one.
  64. The correct answer is C (How much individual values typically differ from the mean of the dataset.): Standard deviation quantifies the typical amount by which individual data values differ, or deviate, from the dataset's mean, providing a measure of how spread out or clustered the values are around that central point. A small standard deviation indicates values are clustered tightly around the mean, while a large standard deviation indicates greater variability among individual values.
  65. The correct answer is A (Patient stays in Unit A vary more widely around the average than stays in Unit B.): A larger standard deviation with an identical mean indicates that individual patient length of stay values in Unit A are more spread out, or variable, around the shared average of 4.5 days, meaning Unit A likely has a mix of both notably short and notably long stays. Unit B's smaller standard deviation suggests its patient stays cluster more tightly and consistently around that same average value.
  66. The correct answer is C (It provides a daily count of patients present in the facility, forming the basis for calculating occupancy and utilization statistics.): The inpatient census is a daily count of patients physically present in the facility at a specific point in time, typically midnight, and this daily figure feeds directly into calculations for bed occupancy rate, average daily census, and other core utilization statistics. Without an accurate daily census, downstream utilization indicators calculated over a longer reporting period would also be inaccurate.
  67. The correct answer is C (The average number of inpatients present in the facility on any given day during that month.): Average daily census represents, on average, how many inpatients were physically present in the facility on any given day across the reporting period, calculated by summing each day's census and dividing by the number of days. This differs from bed occupancy rate, which further relates that average census figure to the facility's total available bed capacity to express utilization as a percentage.
  68. The correct answer is B (Bed occupancy rate is calculated by dividing average daily census by the total bed count and expressing the result as a percentage.): Bed occupancy rate can be calculated by dividing average daily census by the total number of beds available and multiplying by 100 to express it as a percentage, since both figures relate to how many patients, on average, are using the facility's bed capacity. This relationship shows why these utilization statistics are typically reviewed together rather than in isolation during capacity planning.
  69. The correct answer is C (The group of individuals who are genuinely capable of experiencing the event being measured, given their characteristics.): Population at risk refers to the group of individuals who are genuinely capable of experiencing the event being measured, such as women of childbearing age when calculating a maternal mortality rate, rather than the entire general population, some of whom could never experience that outcome. Using an inappropriate denominator that includes people who are not truly at risk produces a rate that understates the true frequency of the event among the relevant group.
  70. The correct answer is B (The calculated rate will be artificially low because the denominator includes many individuals who could never experience the outcome.): Using total regional population instead of live births as the denominator introduces a large number of individuals into the calculation who are not part of the population genuinely at risk of infant mortality, which inflates the denominator and produces an artificially low, and therefore inaccurate, rate. This illustrates why correctly identifying the true population at risk is essential to producing a meaningful and comparable statistic.
  71. The correct answer is B (It controls for underlying differences in population composition that could otherwise distort a direct comparison of rates.): Stratifying data by a relevant variable, such as age group, allows an analyst to compare rates within similar subgroups rather than only comparing crude overall rates, which can be misleading if the two populations being compared have very different underlying compositions, such as one population being much older on average. This technique helps isolate whether a true difference exists after accounting for a known confounding characteristic.
  72. The correct answer is D (Without a shared or mapped terminology standard, coded data from one system may not translate correctly.): When a problem list is coded in a locally developed terminology rather than SNOMED CT, a receiving SNOMED CT based system has no way to correctly interpret those local codes unless a mapping has been established between the two terminologies, so the display failure is a terminology mismatch rather than a system malfunction. Option A incorrectly dismisses the terminology explanation in favor of assuming a malfunction, and options B and C both wrongly assume compatibility or consistent display can occur without any mapping process, which is not how differing terminology standards behave. This scenario is a direct example of why terminology mapping matters for accurate cross-system display.
  73. Option A incorrectly demands identical software everywhere, which is not what interoperability requires since different systems can still exchange data through shared standards, option B incorrectly suggests eliminating standards altogether would help, which is the opposite of how interoperability is achieved, and option D wrongly frames interoperability as a billing matter rather than a clinical continuity goal.   78.   81.   82. Option A incorrectly assigns both standard types to unrelated functions, billing and scheduling, that do not reflect their actual purposes, option B incorrectly claims no meaningful distinction exists between the four, and option D reverses the true roles by claiming SNOMED CT and LOINC define transmission while HL7 and FHIR define clinical meaning.   84.   88. Option A describes an overlay, a different problem where two different individuals are incorrectly merged under one identifier, which is not what is happening here since only one patient is involved.   89. The correct answer is B (A situation where one patient's identifying information and clinical data are incorrectly combined with or attached.): An overlay error occurs when one patient's identifying and clinical information is mistakenly combined with or attached to a different, unrelated patient's record, creating a dangerous mix of two people's data under one identifier. Option A actually describes a duplicate record situation, not an overlay, since it involves the same patient with two separate identifiers rather than two different patients merged together. Options C and D describe unrelated processes, record archiving and standardized documentation, neither of which defines an overlay error in a master patient index.
  90. The correct answer is D (An overlay mixes two different patients' clinical information together.): An overlay is more dangerous than a straightforward duplicate record because it combines two entirely different patients' clinical information under a single identifier, meaning a clinician could act on the wrong patient's allergies, diagnoses, or medications without any warning that the data belongs to someone else. A duplicate record, by contrast, still keeps each patient's information separate, just split across more than one identifier for the same person, which is a data completeness problem rather than a direct clinical safety hazard. Options A and B understate the real danger of overlays, and option C incorrectly claims duplicates are difficult to create, when they are actually a common and well documented master patient index issue.
  91. The correct answer is A (Record linkage, within the context of a Canadian health information system.): Record linkage is the specific process that uses demographic details, such as name, date of birth, and address, combined with probabilistic or deterministic matching algorithms, to determine whether two records likely belong to the same individual. Overlay creation (B) refers to the erroneous merging of two different patients' data, interoperability mapping (C) refers to translating coded data between systems, and terminology standardization (D) refers to using a consistent clinical vocabulary like SNOMED CT, none of which describe the demographic matching process being asked about. Record linkage is the precise term for this identity matching function.
  92. The correct answer is C (To reliably link a single patient's records across different systems and locations without confusing them with another patient.): The primary purpose of a unique enterprise identifier in a master patient index is to reliably link one patient's records across multiple facilities and systems within a health region while ensuring that identifier is never confused with a different patient's records. Option A incorrectly claims the identifier replaces demographic information, when demographics are still used alongside the identifier for verification, option B incorrectly assigns the identifier a diagnosis coding function it does not have, and option D incorrectly frames the identifier as primarily a billing reference with limited identification value, when identification across systems is its core purpose.
