  50. The correct answer is A (Calculate age-adjusted mortality rates for both populations before comparing them.): Age-adjustment, sometimes called standardization, mathematically accounts for differences in the age composition of two populations before their rates are compared, which removes age as a confounding factor and allows for a fairer comparison of underlying mortality risk. Comparing crude rates directly, without this adjustment, would make the hospital's elderly patient population appear to have a much worse outcome even if age-specific mortality risk were actually similar between the two groups.
  51. The correct answer is C (Validity refers to whether the instrument actually measures the concept it is intended to measure.): Validity concerns whether an instrument genuinely measures the underlying concept it claims to measure, such as a satisfaction survey truly capturing patient satisfaction rather than an unrelated concept like general mood. This is distinct from reliability, which concerns whether the instrument produces consistent, repeatable results when applied under similar conditions, regardless of whether it is measuring the right thing.
  52. The correct answer is B (Validity.): A tool can be highly reliable, meaning it produces consistent and repeatable results, while still lacking validity, meaning it fails to actually measure the intended underlying concept, and these are two separate and independent properties of any measurement instrument. In this scenario the consistency described indicates strong reliability, but the failure to capture the intended concept indicates a validity problem.
  53. The correct answer is A (Quantitative data are numeric and measurable, while qualitative data are descriptive and typically capture themes, opinions, or experiences.): Quantitative data consist of numeric, measurable values that can be statistically analyzed, such as length of stay or infection rates, while qualitative data are descriptive in nature, often gathered through interviews or open-ended survey responses, and are used to capture themes, opinions, or lived experiences that numbers alone cannot express. Neither type is inherently more accurate; the appropriate choice depends on the nature of the research question being asked.
  54. The correct answer is B (A qualitative method, such as structured interviews.): Understanding the underlying reasons behind a patient's decision or experience typically requires a qualitative approach, such as structured or semi-structured interviews, which can capture nuance, context, and patient perspective in ways that a purely numeric count cannot. The readmission rate itself, a quantitative measure, tells the researcher how often the event occurred but says nothing about the patient's own reasoning behind it.
  55. The correct answer is B (To ensure participants understand the nature, risks, and purpose of the study and voluntarily agree to take part.): Informed consent ensures that research participants understand what the study involves, including its purpose, procedures, potential risks, and their right to withdraw, and that they agree to participate voluntarily and with full knowledge of these facts. This is a foundational ethical requirement in research involving health information and does not, by itself, have any bearing on whether the study's results turn out to be statistically significant.
  56. The correct answer is B (To evaluate whether the study protects participant rights, safety, and privacy while weighing potential risks against expected benefits.): A research ethics board is responsible for reviewing a proposed study to ensure it adequately protects the rights, safety, privacy, and dignity of participants, and that the potential risks of participation are reasonable relative to the expected benefits of the research. This ethical oversight is a required step before data collection begins for any study involving human participants or their health information.
  57. The correct answer is D (Selecting a representative subset of a larger population to study, rather than collecting data from every individual in that population.): Sampling involves selecting a subset of individuals from a larger population of interest, using a defined method intended to make that subset representative of the whole, which allows researchers to draw conclusions about the broader population without the time and cost of collecting data from every single individual. A sample that is not representative, due to a flawed selection method, can introduce bias that undermines the validity of the study's conclusions.
  58. The correct answer is A (Systematic sampling.): Systematic sampling selects participants at a fixed, regular interval, such as every tenth chart, from an ordered list, which is a straightforward method that can approximate a random sample as long as the ordering of the list is not related in some way to the characteristic being studied. This differs from simple random sampling, where each individual has an equal and independently determined probability of selection using a random process rather than a fixed pattern.
  59. The correct answer is A (Evaluation of medical necessity and appropriateness of care after the patient has already been discharged.): Retrospective review occurs after a patient's episode of care has concluded, typically as part of billing audits, quality analysis, or claims review, evaluating whether the services provided were medically necessary and appropriately documented after the fact. This differs from prospective review, which occurs before the service is delivered, and concurrent review, which occurs during an active admission.
  60. The correct answer is B (Retrospective review.): Reviewing a pattern of already-denied claims from the past year is a retrospective activity because it examines completed episodes of care and billing outcomes after the fact, even though the ultimate goal of identifying patterns may be to prevent future denials. The defining feature of retrospective review is the timing of the review relative to when care was delivered, not the purpose the findings will later serve.
  61. The correct answer is D (A proportion.): Because the number of beds occupied by patients with the condition is a subset of the total bed count at that point in time, this calculation is a proportion, which simply expresses what fraction of a whole a specific part represents without necessarily involving a defined time period or a standard population base. A rate would be more appropriate if the analyst instead wanted to express new cases occurring over a defined time interval relative to a population at risk.
  62. The correct answer is C (Hospitals may treat patients with very different levels of clinical complexity, and unadjusted comparisons can be misleading without accounting for this.): Case-mix adjustment accounts for differences in the complexity, severity, and type of patients treated at different facilities, since a hospital that primarily treats highly complex cases would be expected to have a longer average length of stay than one treating mostly routine cases, even if both are equally efficient. Without this adjustment, a direct comparison of raw average length of stay figures between two hospitals with very different patient populations risks producing a misleading conclusion about relative performance.
  63. The correct answer is D (Reduced average length of stay and improved bed availability without compromising patient outcomes.): Shifting appropriate elective procedures from an inpatient overnight stay to a same-day surgical pathway can reduce average length of stay for the affected admissions and free up inpatient bed capacity for patients who genuinely require an overnight stay, all while maintaining safe, high-quality outcomes for patients whose conditions are appropriate for same-day care. This is a common utilization management strategy aimed at improving efficiency without expanding physical bed capacity.
  64. The correct answer is D (How often each distinct value or range of values occurs within a dataset.): A frequency distribution table organizes raw data by listing each distinct value, or range of values, along with a count of how many times that value occurs within the dataset, providing the foundation from which charts such as histograms are typically built. This differs from a measure of correlation, which describes the relationship between two separate variables rather than simply summarizing how often values occur.
  65. The correct answer is C (The mean.): The mean is calculated by summing all values and dividing by the count, so it is sensitive to every value in the dataset, including a smaller number of unusually high values forming a long tail, which pulls the mean in that direction. The median, being based on position rather than magnitude, is comparatively resistant to this effect, which is why analysts often report both the mean and median together when a distribution is skewed.
  66. The correct answer is D (A proportion.): Because the 14 records with errors are a subset of the 200 total records reviewed, expressing this relationship as a percentage produces a proportion, which is the correct statistical term regardless of whether the underlying subject matter concerns disease. Rates specifically describe the frequency of events over a defined time period relative to a population at risk, which is not the framing used in this coding accuracy audit example.
  67. The correct answer is C (It allows rates calculated from populations of very different sizes to be meaningfully compared with one another.): Using a standardized population base, such as expressing a rate per 1,000 or per 100,000 people, allows analysts to meaningfully compare rates calculated from populations of very different total sizes, since a raw count of cases alone says little without knowing the size of the population from which it arose. Without this standardization, a region with a larger overall population would tend to show a higher raw case count even if its true underlying rate of disease were actually lower.
  68. The correct answer is D (Whether both provinces used the same case definition and diagnostic criteria when identifying cases.): Even after adjusting for differences in age structure between two populations, a valid comparison of disease rates also requires that both provinces applied the same case definition and diagnostic criteria when identifying and counting cases, since differences in how a disease is defined or diagnosed can create the appearance of a rate difference that does not actually reflect a true difference in disease occurrence. This reflects the same consistency principle that applies broadly across health information data quality.
  69. Using an unrelated denominator, such as total hospital discharges, would not produce a meaningful or standard fetal death statistic.
  70. The correct answer is B (High inter-rater agreement, where different coders assign the same code to the same case, indicates strong reliability of the coding process.): Inter-rater agreement measures how consistently different coders assign the same code to the same clinical documentation, and high agreement across coders indicates strong reliability of the coding process itself. However, reliability alone does not guarantee validity, since multiple coders could consistently agree on an incorrect code, which would represent high reliability paired with poor accuracy.
  71. The correct answer is D (A point prevalence measure.): Counting how many currently hospitalized patients have a pressure injury on one specific audit day is an example of point prevalence, which captures all existing cases, whether recently developed or longer-standing, at a single moment in time rather than tracking new occurrences over an extended period. This differs from incidence, which would instead track how many NEW pressure injuries developed during a defined time period among patients who did not previously have one.

Option B incorrectly describes deterministic linkage's exact-match requirement as if it were probabilistic linkage's defining feature, reversing the two methods. A newborn temporarily registered under the mother's identifier is a classic overlay risk because the infant's own clinical events (feeding, weight, treatments) can be documented against the mother's chart if the separate newborn record is not created and cross-referenced promptly. This is distinct from a simple duplicate, where the same patient has two separate unmerged records; here it is two different patients sharing one identifier.   78. The correct answer is B (Poor matching could either merge unrelated patients' data together or fail to link a single patient's own records together.): In a health information exchange, multiple organizations are contributing and pulling records for the same population, so a matching algorithm that is too loose risks creating overlays by merging different patients, while one that is too strict risks leaving one patient's records fragmented across sites as unlinked duplicates. Both failure modes undermine the core purpose of the exchange, which is to give clinicians a single, accurate, complete view of a patient regardless of where they were treated. This audit trail is essential for accountability and for reconstructing the record's history if the merge is later questioned.   80. This multi-field approach reduces both the risk of failing to link the same patient's existing record (creating a duplicate) and the risk of incorrectly linking two different patients (creating an overlay). Without this cross-organizational matching function, an HIE could not reliably assemble a complete picture of a patient's care. Option B incorrectly claims the MPI loses relevance once data crosses organizations, when in fact that is exactly when it matters most; options C and D wrongly limit its scope to imaging or claim it is made obsolete by messaging standards like HL7/FHIR, which handle data transport, not patient identity matching.
  87. The correct answer is D (To ensure uncertain, low confidence matches are verified by a person.): Requiring manual review only for matches scored below a defined confidence threshold reflects a risk-based approach: matches the algorithm is highly confident about can proceed automatically, while uncertain matches, where the risk of either a missed link or a false merge is higher, are routed to a trained person for verification before any action is taken. This balances efficiency with patient safety by focusing limited human review time where it adds the most value. Option A is wrong because it would require reviewing even high-confidence matches unnecessarily, option B eliminates the efficiency benefit of automated matching entirely, and option C misapplies manual review to matches that are already highly reliable.
  88. The correct answer is B (Nearly every downstream clinical, administrative, and analytic use of a patient's data depends on that data.): The master patient index sits upstream of virtually every other function in a health information system, since clinical documentation, billing, scheduling, quality reporting, and analytics all rely on the patient being correctly and uniquely identified first. If the MPI itself is inaccurate, errors propagate outward into every system and process that depends on patient identity, which is why MPI quality is described as foundational. Option A wrongly limits its impact to scheduling alone, option C incorrectly claims MPI quality only matters for organizations without electronic systems, and option D falsely suggests MPI problems resolve themselves without deliberate governance, when in fact they require active management.
  89. The correct answer is B (A possible overlay in which her identifier has become incorrectly linked to another patient's data.): A patient seeing lab results on her portal that belong to someone else strongly suggests her identifier has been incorrectly connected to another individual's clinical data, which is the hallmark of an overlay rather than a simple unresolved duplicate. This should be treated as an urgent patient safety and privacy issue requiring immediate investigation of how the identifiers became linked. Option A incorrectly frames this as an error-free outcome, and options C and D wrongly minimize the issue as an imaging-only interoperability concern or as a routine, expected result of registration, when it is neither routine nor expected.
  90. The correct answer is D (Investigate further using additional identifying details before taking any merge action, to avoid mistakenly creating an overlay.): When identity is not yet certain, the appropriate step is to gather more corroborating information, such as additional demographic details, prior visit history, or direct patient confirmation, before merging, because a premature merge based on insufficient certainty risks creating an overlay by combining two different patients' data. This cautious, evidence-based approach protects both data integrity and patient safety. Option A is dangerous because it merges without adequate verification, option B is excessive and would destroy legitimate clinical data, and option C ignores a real potential duplicate that needs resolution.
  91. The correct answer is A (Providing structured training and hands-on practice with the new system well before the go-live date.): Frontline staff transition most smoothly to a new clinical information system when they have had structured, hands-on training and practice time before the system actually goes live, so they can build competence and confidence in a low-stakes environment rather than learning entirely on the fly during real patient care. Adequate pre-go-live training reduces errors, frustration, and workflow disruption during the critical early period after launch. Options B, C, and D each describe withholding notice, support, or the opportunity to ask questions, all of which would increase staff resistance and the likelihood of a difficult transition.
  92. The correct answer is D (To test the system with a smaller group of users in a controlled setting.): A pilot phase is designed to roll the new system out to a limited group of users or a single unit first, allowing the project team to identify technical issues, workflow gaps, and training needs in a controlled, lower-risk setting before committing to a full organization-wide launch. Lessons learned during the pilot inform adjustments that improve the eventual broader rollout. Option A incorrectly frames the pilot as a permanent limitation with no plan to expand, and options B and C are wrong because a pilot is itself a form of structured testing and does not eliminate the need for staff training.
  93. The correct answer is C (Engaging staff early, communicating the reasons for change, and involving frontline users in planning and feedback.): Resistance from long-tenured staff accustomed to paper-based workflows is best addressed through genuine early engagement, clear communication about why the change is happening, and giving frontline users a real voice in planning and providing feedback, since people are far more likely to accept a change they helped shape and understand. This approach builds trust and buy-in rather than simply imposing the change on staff. Options A, B, and D all describe reducing or eliminating staff input and communication, which would predictably increase resistance rather than reduce it.
  94. The correct answer is D (The minimum necessary or need-to-know principle, limiting access to only the information required for a staff member's specific role.): Role-based access controls that limit what an individual staff member can view based on their specific job function reflect the minimum necessary, or need-to-know, principle, which holds that individuals should be granted access only to the specific information genuinely required to perform their role, rather than blanket access to an entire record. This reduces the risk of unauthorized viewing or misuse of sensitive information unrelated to a staff member's actual responsibilities. It does not eliminate legitimate access for those who do need broader information for their role, such as the treating physician. Well-designed role-based access is a foundational technical safeguard expected under most provincial health privacy frameworks.
  95. The correct answer is C (A locked door and controlled key card access restricting entry to a records storage room.): A locked door with controlled key card access restricting who may physically enter a records storage room is a classic example of a physical safeguard, meaning a measure that controls physical access to the location where information or systems are kept, as distinct from technical safeguards like encryption or passwords, and administrative safeguards like training or policy. Recognizing this three-part categorization, commonly used in privacy and security frameworks, helps organizations ensure they are addressing all necessary layers of protection rather than over-relying on only one category. A comprehensive security program combines physical, technical, and administrative safeguards working together. Missing any one category can leave a meaningful gap even if the other two are strong.
  96. The correct answer is C (To reduce the risk that an unattended, still logged-in session could be exploited by an unauthorized individual.): An automatic session timeout that logs a user out after a period of inactivity is a technical safeguard specifically designed to reduce the risk that an unattended workstation, left logged in, could be exploited by someone else walking by to view or misuse patient information without proper authorization. This is a common and relatively low-friction control that meaningfully reduces a real, everyday risk in busy clinical environments where staff frequently step away from workstations. It does not delete any underlying data, only ends the active session requiring the user to re-authenticate. This type of control is often paired with a general policy requiring staff to manually lock their workstation whenever stepping away.
  97. The correct answer is D (To enable detection and investigation of unauthorized or inappropriate access to patient information after the fact.): Detailed audit logs recording who accessed which patient record, and when, exist primarily to enable detection and investigation of unauthorized or inappropriate access, such as an employee inappropriately viewing a record with no legitimate clinical purpose, after the fact, supporting both proactive monitoring and reactive breach investigation. This detective control complements, but does not replace, preventive controls such as access restrictions and authentication at the point of login, since a determined insider with legitimate credentials could still misuse their access without a log to later detect the pattern. Audit logs also support accountability and can be an important source of evidence in a privacy breach investigation. Payroll tracking is not the purpose of this type of clinical system audit logging.
  98. The correct answer is A (The risk that a lost or stolen laptop would expose readable patient data to whoever obtains the device.): Encrypting data stored on portable devices such as laptops directly addresses the risk that if the device is lost or stolen, the patient data on it would remain unreadable and effectively protected from unauthorized access by whoever comes into possession of the device, absent the proper decryption key or credentials. This is a widely recommended technical safeguard specifically because portable devices carry a meaningfully higher risk of loss or theft compared to information kept on secured servers within a controlled facility. Properly implemented encryption does not prevent authorized staff with the correct credentials from accessing the data when needed. This safeguard significantly reduces, though does not eliminate, the overall risk profile associated with using portable devices for clinical work outside a secured environment.
  99. The correct answer is A (Using an encrypted or secure email transmission method.): Using an encrypted or otherwise secure transmission method, such as a dedicated secure health information exchange portal or an encrypted email or messaging service, is a recognized technical safeguard appropriate for sending sensitive health information to external recipients, since standard unencrypted email can be intercepted or misdirected without adequate protection. Sending information through a personal, non-work email account would also generally violate organizational policy and create significant security and accountability gaps, since personal accounts typically lack the same institutional security controls and audit capability. Posting sensitive information on a public website would be a severe privacy breach rather than any form of legitimate transmission. Clear policy guidance and staff training on approved secure transmission methods help prevent inadvertent use of insecure channels.
  100. The correct answer is D (It allows for individual accountability by linking specific system actions to a specific, identifiable user.): Requiring unique, individually assigned login credentials rather than a shared generic login allows the system to attribute specific actions, such as viewing or modifying a particular record, to a specific, identifiable individual, supporting meaningful audit logging, accountability, and effective investigation of any suspected inappropriate access. A shared login makes it effectively impossible to determine which specific staff member performed a given action, significantly undermining both accountability and the practical usefulness of audit logs. Unique credentials still require appropriate password protection and do not by themselves grant unrestricted access, since role-based permissions typically remain layered on top of individual authentication. This is considered a foundational technical and administrative safeguard in any health information system.
  101. The correct answer is B (A written policy requiring regular mandatory privacy and security training for all staff with access.): A written policy requiring regular, mandatory privacy and security training for staff is a classic example of an administrative safeguard, meaning an organizational policy, procedure, or program designed to manage and reduce risk through people and process, as distinct from a technical safeguard like a firewall or encryption protocol, or a physical safeguard like a locked cabinet. Administrative safeguards also commonly include background checks, role definitions, incident response procedures, and periodic policy review processes. A comprehensive security program requires all three categories working together, since strong technical controls can still be undermined by staff who are inadequately trained on proper practices. Recognizing which category a given safeguard falls into helps organizations ensure they have not neglected any one dimension of their overall security posture.
  102. The correct answer is B (That protecting health information also involves safeguarding the availability and integrity of the systems storing it.): Environmental monitoring protecting server infrastructure from conditions like excessive heat or humidity reflects the broader recognition that information security includes protecting the availability and integrity of systems storing health information, not solely preventing unauthorized human access or viewing, since equipment failure from an uncontrolled environment could result in data loss or corruption just as seriously as a security breach. This reflects the common security framework addressing confidentiality, integrity, and availability together as core objectives. Focusing exclusively on preventing unauthorized viewing while neglecting environmental or system availability risks would leave an important gap in an organization's overall protection of patient information. Physical infrastructure safeguards like this often work alongside access controls to provide comprehensive protection for critical health information systems.
  103. The correct answer is C (Clear contractual commitments regarding data security safeguards, breach notification obligations, data location, and a clear delineation of vendor responsibilities.): When reviewing a vendor contract for hosting sensitive patient health information, the most important security-related provisions to confirm include clear, specific commitments regarding the vendor's data security safeguards, its obligations to promptly notify the custodian of any breach affecting the data, where the data will be physically stored or processed, and a clear delineation of the vendor's responsibilities for protecting the information consistent with the custodian's own legal obligations. These provisions are essential because the custodian generally remains legally accountable for the information even though a third-party vendor is providing the hosting service. Irrelevant business details, such as a vendor's interface colour scheme or unrelated product lines, have no bearing on the security adequacy of the arrangement. Thorough vendor due diligence and contract review is an important HIM and privacy governance responsibility, particularly as more health information moves to cloud-based systems.
  104. The correct answer is D (To reduce the risk associated with a compromised password remaining valid and exploitable indefinitely.): A policy requiring periodic password changes and prohibiting reuse of recent previous passwords is intended to reduce the window during which a compromised password, whether obtained through phishing, a data breach elsewhere, or another means, remains valid and could be exploited by an unauthorized party to gain access to the system. This measure works alongside other access controls, such as strong password complexity requirements and multi-factor authentication where available, rather than replacing the need for them. While password policies do require some additional staff effort, their purpose is specifically security risk reduction rather than simply imposing burden for its own sake. Many current security frameworks now favour multi-factor authentication and monitoring for compromised credentials as complementary or even alternative approaches alongside traditional password rotation policies.
  105. The correct answer is A (To proactively identify security vulnerabilities before a genuine malicious actor can exploit them.): Penetration testing involves authorized security professionals simulating the techniques a real attacker might use in order to proactively identify vulnerabilities in a system's defenses before a genuine malicious actor has the opportunity to exploit them, allowing the organization to remediate weaknesses in a controlled, deliberate manner. This is a recognized proactive technical safeguard and stands in contrast to actually causing an uncontrolled data breach, which is never the intended outcome of a properly scoped and authorized test. This practice is relevant and valuable across many sectors, including healthcare, given the sensitivity of the information involved and the significant potential harm from a successful attack. Results from penetration testing typically feed into a broader vulnerability remediation and security improvement program rather than any unrelated human resources decision.
  106. The correct answer is D (It requires a user to provide two or more independent forms of verification.): Multi-factor authentication strengthens access security by requiring a user to provide two or more independent forms of verification, commonly something the user knows such as a password, combined with something the user has such as a one-time code sent to a registered device, meaning that even if one factor, like a password, is compromised through phishing or another means, an unauthorized party would still need the second factor to gain access. This layered approach significantly reduces the practical risk of unauthorized access compared to relying on a password alone. It does not eliminate the password requirement but adds an additional, independent layer beyond it. Given the sensitivity of health information, multi-factor authentication is increasingly considered an important, sometimes expected, safeguard for systems storing this type of data.
  107. The correct answer is B (A physical safeguard, such as repositioning monitors, installing privacy screens.): The risk of patient information being visible on a screen to unauthorized passersby in a public hallway is fundamentally a physical safeguard issue, best addressed through measures such as repositioning monitors away from public sightlines, installing privacy screens that limit the viewing angle, or otherwise adjusting the physical layout of the workstation area. Encryption, password policies, and retention schedules, while all important safeguards in their own right, do not directly address this particular type of visual exposure risk occurring while an authorized user is actively and legitimately using the system. This scenario illustrates why physical environment design is a genuinely important, sometimes underappreciated, dimension of health information security alongside technical and administrative measures. Regular walkthroughs of clinical areas can help identify this type of overlooked physical vulnerability.
  108. The correct answer is D (Access should be assigned based on the minimum level genuinely necessary for each specific role.): An access control matrix should be designed around the minimum necessary, or least privilege, principle, meaning each defined role is granted only the specific level and scope of access genuinely required to perform its legitimate functions, rather than defaulting to broad or unrestricted access for convenience. This proactive, preventive control reduces the overall risk surface by limiting what any individual account could potentially access even before any misuse occurs. While audit logs remain an important detective control for identifying misuse after the fact, they do not substitute for the preventive value of properly scoped access in the first place. A well-designed access control matrix is typically reviewed and updated as roles, systems, and organizational needs evolve over time.
  109. The correct answer is B (Availability of the health information system, ensuring authorized users can continue to access critical patient information.): A properly functioning backup power system that maintains uninterrupted operation of the electronic health record system during a power outage primarily supports the availability objective within the widely recognized confidentiality, integrity, and availability framework for information security, ensuring that authorized clinical staff can continue accessing critical patient information when needed for ongoing care, rather than experiencing a disruptive outage at a potentially critical moment. While confidentiality and integrity remain separately important security objectives, addressed through different safeguards such as access controls and data validation checks, this specific measure is most directly aimed at maintaining system availability. Retention scheduling for archived paper records is an unrelated records management function rather than a technical continuity measure. Recognizing which specific objective a given safeguard addresses helps organizations ensure balanced coverage across all three dimensions.
  110. The correct answer is D (The risk that a former employee with no ongoing legitimate need could continue to access patient.): Promptly revoking a departing employee's system access upon termination directly mitigates the risk that a former staff member, who no longer has any legitimate role-based need for access, could continue to view or misuse patient information after their employment relationship has ended, whether intentionally or simply because the access was never properly deactivated. This is a critical, sometimes overlooked, administrative and technical safeguard, since former employee accounts left active represent a meaningful and avoidable vulnerability. It has no bearing on storage capacity, current employees' own password management, or patients' independent access rights, which are governed by entirely separate processes. A well-designed offboarding checklist that includes immediate access revocation is a standard component of mature health information security programs.
  111. The correct answer is A (Unmanaged personal devices may lack adequate security controls.): Allowing remote access from personal, unmanaged devices without additional security controls raises significant concerns because such devices may lack the encryption, current security patching, endpoint malware protection, and centralized management controls typically applied to institutionally managed devices, increasing the risk that a compromised or inadequately secured personal device could become a pathway for unauthorized access to or exfiltration of sensitive patient data. This is why many healthcare organizations implement specific requirements, such as mandatory use of a secure virtual private network, endpoint security software, and multi-factor authentication, before permitting any remote access, regardless of device ownership. Remote access itself is not categorically prohibited under Canadian law, but it must be implemented with appropriate compensating safeguards given the elevated risk profile. A documented remote access policy addressing these specific technical requirements is an important governance tool for any organization permitting this practice.
  112. The correct answer is A (Layering multiple independent safeguards, such as physical, technical.): Defence in depth refers to the deliberate layering of multiple, independent safeguards across the physical, technical, and administrative categories, so that if any single control fails, is bypassed, or is compromised, other layers of protection remain in place to reduce the overall risk of unauthorized access, use, or disclosure of sensitive information. This approach recognizes that no single safeguard, however strong, can realistically provide complete protection on its own against the full range of potential threats an organization may face. Relying exclusively on one measure, such as encryption alone with no other controls, would leave significant gaps if that single measure were ever defeated or misconfigured. This layered principle underlies well-designed health information security programs across physical facilities, technical systems, and organizational policy and training.
  113. The correct answer is C (An intrusion detection or account monitoring system configured to flag anomalous authentication activity.): An unusually high volume of failed login attempts within a short timeframe is a classic indicator that an intrusion detection or account monitoring system, configured to flag anomalous authentication activity such as a possible brute-force password guessing attempt, would be designed to detect and alert security staff to. This type of automated monitoring is an important detective and often preventive technical safeguard, since it can trigger a timely response, such as temporarily locking the account, before an attacker successfully gains unauthorized access. Records retention schedules, consent forms, and access response letters are entirely unrelated administrative and legal documents with no role in real-time security monitoring. Effective monitoring systems like this are an important complement to preventive controls such as strong password requirements and multi-factor authentication.
  114. The correct answer is B (An administrative safeguard, since it is an organizational policy governing the response to a security incident.): A written incident response plan is itself an administrative safeguard, representing a documented organizational policy and procedure that governs how staff should identify, contain, investigate, and respond to a suspected security incident, ensuring a consistent, timely, and well-coordinated response rather than an improvised reaction in the moment of a genuine crisis. While the plan may reference and coordinate the use of various technical tools, such as audit logs or account lockdown capabilities, the plan document itself falls into the administrative safeguard category alongside other organizational policies and training programs. Having a documented, regularly tested plan is widely recognized as an important element of mature security governance, since the quality and speed of an organization's response can meaningfully affect the ultimate severity and cost of an actual incident. Plans that are never reviewed, updated, or practiced provide substantially less real protective value than one that is actively maintained.
  115. The correct answer is C (It supports accountability and after-the-fact review by documenting a legitimate.): Recording the specific reason for each visitor's access, alongside their identity and the date and time, supports accountability and provides a documented, reviewable record of a legitimate purpose for each instance of physical entry into a sensitive records storage area, which can be valuable if an unauthorized access or missing record incident is later investigated. This administrative practice complements, rather than replaces, the underlying physical safeguard of keeping the area locked and access-controlled in the first place, since a sign-in log alone would not actually prevent unauthorized entry. The value of documenting a specific reason applies to any visitor accessing the sensitive area, not only those who happen to be non-employees, since even employees visiting outside their normal role should have their purpose documented. Thoughtful physical access logging is a practical, relatively low-cost safeguard that meaningfully strengthens overall accountability.
  116. The correct answer is D (Accreditation Canada operates independently but its status may be required by provincial funders.): Accreditation Canada is an independent, not-for-profit body rather than a government department, but provincial governments and funders may choose to require or reference accreditation status as a condition of funding or licensure, giving the accreditation process practical regulatory weight even though it is not itself a government agency. This independent-but-influential status is an important nuance for understanding how quality assurance functions in the Canadian system. It illustrates how non-governmental bodies can still shape system-level accountability.
  117. The correct answer is C (The Canadian Institute for Health Information, which standardizes and reports national health data.): CIHI develops and maintains national data standards, classification systems, and case-mix methodologies used to consistently classify hospital cases across provinces for funding and reporting purposes, making it the primary national reference point for this type of standardized definition work. While provincial ministries adapt and apply these standards locally, the underlying national classification framework originates from CIHI. This makes CIHI one of the most directly relevant national bodies to day-to-day CHIM practice.
  118. The correct answer is D (The Public Health Agency of Canada, which leads national public health surveillance.): PHAC is the federal agency mandated to lead national public health surveillance, coordinate outbreak data collection across provinces and territories, and issue national guidance during infectious disease emergencies, working collaboratively with provincial and territorial public health authorities who retain jurisdiction over local response. This coordinating role was a direct outcome of PHAC's creation following the 2003 SARS outbreak, which revealed gaps in national public health coordination. Recognizing PHAC's emergency coordination role is a key recall point.
  119. The correct answer is A (CIHI is an independent, not-for-profit organization funded jointly by federal, provincial, and territorial governments.): CIHI operates as an independent, not-for-profit organization governed by its own board, though it receives funding contributions from federal, provincial, and territorial governments in recognition of the shared national value of standardized health data. This independent governance structure allows CIHI to maintain data standards and reporting functions across jurisdictional boundaries. CIHI does not license or regulate individual health professionals, which is instead the role of provincial regulatory colleges.
  120. The correct answer is C (The Canadian Institute for Health Information, which standardizes and reports national health data.): CIHI compiles and reports national data on the health workforce, including counts and characteristics of regulated health professionals such as physicians, nurses, and other providers, supporting health human resources planning across Canada. This workforce data function is one of several distinct data domains CIHI maintains alongside hospital discharge and financial data. Understanding CIHI's broad data mandate helps CHIM professionals identify the correct source for various types of national health system information.
  121. The correct answer is B (Each body serves a specialized function within a complex, decentralized health system.): given Canada's decentralized, provincially delivered health system, distinct national bodies have developed to serve specialized functions, regulatory policy through Health Canada, public health emergency coordination through PHAC, data standardization through CIHI, and voluntary quality accreditation through Accreditation Canada, allowing each to develop deep expertise in its area while respecting provincial delivery jurisdiction. This specialization reflects practical governance needs rather than arbitrary duplication. Recognizing the functional rationale behind this structure supports a deeper understanding of system governance.
  122. The correct answer is C (Allocating provincial health care funding and setting provincial health policy.): provincial ministries of health hold the authority to allocate their own health care budgets and set provincial health policy, a governmental function CIHI does not perform since CIHI's mandate is centered on data collection, standardization, and public reporting rather than direct policy-making or funding allocation. CIHI instead supports evidence-informed decision-making by supplying the data that ministries and other stakeholders use. This distinction highlights the difference between a data agency and a governing authority.
  123. Accreditation Canada's reports focus on quality standard compliance rather than comparative statistical benchmarking, and PHAC and Health Canada's data holdings serve different purposes. Choosing the correct national data source for a given analytic question is a practical CHIM competency.
  124. This structure is intended to promote objectivity and credibility in data standards and quality assessment. Understanding this governance model helps explain why these bodies are trusted sources of standardized information across a decentralized system.
  125. This regulatory function is separate from PHAC's public health surveillance role and from CIHI's data reporting role. Recognizing Health Canada's regulatory scope over health products is a distinct recall point from its Canada Health Act administration role.
  126. This reporting function supports evidence-based system planning at both the provincial and national levels. CIHI's credibility depends on its arm's length, standardized approach to data collection and reporting.
  127. The correct answer is B (The 2003 SARS outbreak revealed gaps that led to a dedicated public health agency.): the severe acute respiratory syndrome outbreak in 2003 exposed significant weaknesses in Canada's national public health surveillance and emergency coordination capacity, leading directly to the creation of the Public Health Agency of Canada in 2004 as a distinct federal body dedicated to public health functions. Health Canada continued to exist alongside PHAC, retaining its broader regulatory mandate. This historical context is commonly referenced to explain why two separate federal health bodies exist.
  128. CIHI and PHAC occupy their own distinct roles, data reporting and public health coordination respectively, neither of which is accurately described as a voluntary accreditation body. Correctly categorizing these organizations' governance status is a useful exam skill.

This aggregated, comparative reporting is central to CIHI's mandate to support system-level decision-making. Individual patient records remain protected health information not published in this manner.

These standards are not tied to funding calculations or salary determination, which fall under separate provincial and funding-body processes. HIM departments are frequently directly responsible for meeting documentation-related accreditation standards.

This structure reflects the broader federal-provincial balance found throughout the Canadian health system.
  132. This makes CIHI the appropriate and most authoritative national resource for coding standards questions. Familiarity with CIHI's coding standards role is foundational to CHIM professional practice.

This distinction clarifies why CIHI's influence operates through data, evidence, and standards rather than direct command authority. Understanding this scope difference avoids a common misconception about CIHI's powers.
  134. An individual informal fix does not address the systemic policy gap for all records going forward. Waiting for an actual breach incident before acting is a reactive approach to a preventable and already identified risk.

Coding around the ambiguity or relying on unrelated precedent from other clinics both risk an inaccurate result that may not reflect this specific clinic's actual documentation intent. Seeking clarification protects both accuracy and the integrity of the coded data.

Broad managerial authority alone does not eliminate this risk. General employee consent to being monitored does not mean every possible use of that monitoring capability is automatically appropriate or unconcerning.
  141. The correct answer is D (Decline the instruction and maintain appropriate documentation consistent with standard, legitimate investigation practices.): Deliberately avoiding proper documentation specifically to evade a future legitimate records request undermines transparency and accountability, and an ethical professional should decline such an instruction and maintain documentation consistent with standard, legitimate investigation practices regardless of managerial preference. Following the instruction, even while secretly keeping a personal copy, still leaves the official record incomplete and misleading. Whether the investigation involves patient information does not change the underlying integrity concern with deliberately evading proper documentation.
  142. The correct answer is D (Recommit to coding.): Recognizing a potential unconscious influence from funding awareness is an important first step, and the appropriate response is to recommit to documentation based coding standards and use peer review as a check when self doubt arises, rather than assuming any awareness of funding context is harmless. A permanent transfer is an extreme response to a manageable professional discipline issue. Deliberately adjusting coding in the opposite direction introduces its own inaccuracy rather than restoring objectivity.
  143. The correct answer is C (Decline to give clinical advice and direct the patient to appropriate emergency or clinical resources.): Providing clinical advice falls outside the scope of practice of a HIM professional regardless of staffing pressures, and doing so risks patient safety and exceeds professional and legal boundaries. Reviewing a chart to offer an informal diagnosis compounds the scope violation with an inappropriate access to information. Directing the caller to proper clinical resources is the accountable response within her actual role.
  144. The correct answer is B (The staff member's careful verification practice reflects appropriate diligence rather than a performance problem.): Thorough identity verification before releasing personal health information is an appropriate professional practice, and a reasonable investigation should recognize that time spent on genuine diligence is not itself a performance failure, even if it results in a slower processing pace than colleagues. Instructing the staff member to sacrifice verification thoroughness for speed would create real privacy risk. The more appropriate response is likely to examine whether other staff members are being sufficiently careful, not to discourage this employee's diligence.
  145. The correct answer is A (Ownership generally follows the employer's intellectual property policy since the work was created during paid employment.): Work created during paid employment using organizational time and resources typically falls under the employer's intellectual property policy, and understanding this genuinely is an ethical as well as legal consideration relevant to professional conduct around ownership expectations. Assuming personal ownership simply because she wrote the material ignores standard employment work product principles. Dismissing the question as purely legal ignores that professionals should understand and respect these ownership expectations as part of workplace integrity.
  146. The correct answer is B (Whether the logging system captures sufficiently detailed, accurate, and retained access records to support an investigation.): The core ethical and security consideration for any physical access logging system is whether it captures sufficiently detailed, accurate, and adequately retained records to genuinely support an investigation when one becomes necessary, since a system that cannot reconstruct who accessed a secure area defeats its protective purpose. The system's age, original cost, or daily convenience are secondary operational factors that do not address this core investigative capability question.
  147. The correct answer is A (Query the surgeon to clarify which technique was actually performed before finalizing the procedure code.): A genuine discrepancy between clinical documents describing different surgical techniques should be resolved through a surgeon query rather than the coder guessing based on document order or administrative convenience, since the correct procedure code depends on what actually occurred. Coding based on entry order does not establish clinical accuracy. Coding both techniques as separate procedures when only one occurred would misrepresent the actual surgery performed.
  148. The correct answer is D (Decline the offer or disclose it fully to the committee before participating in any evaluation.): Accepting a significant benefit tied to a procurement decision creates a conflict of interest that can compromise, or appear to compromise, objective judgment, so ethical practice requires either declining the offer or fully disclosing it before participating in the evaluation. Attending quietly and hoping the outcome happens to align with the gift does not resolve the underlying conflict. Requesting confidentiality from her employer compounds the problem by concealing the conflict rather than disclosing it.
  149. The correct answer is C (Develop and communicate a clear coding policy consistent with official guidelines to standardize practice.): Coding must generally follow the documented clinical diagnosis rather than an independent interpretation of a lab value, and inconsistent practice across coders undermines data accuracy and integrity. A clear, guideline based policy resolves the ambiguity in a defensible, standardized way. Simply allowing continued inconsistency or adopting an extreme rule in either direction fails to address the underlying accuracy and standardization problem.
  150. The correct answer is A (Present the complete and accurate data.): Integrity in data reporting requires presenting a complete and accurate picture, even when some findings are less favourable to a desired outcome such as securing a grant. Selectively omitting or minimizing unfavourable data misrepresents the organization's actual performance to reviewers making funding decisions. Waiting to disclose only if specifically asked still constitutes a misleading initial presentation of the data.
  151. The correct answer is B (Retention periods should be based on legitimate audit, legal, and operational needs rather than indefinite storage.): Sound information governance requires setting retention periods based on genuine legal, audit, and operational needs, since indefinite retention of any records, including access approval documentation, can itself create unnecessary risk and administrative burden without a corresponding legitimate purpose. Defaulting to the longest possible period without an identified need does not reflect thoughtful governance. Basing the decision on unrelated policies ignores the specific purpose and requirements applicable to this particular type of documentation.
  152. The correct answer is C (Default values that go unchanged can result in inaccurate documentation that misrepresents the patient's actual condition.): Documentation accuracy is a core concern for HIM professionals, and default values that clinicians habitually leave unchanged can produce a health record that does not truly reflect the individual patient's presentation, affecting both clinical care and downstream coding accuracy. The mere technical ability to change a default does not address the practical reality that defaults are frequently left as is. Facilities share responsibility with vendors for how documentation tools are actually configured and used.
  153. The correct answer is D (Integrity, since falsifying documentation about work completion misrepresents the true state of the department's performance.): Being asked to document something false about deadline compliance is fundamentally a request to misrepresent facts, which squarely engages the professional's obligation of honesty and integrity in all documentation and reporting. Competence, confidentiality, and scope of practice are not the central issues raised by a request to falsify a completion record. The professional should decline the request and document the situation accurately.
  154. The correct answer is D (The lack of need to know based access controls increases the risk of unauthorized viewing.): Access should be limited to what an individual genuinely needs for their role, since overly broad access substantially increases the risk of curiosity based or otherwise unauthorized viewing of sensitive information. A signed confidentiality agreement does not substitute for actual technical controls limiting what can be accessed. This is a structural risk relevant to facilities of any size, not just large institutions.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Outside the uterus, such as on the ovaries or pelvic peritoneum.): endometriosis occurs when endometrial-like tissue implants and grows outside the uterine cavity, commonly on pelvic structures, causing pain and potential infertility. Option B describes the normal location of the endometrium, not the abnormal finding that defines this condition. Option C limits the location too narrowly to only one structure. Option D also limits the location incorrectly to only one structure rather than the broader pelvic distribution typical of this disease.
  2. The correct answer is D (Proteinuria, meaning protein detected in the urine on a routine laboratory panel.): preeclampsia is classically defined by new-onset hypertension after twenty weeks gestation together with proteinuria or evidence of other organ dysfunction. Option B describes a finding more associated with gestational diabetes, a different pregnancy complication. Option C describes a finding not typically part of the diagnostic criteria for preeclampsia. Option A describes a finding associated with diabetic ketoacidosis rather than preeclampsia.
  3.
