Option A describes a hormone important earlier in the cycle for follicle development, not the trigger for ovulation itself. Option B describes a hormone that rises after ovulation to support the uterine lining. Option D describes a hormone produced during follicle development that contributes to but does not itself trigger the surge.
  4. The correct answer is B (One or both testes, which remain within the abdomen or inguinal canal.): cryptorchidism describes the failure of one or both testes to descend into the scrotum during fetal development, a finding often identified on newborn examination. Option A describes the prostate, a structure that normally does not descend and is unrelated to this diagnosis. Option C describes the bladder, an unrelated structure. Option D describes a different congenital condition affecting penile development rather than testicular descent.
  5. The correct answer is D (Human papillomavirus, a common sexually transmitted virus linked to cervical dysplasia.): persistent infection with high-risk strains of human papillomavirus is strongly linked to the development of cervical dysplasia and cancer. Option A describes a virus associated with genital ulcers, not cervical dysplasia. Option C describes a virus primarily affecting the liver, unrelated to cervical pathology. Option B describes a virus primarily relevant to congenital infection, not cervical neoplasia.
  6. The correct answer is A (Hemoglobin, the iron-containing protein within red blood cells that transports oxygen.): this iron-containing protein within red blood cells binds oxygen in the lungs and releases it to tissues throughout the body. Option B describes a plasma protein important for maintaining oncotic pressure, not oxygen transport. Option C describes a clotting protein, unrelated to oxygen carriage. Option D describes an oxygen-binding protein found in muscle tissue, not circulating red blood cells.
  7. The correct answer is C (Hemoglobin, which requires iron to bind and carry oxygen.): iron is an essential component of the heme group within hemoglobin, so deficiency impairs hemoglobin synthesis and produces small, pale red blood cells. Option B describes a clotting protein whose formation depends on calcium, not iron. Option A describes a breakdown product of hemoglobin rather than a molecule requiring iron for its own synthesis. Option D describes a hormone that stimulates red cell production but is not itself iron dependent.
  8. The correct answer is A (Immunoglobulin E, bound to mast cells and basophils.): allergen binding to IgE already attached to mast cells and basophils triggers rapid release of histamine and other mediators, producing the systemic reaction seen in anaphylaxis. Option B describes an antibody important in long-term immunity but not the primary mediator of acute allergic reactions. Option C describes an antibody protecting mucosal surfaces, not driving anaphylaxis. Option D describes an antibody produced early in an immune response but not the one directly triggering mast cell degranulation.
  9. The correct answer is D (They filter lymph and house lymphocytes that mount immune responses.): lymph nodes trap pathogens and debris carried in lymph fluid while providing a site where lymphocytes can proliferate and respond to antigens. Option A describes a function of bone marrow, not lymph nodes. Option C describes a function of the gallbladder, unrelated to the lymphatic system. Option B describes a cardiovascular reflex mechanism, unrelated to lymph node function.
  10. The correct answer is C (Factor VIII, an essential component of the clotting cascade.): hemophilia A is an X-linked inherited deficiency of clotting factor VIII, impairing the intrinsic coagulation pathway and causing prolonged bleeding. Option B describes a nutrient needed for factor synthesis generally, but hemophilia A specifically involves a genetic factor deficiency, not a vitamin deficiency. Option A describes platelets, which are typically normal in number and function in hemophilia. Option D describes a different clotting protein not specifically deficient in hemophilia A.
  11. The correct answer is B (B lymphocytes undergoing malignant transformation within lymph node germinal centers.): Hodgkin lymphoma arises from malignant B lymphocytes, and the presence of characteristic Reed-Sternberg cells on biopsy is a defining diagnostic feature distinguishing it from non-Hodgkin lymphomas. Option A describes red blood cell precursors, unrelated to this lymphoid malignancy. Option C describes platelet-producing cells, unrelated to Hodgkin lymphoma. Option D describes a different white blood cell lineage not implicated in this particular malignancy.
  12. The correct answer is A (Neither the A antigen nor the B antigen present on the red cell surface.): type O blood lacks both the A and B surface antigens, making it compatible as a universal red blood cell donor in most transfusion scenarios. Option C describes type AB blood, which carries both antigens. Option B describes type A blood, carrying only the A antigen. Option D describes type B blood, carrying only the B antigen.
  13. The correct answer is D (The rate at which red blood cells settle to the bottom of a tube.): inflammation causes changes in plasma proteins that make red blood cells aggregate and settle faster, and this settling rate is used as a nonspecific marker of systemic inflammation. Option B describes a red blood cell count, a different laboratory test entirely. Option C describes mean corpuscular volume, a distinct red cell index. Option A describes hematocrit, another separate laboratory measurement.
  14. The correct answer is B (The retina, lining the back of the eye.): the retina contains photoreceptor cells that convert focused light into electrical signals transmitted to the brain via the optic nerve. Option A describes a transparent structure that helps focus light but does not itself sense it. Option C describes another focusing structure, not the light-sensitive layer. Option D describes the tough protective outer coat of the eyeball, not the sensory retina.
  15. The correct answer is C (The inner ear cochlea or the auditory nerve pathway.): sensorineural hearing loss results from damage to the cochlear hair cells or the auditory nerve itself, impairing the neural transmission of sound rather than its mechanical conduction. Option A describes a cause of conductive hearing loss, a distinct category from sensorineural loss. Option B also describes a mechanical, conductive cause rather than a sensorineural one. Option D describes another conductive cause involving the eardrum rather than the inner ear or nerve.
  16. The correct answer is D (Clouding or opacification of the normally clear lens.): a cataract develops when proteins within the lens clump together, causing progressive clouding that scatters light and blurs vision. Option A describes glaucoma, a distinct eye condition affecting the optic nerve. Option C describes macular degeneration, a separate retinal condition. Option B describes retinal detachment, an unrelated structural problem.
  17. The correct answer is A (The semicircular canals, fluid-filled inner ear structures detecting rotational head movement specifically.): these fluid-filled structures within the inner ear detect angular acceleration and rotational head movement, contributing to the sense of balance. Option B describes the hearing structure of the inner ear, not the balance-related structure. Option C describes a middle ear structure involved in sound transmission, not balance. Option D describes small bones in the middle ear that amplify sound, unrelated to equilibrium.
  18. The correct answer is B (Sustained elevated intraocular pressure compressing the optic nerve.): glaucoma involves impaired drainage of aqueous humor, raising intraocular pressure that gradually damages optic nerve fibers and can lead to irreversible vision loss. Option A describes an infectious process, not the mechanism underlying typical glaucoma. Option C describes an autoimmune corneal process unrelated to glaucoma. Option D describes cataract formation, a separate condition from glaucoma.
  19. The correct answer is C (To obtain clarification on ambiguous, incomplete, or conflicting documentation needed to assign an accurate code.): A physician query exists to resolve genuine documentation gaps, such as an unclear diagnosis, conflicting notes, or a clinical finding that lacks an explicit diagnostic label, so the coder can assign a code that is both accurate and supported by the record. Queries must be clinically grounded and non-leading, seeking clarification rather than directing the physician toward a particular answer. This process protects both coding accuracy and compliance, since codes assigned without adequate documentation support are vulnerable to audit findings. The other options either misstate the purpose as revenue-driven or administrative, which is inconsistent with recognized query standards.
  20. The correct answer is C (Query the physician to confirm whether the clinical finding represents a diagnosis to be documented.): A laboratory value alone, such as an elevated creatinine consistent with acute renal failure, cannot be coded as a diagnosis unless a physician or other qualified practitioner has documented that finding as a clinical diagnosis. Coding directly from a lab result without physician confirmation risks assigning a diagnosis that was never clinically established, which is both a compliance and accuracy problem. The appropriate response is a compliant, non-leading query asking the physician to confirm or clarify whether the abnormal finding represents a diagnosis for this admission. Ignoring the finding or assuming it is insignificant would fail to capture a potentially reportable condition if the physician does confirm it.
  21. The correct answer is A (A query that suggests a specific diagnosis primarily to increase reimbursement rather than clarify genuine ambiguity.): A leading, non-compliant query is one that steers the physician toward a specific diagnosis, particularly one that would increase the resource intensity weight or reimbursement, rather than neutrally presenting clinical indicators and asking for clarification. This crosses from legitimate clarification into directing the outcome, which compromises the integrity of the coded data and creates real audit risk. A compliant query, by contrast, presents objective clinical findings such as lab values or vital signs and asks the physician to confirm or clarify, exactly as described in options B and D. Retaining the query in the permanent record, as in option C, describes good documentation practice, not a leading query.
  22. The correct answer is A (It provides an auditable trail showing coded diagnoses are supported by clarified physician documentation.): Retaining physician query responses in the permanent health record creates a clear, auditable link between the coder's clarification request and the physician's confirmation, demonstrating that a coded diagnosis is grounded in documented clinical judgment rather than coder inference. This trail is essential during external CIHI data quality reviews or funding audits, where reviewers need to see the basis for a coded condition. There is no provincial requirement to destroy queries within thirty days, and treating queries as having no bearing on accuracy or as optional to retain contradicts standard coding compliance practice.
  23. The correct answer is C (Targeted education for the affected coders along with reinforced query practices for hospital-acquired conditions.): When an audit finds a systemic pattern of missed type 2 diagnoses for hospital-acquired infections across several coders, the appropriate first step is a corrective, educational response rather than punitive action, since the pattern points to a training or process gap rather than individual misconduct. Reinforcing query practices ensures coders proactively seek clarification when documentation of a hospital-acquired condition is ambiguous or incomplete. Immediate termination is disproportionate before root cause is understood, and discontinuing audits or removing hospital-acquired infection tracking would eliminate the very mechanism that caught the problem.
  24. The correct answer is C (A concurrent review occurs during the stay or before submission, while a retrospective review occurs after.): Concurrent coding quality review takes place while the patient is still admitted or before the abstract is finalized and submitted to CIHI, allowing errors to be caught and corrected before the data becomes part of the permanent submitted record. Retrospective review happens after submission, typically as part of a broader audit or quality improvement cycle, and can identify systemic patterns across many already-submitted abstracts. Both review types serve quality assurance but differ fundamentally in timing relative to abstract submission. The review type is not defined by whether it is DAD or NACRS, nor is it typically conducted by an external government agency as a matter of course.
  25. The correct answer is A (It helps identify inconsistencies in how coders apply sequencing rules, supporting more standardized abstracting.): Inter-rater reliability measures the degree to which different coders, reviewing the same chart, independently select the same most responsible diagnosis and apply the same sequencing logic. Low inter-rater reliability signals that coders are interpreting CIHI sequencing rules or diagnosis typing conventions inconsistently, which directly undermines the comparability and accuracy of the case mix data submitted nationally. Tracking this metric lets a department target education toward the specific rules or scenarios generating disagreement. It has nothing to do with typing speed or bonus rankings, which are unrelated administrative metrics.
  26. The correct answer is C (A potential compliance concern, since diagnosis codes generally require physician or qualified practitioner confirmation.): Coding a diagnosis based solely on a nursing note, without any physician or qualified practitioner documentation or a query confirming the condition, raises a genuine compliance concern because CIHI standards generally require that a diagnosis be established by a physician or another authorized practitioner before it is coded. Nursing documentation can support clinical context but does not, on its own, substitute for a physician-confirmed diagnosis in most circumstances. This gap exposes the abstract to being flagged in an external audit as unsupported. The concern applies broadly across DAD abstracting and is not limited to whether the diagnosis happens to be the most responsible diagnosis.
  27. The correct answer is D (To ensure queries are compliant, consistently documented, and defensible during external audits.): A formal coding query policy standardizes how queries are worded, submitted, and retained, ensuring every query is clinically grounded, non-leading, and properly documented as part of the permanent record. This consistency protects the hospital during external CIHI or provincial data quality audits, since a formal process demonstrates that clarifications were obtained through a defensible, compliant method rather than ad hoc conversations that leave no trail. Informal queries create real compliance risk because they are difficult to verify after the fact and may inadvertently lead the physician. Formal policies do not reduce the need for documentation training, they reinforce it.
  28. The correct answer is C (Review the record against CIHI sequencing guidelines and, if unclear, query the physician for clarification.): When the coded most responsible diagnosis and the physician's stated primary reason for admission in the discharge summary appear to conflict, the coder's first step is to re-examine the full record against CIHI's sequencing and MRDx selection guidelines, since coding decisions are based on the entire clinical picture, not any single note in isolation. If the discrepancy remains unresolved after that review, a compliant, non-leading query to the physician is the appropriate next step to clarify intent. Automatically changing the code to match the discharge summary wording, or assuming coder judgment always overrides physician documentation, both bypass the structured review and query process that CIHI standards require.
  29. The correct answer is D (Ambiguous or incomplete physician documentation that does not clearly establish clinical significance or timing.): Coding audits repeatedly find that undercoding of comorbidities and complications traces back to documentation that fails to clearly state whether a condition was present on admission, developed during the stay, or was clinically significant enough to meet CIHI's reporting threshold. Without clear documentation of timing and significance, coders may be unable to confidently assign or type a diagnosis, leading to conservative undercoding rather than risking an unsupported code. This is why strengthening physician documentation and coder query practices is the standard corrective response to this exact audit finding. The other options describe implausible causes unrelated to documentation quality.
  30. The correct answer is C (Comparing internal audit accuracy rates against national or provincial data quality standards.): Benchmarking coding accuracy meaningfully requires comparing a department's own internal audit results, such as error rates or inter-rater reliability, against established national or provincial data quality standards published by CIHI or the relevant jurisdiction. This gives HIM leadership an objective reference point for how the department's coding performance compares to similar facilities across the system. Metrics unrelated to coding output, such as cafeteria revenue or parking capacity, have no bearing on coding accuracy and are not recognized benchmarking methods in health information management.
  31. The correct answer is D (Generally no, since the response should clearly and specifically confirm the clinical condition being queried.): A vague physician response such as a single word of agreement, with no explicit confirmation of the specific condition being queried, does not provide adequate documentation to support assigning the queried diagnosis code. CIHI and recognized coding query standards expect the physician's response to clearly and specifically address the clinical question raised, so the coder has a defensible basis for the code. Accepting an unclear response as sufficient creates the same compliance risk as coding directly from an unconfirmed clinical finding. Whether the original query was verbal or written does not change this requirement for a specific, clear response.
  32. The correct answer is B (It is clinically relevant, non-leading, and based on findings actually documented in the health record.): A compliant physician query must be grounded in documentation that is actually present in the chart, phrased neutrally so it does not suggest a specific answer, and focused on resolving genuine clinical ambiguity relevant to the diagnosis in question. Referencing real clinical indicators from the record, as in the correct answer, is what distinguishes a defensible query from a leading one that steers the physician toward a particular, often higher-weighted, diagnosis. Submitting queries anonymously or deliberately avoiding reference to specific documentation would actually weaken the query's clinical basis and defensibility, not strengthen it.
  33. The correct answer is C (It provides continuous feedback that helps identify and correct errors before they accumulate.): An ongoing monthly peer review process gives coders regular, individualized feedback on their abstracts, allowing recurring errors, such as misapplied sequencing rules or missed comorbidities, to be caught and corrected early rather than persisting across hundreds of future charts. This continuous quality loop is more effective than a one-time or infrequent audit because it reinforces correct practice while patterns are still small and easy to address through targeted coaching. It does not eliminate the value of external CIHI data quality studies, nor does it reduce the importance of coders pursuing further certification, which are separate, complementary quality mechanisms.
  34. The correct answer is B (Code the acute exacerbation of COPD as MRDx.): The most responsible diagnosis is the condition determined, after study, to be chiefly responsible for the patient's admission and the resources used during the stay, and here that is clearly the acute exacerbation of COPD, confirmed by the respiratory acidosis on arterial blood gases and treated with bronchodilators and non-invasive ventilation. Coding only the underlying chronic bronchitis would fail to capture the acute event that actually drove this admission and its resource use. Respiratory failure and the blood gas result itself are supporting clinical findings, not appropriate substitutes for the confirmed acute exacerbation diagnosis that CMG+ grouping depends on.
  35. The correct answer is A (As a type 1 pre-admit comorbidity, according to national coding guidance.): Well-controlled asthma that exists before admission and requires no additional intervention beyond the patient's usual home medication is coded as a type 1, pre-admit comorbidity, since it was present on admission but is not the focus of active new management during this stay. This typing correctly reflects that the condition contributes background clinical context without driving the resources consumed for the pneumonia admission. It would not be coded as a type 2 diagnosis, which is reserved for conditions that develop or are actively newly managed during the episode, nor should it be omitted entirely, since documented pre-existing comorbidities are still reportable.
  36. The correct answer is B (The organism should be coded in addition to the pneumonia when documentation supports it.): When a causative organism such as Streptococcus pneumoniae is confirmed by culture and documented by the physician, ICD-10-CA convention supports assigning both the pneumonia diagnosis code and a separate code identifying the organism, since this level of specificity provides a more complete clinical picture and supports appropriate case mix grouping. Omitting the organism code when the documentation clearly supports it understates the specificity of the abstract. The organism is never coded in place of the pneumonia diagnosis, and this convention applies equally whether the record is a DAD or NACRS abstract, so restricting it to one abstract type is incorrect.
  37. The correct answer is C (As a type 2 diagnosis, since it is a complication that developed during this admission.): Ventilator-associated pneumonia that develops five days into an admission for an unrelated condition, such as major trauma, meets the definition of a type 2 diagnosis because it arose after admission and required additional clinical attention and resources during the same episode of care. This typing is essential because it flags the pneumonia as a complication of care rather than a pre-existing condition, which matters both for CMG+ resource weighting and for hospital-acquired infection tracking. It would not replace the original trauma diagnosis as MRDx, since trauma remains the reason the patient was admitted, and the fact that a device was involved does not exempt the condition from being coded.
  38. The correct answer is A (The acute hypercapnic respiratory failure, the condition established as driving treatment and resource use.): MRDx selection depends on identifying the condition that, after full clinical study, is determined to be chiefly responsible for the admission and the resources consumed, and here the acute hypercapnic respiratory failure requiring ongoing high-flow oxygen therapy throughout the stay clearly drove treatment intensity. The underlying emphysema and chronic hypoxemia are important background comorbidities but represent the patient's baseline chronic state rather than the acute event prompting this specific admission. MRDx is never determined by which condition happens to appear first in an early triage note, since that reflects initial impression rather than the confirmed clinical picture established through the full course of the stay.
  39. The correct answer is D (It is generally not coded, since it has no bearing on this episode and needs no treatment during this admission.): A chronic condition that is well controlled, requires no treatment during the stay, and has no clinical relevance to the reason for admission generally falls below the threshold CIHI sets for reportability, since coding standards require a condition to have some bearing on the current episode of care, such as affecting length of stay, treatment, or nursing care. Seasonal allergic rhinitis with no active symptoms or intervention during an unrelated orthopedic admission does not meet that threshold. It would not be coded as MRDx or as a comorbidity requiring active treatment, since neither accurately describes a condition with zero clinical impact on this stay.
  40. The correct answer is D (Code both the myocardial infarction and the coronary intervention procedure, since both reflect the episode.): An admission for acute ST-elevation myocardial infarction treated with emergency percutaneous coronary intervention and stent placement requires both a diagnosis code for the infarction and a separate CCI intervention code for the procedure performed, since CIHI abstracting captures diagnoses and interventions as distinct, complementary data elements. Coding only one or the other would leave an incomplete picture of the episode, understating either the clinical severity or the resource-intensive intervention actually delivered. Procedure codes are not optional for cardiac cases, and combining diagnosis and intervention into one undifferentiated code is inconsistent with how ICD-10-CA and CCI are structured as separate classification systems.
  41. The correct answer is D (As a type 1 pre-admit comorbidity that is documented but not the focus of active management.): Long-standing hypertension that is well controlled and requires no medication adjustment during an elective admission for an unrelated procedure, such as hip replacement, is typed as a pre-admit comorbidity, reflecting that the condition existed before admission and remained stable without new intervention. This typing acknowledges the condition's presence without overstating its clinical impact on this particular stay. It would not be coded as MRDx, since the hip replacement was the actual reason for admission, and simple ongoing monitoring of blood pressure does not meet the threshold for type 2 diagnosis, which requires active new management or a condition that develops during the stay.
  42. The correct answer is B (As a type 2 diagnosis or significant comorbidity if actively managed and affecting care this stay.): Atrial fibrillation that is being actively managed with rate control medication adjustments during an admission for congestive heart failure exacerbation meets the criteria for a significant comorbidity, or type 2 diagnosis if it newly developed, because active management during the stay demonstrates it genuinely affected the care and resources used for this episode. This is distinct from a condition simply documented in the history with no active intervention, which would instead be typed as a pre-admit comorbidity. It would not supersede the heart failure exacerbation as MRDx, since heart failure remains the condition chiefly responsible for the admission, and downgrading a clearly managed cardiac diagnosis to a symptom code would understate its clinical significance.
  43. The correct answer is C (Code unstable angina as MRDx along with the artery disease findings and this stay's angiography.): Unstable angina, the condition that prompted this admission and directed the diagnostic workup, is appropriately selected as MRDx, with the confirmed multivessel coronary artery disease findings coded as significant comorbidities and the diagnostic coronary angiography performed during this stay coded as the intervention actually carried out. The future bypass surgery cannot be coded on this abstract because it was not performed during this admission, only planned. A nonspecific chest pain symptom code would understate the confirmed diagnoses already established by angiography, and omitting the angiography procedure code is incorrect since diagnostic procedures performed during the stay are still reportable interventions.
  44. The correct answer is D (As a significant comorbidity, typed as pre-admit but actively managed and affecting resource use.): Chronic anemia that required an active intervention such as a blood transfusion during the stay to support cardiac function demonstrates that the pre-existing condition genuinely affected the resources used during this admission for decompensated heart failure, making it a reportable significant comorbidity even though its pre-admit typing reflects that it existed before the stay began. Anemia confirmed by lab values and actively treated is a legitimate diagnosis, not merely a laboratory finding to be disregarded. It would not replace heart failure as the most responsible diagnosis, since heart failure remains the reason for this admission, and it should not be withheld from coding simply because the transfusion, rather than a new onset, prompted the intervention.
  45. The correct answer is A (Code acute appendicitis as MRDx along with the appendectomy intervention code.): An uncomplicated admission for acute appendicitis treated with emergency appendectomy is coded with the confirmed diagnosis of acute appendicitis as MRDx, since this is the condition that drove the admission and the surgery, along with a separate CCI code capturing the appendectomy intervention performed. Coding only the procedure without a diagnosis, or substituting a nonspecific abdominal pain symptom code when a definitive diagnosis has already been confirmed surgically and pathologically, would both understate the clinical picture. Appendicitis is coded as the diagnosis driving the admission, not downgraded to a type 2 diagnosis simply because surgery was required, since surgery is the expected treatment for this condition rather than a complication.
  46. The correct answer is B (Code diverticular disease with hemorrhage using the applicable combination code supported by findings.): When colonoscopy confirms diverticulosis with active bleeding from a diverticulum in a patient admitted for lower gastrointestinal bleed, ICD-10-CA provides a combination code capturing diverticular disease with hemorrhage, which should be used because it most precisely reflects the confirmed clinical and endoscopic findings. Coding only a nonspecific gastrointestinal bleeding code, or diverticulosis alone without reference to the bleeding, would each omit part of the confirmed clinical picture that colonoscopy specifically established. The diagnosis code is still required in addition to any procedure code for the colonoscopy itself, since diagnosis and intervention codes serve distinct purposes on the abstract.
  47. The correct answer is D (As a type 1 pre-admit comorbidity, documented but not the focus of active treatment.): Chronic, stable gastroesophageal reflux disease that requires no change in management during an admission for an unrelated elective bariatric procedure is typed as a pre-admit comorbidity, since it was present before the admission and did not require new or active intervention during this stay. This typing captures the condition's presence in the patient's clinical history without implying it affected the resources used for the bariatric surgery. It would not be coded as MRDx, since the bariatric procedure was the actual reason for admission, and it should not be omitted, since documented pre-existing comorbidities relevant to the patient's overall clinical picture are still reportable.
  48. The correct answer is C (Code acute pancreatitis as MRDx and alcohol dependence as a significant comorbidity actively managed.): Acute pancreatitis is the condition that prompted this admission and is therefore coded as MRDx, while the alcohol dependence, documented as actively addressed through counselling during the stay, meets the criteria for a significant comorbidity because it was genuinely managed as part of this episode of care rather than simply noted in the history. Both conditions are reportable and should be coded separately, since combining them into a single undifferentiated code would obscure the distinct clinical picture the documentation actually supports. ICD-10-CA does include codes for substance use disorders, so omitting alcohol dependence entirely would understate a clinically significant, actively treated condition.
  49. The correct answer is D (Code the fracture as MRDx, with an external cause code for the fall added as supplementary.): A closed femoral neck fracture requiring surgical fixation after a fall is coded with the fracture itself as MRDx, since the fracture is the condition that actually required admission and treatment, while the fall is captured through a supplementary external cause code that documents how the injury occurred. This sequencing reflects the standard convention that the injury diagnosis, not the mechanism of injury, is sequenced as the reason for admission. Coding only the external cause and omitting the fracture, or combining both into one undifferentiated code, would fail to properly identify the actual condition treated and the specific intervention performed.
  50. The correct answer is A (Descriptive statistics summarize and describe the characteristics of a dataset itself, while inferential statistics use sample data to draw conclusions about a larger population.): Descriptive statistics, such as the mean, median, mode, and standard deviation, are used to summarize and describe the characteristics of the specific dataset being analyzed, while inferential statistics use data collected from a sample to draw broader conclusions, estimates, or predictions about a larger population from which that sample was drawn. Both types of statistics serve important but distinct roles in health information analysis and research.
  51. The correct answer is C (A duplication or unique identification error.): This scenario describes a duplication and unique identification problem, since the same real-world patient has been fragmented across multiple separate records due to inconsistent name spelling, which can seriously distort statistics such as prevalence or readmission rates by causing one individual's data to be counted as though it belonged to multiple people. Master patient index management and rigorous identity-matching processes exist specifically to prevent and correct this type of data quality issue.
  52. The correct answer is D (An incorrectly defined denominator will produce a rate or proportion that does not accurately reflect the true frequency of the event being measured.): The denominator used in any rate or proportion must correctly represent the population genuinely capable of experiencing the event being measured; if that denominator is defined incorrectly, whether too broad or too narrow, the resulting statistic will misrepresent the true frequency of the event, regardless of how accurate the numerator count itself may be. This principle applies broadly across every type of rate and proportion used in health information analysis, not only maternal or infant statistics.
  53. The correct answer is A (Whether the data collected actually serve a meaningful purpose for the intended user or decision.): Relevancy asks whether a given data element genuinely serves a useful purpose for the decision or report it was collected to support, since collecting data simply because it is available does not guarantee that data will be meaningful or actionable. A health information system that captures large volumes of irrelevant data can obscure the genuinely important elements and add unnecessary burden to data collection and storage.
  54. The correct answer is D (Master patient index management.): Master patient index management uses standardized patient identifiers and identity verification procedures at the point of registration to ensure that each unique individual is matched to a single medical record number, preventing the fragmentation of one patient's health information across multiple duplicate records. This process is essential to maintaining data integrity across every downstream statistic that relies on accurate patient identification.
  55. The correct answer is B (Even a correctly calculated rate can be misleading if the two facilities used different definitions for what counts in the numerator or denominator.): Two facilities can each correctly calculate a rate using their own internal definitions, yet still produce numbers that are not truly comparable if one facility defines the numerator or denominator differently than the other, such as including or excluding certain patient types. Reviewing both definitions before comparing rates is a basic safeguard against drawing an invalid conclusion from what appears to be a straightforward numeric comparison.
  56. The correct answer is A (A structure measure evaluates the resources and systems in place, while a process measure evaluates the actions or steps taken during care delivery.): Structure measures evaluate the resources, staffing levels, equipment, and systems an organization has in place to deliver care, while process measures evaluate the specific actions, steps, or interventions carried out during the delivery of that care, such as whether a recommended screening was performed. Outcome measures form a third category, evaluating the actual results of care, such as recovery or mortality, and together these three measure types form the foundation of the Donabedian quality framework.
  57. The correct answer is C (A process measure.): Tracking whether a specific recommended action, such as administering a medication within an appropriate timeframe, was actually carried out is a process measure, since it evaluates adherence to a defined step in the care delivery pathway rather than the resources available or the ultimate clinical result. Process measures are often chosen because they are more directly and quickly actionable than outcome measures, which can be influenced by many factors beyond the specific process being studied.
  58. The correct answer is A (A measure of the actual result of care, such as mortality, complication rate, or functional recovery.): An outcome measure evaluates the actual end result of the care that was delivered, such as mortality rate, complication rate, or a patient's functional status after treatment, making it the measure most directly tied to what ultimately matters to the patient. Outcome measures can be harder to interpret than process measures, however, because they are influenced by many factors beyond the quality of care alone, including patient severity and comorbidities.
  59. The correct answer is D (It provides a structured, iterative framework for testing a small-scale change, evaluating its effect, and deciding whether to adopt, adapt, or abandon it.): The Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle is a structured, iterative improvement framework in which a team plans a small-scale change, implements it, studies the results against the original goal, and then acts on what was learned by adopting, adapting, or abandoning the change before scaling it further. This iterative small-scale testing approach allows a quality improvement team to learn quickly with lower risk than implementing a large-scale change organization-wide from the outset.
  60. The correct answer is D (Analyze what was learned and revise or redesign the change before testing it again in another cycle.): The Plan-Do-Study-Act cycle is inherently iterative, meaning a disappointing result from one cycle is expected to inform a revised approach that is then tested again in a subsequent cycle, rather than being treated as a final failure or, conversely, being scaled up despite not working. This iterative learning process is what allows quality improvement teams to refine an intervention until it reliably produces the intended result.
  61. The correct answer is A (A third variable that is related to both variables being studied and can distort the apparent relationship between them.): A confounding variable is a third factor that is associated with both the exposure and the outcome being studied, and if it is not properly accounted for, it can create the false appearance of a relationship between the exposure and outcome, or mask a true relationship that actually exists. Identifying and controlling for confounding variables, whether through study design or statistical adjustment, is essential to drawing a valid conclusion from observational research.
  62. The correct answer is D (A confounding variable, warmer temperature, independently increases both ice cream sales and swimming activity.): This is a classic example of confounding, where a third factor, warmer weather, independently drives both increased ice cream sales and increased swimming activity, and the increased swimming activity in turn drives up the actual risk of drowning, creating a statistical association between ice cream sales and drowning that has no direct causal relationship at all. This illustrates why correlation observed in data does not by itself establish causation.
  63. The correct answer is A (Correlation indicates that two variables tend to change together, while causation means that a change in one variable directly produces a change in the other.): Correlation describes a statistical association in which two variables tend to change together, but this alone does not prove that one variable is directly responsible for producing the change in the other, since confounding variables, reverse causation, or pure coincidence can also produce an observed correlation. Establishing true causation typically requires a more rigorous study design, such as a randomized controlled trial, that can rule out these alternative explanations.
  64. The correct answer is A (Standard deviation.): Standard deviation is calculated by finding each value's deviation from the mean, squaring those deviations to eliminate negative values, averaging the squared deviations, and then taking the square root of that average, which returns the measure of spread to the original units of the data. This calculation makes standard deviation more sensitive to the full distribution of values than the range, which considers only the two most extreme values.
  65. The correct answer is D (Standard deviation.): Standard deviation directly measures how tightly or loosely individual data values cluster around the mean, so comparing the standard deviation of two datasets with an identical mean reveals which dataset has less variability, and therefore more consistent values, and which has more spread-out, variable values. Mode and sample size do not measure dispersion around a central value and would not answer this particular question.
  66. The correct answer is B (A pie chart.): A pie chart is designed to show how individual parts contribute proportionally to a single combined whole at one point in time, which makes it poorly suited to displaying a continuous trend across 24 separate months, since a pie chart has no natural way to represent the passage of time or sequential change. A line graph, run chart, or chronologically ordered bar chart are all far better suited to displaying this type of month-by-month trend.
  67. The correct answer is A (It ranks categories of a problem from most to least frequent, combined with a cumulative percentage line, to help identify which few categories account for most of the problem.): A Pareto chart combines a descending bar chart of problem categories, ranked from most to least frequent, with a cumulative percentage line overlaid on the same graph, allowing a quality improvement team to quickly identify the small number of categories responsible for the majority of a problem, consistent with the Pareto principle that a large share of effects often come from a small share of causes. This makes it a useful tool for prioritizing which issues to address first with limited improvement resources.
  68. The correct answer is D (Prioritize improvement efforts on those two categories first, since addressing them offers the greatest potential impact.): The core principle behind using a Pareto chart is to focus limited quality improvement resources on the small number of categories responsible for the largest share of a problem, so identifying that two categories account for the majority of discharge delays should lead the team to prioritize those two areas first, since addressing them offers the greatest potential improvement for the effort invested. This targeted approach is generally more effective than spreading improvement efforts evenly across every possible cause regardless of its actual contribution to the problem.
  69. The correct answer is A (A visual tool that organizes potential causes of a problem into major categories branching off a central problem statement.): A cause-and-effect diagram organizes potential contributing causes of a defined problem into major branching categories, such as people, process, equipment, and environment, which helps a quality improvement team systematically brainstorm and visualize all the possible factors that could be contributing to an identified problem before selecting which causes to investigate further. This tool is typically used earlier in a quality improvement project, often before data collection tools such as a Pareto chart are applied.
  70. The correct answer is A (The number of days used in the denominator must match the actual number of days in the specific month being analyzed.): Average daily census is calculated by summing each day's census count and dividing by the actual number of days in the specific reporting period being analyzed, so using 30 days for April is correct since April genuinely has 30 days, while using an incorrect day count for any other month would distort the resulting average. This is a common source of calculation error when analysts do not adjust the denominator for months of differing lengths.
  71. The correct answer is C (Small numbers of events can produce large swings in a calculated rate from one period to the next, even without any true underlying change in risk.): When the number of events being counted is small, such as only 3 infections in a month, even a difference of one additional or one fewer case can produce a large percentage swing in the calculated rate from one period to the next, which can make random, ordinary variation look like a meaningful trend if interpreted without caution. Analysts often need to look at trends over a longer period, or apply statistical methods appropriate for small numbers, to avoid over-interpreting this kind of natural fluctuation.
  72. The correct answer is A (Inadequate attention to workflow redesign, training, and staff adoption alongside the technical rollout.): A system can be technically sound and still fail to deliver its intended benefits if the organization neglects the human side of implementation, meaning redesigning workflows to fit the new tool, training staff adequately, and actively supporting adoption. Benefits realization depends on people actually using the system effectively in their daily work, not just on the software functioning correctly. Options B, C, and D each point to trivial or irrelevant technical details, such as programming language, icon colour, or server count, none of which meaningfully explain why an implementation fails to achieve its intended benefits.

This targeted approach increases relevance and buy-in across the organization. Option A describes an untailored, generic approach that undermines engagement, and options B and D describe limiting or delaying communication, both of which would leave frontline staff uninformed and more resistant to the change.
  74. This hands-on support directly reduces frustration and helps establish good habits with the new workflow from the start. Option A is wrong because support is needed most, not ended, right at go-live, and options B and C incorrectly limit the plan to testing or hardware with no staff support component, which misses the purpose of a go-live support plan entirely.
  75. The correct answer is C (User acceptance testing allows actual end users to verify that the system supports their real workflows.): User acceptance testing is conducted specifically so that the people who will actually use the system day to day can confirm it functions correctly for their real tasks and workflows before the organization commits to a full launch, catching usability or functional gaps that technical testing alone might miss. This step helps ensure the system will genuinely work in practice, not just in theory. Option A wrongly dismisses it as a mere legal formality, option B incorrectly claims it replaces training, and option D is inaccurate because meaningful user acceptance testing requires actual clinical staff involvement, not the vendor alone.
  76. The correct answer is D (Determining how a nurse will document a medication administration within the new electronic system compared to the paper process.): Workflow redesign focuses on how clinical and administrative tasks will actually be performed differently using the new system, such as how a nurse documents a medication administration compared to the prior paper or legacy electronic process, which directly affects daily practice and patient care. This is fundamentally different from purely technical infrastructure decisions. Options A, B, and C all describe physical or technical infrastructure considerations, such as server room size, cabling, or electrical voltage, which relate to the technical build rather than to how staff will actually use the system in their clinical work.
  77. The correct answer is D (A learning curve is common as staff adjust to new workflows and become fully proficient with the new system.): A temporary dip in productivity right after go-live is a well-recognized and expected part of implementation, even when staff have received prior training, because applying new knowledge under real workload pressure takes practice, and speed and comfort typically improve over the following days to weeks as staff gain hands-on experience. This expected learning curve should be planned for, not treated as a sign of failure. Option A wrongly concludes training should be reduced, option B jumps to abandoning a system based on a normal and temporary dip, and option C incorrectly denies any connection between the productivity drop and the recent system change.
  78. This peer-level advocacy is a key driver of grassroots adoption. Option A incorrectly describes an external consultant with no ongoing staff relationship, and options C and D wrongly limit the role to budget approval or technical specification writing, neither of which reflects the champion's actual frontline adoption-support function.
  79. The correct answer is B (A desire to identify and resolve issues in a smaller, more manageable scope.): A phased rollout across departments allows the organization to identify technical glitches, workflow problems, and training gaps within a limited scope first, then apply those lessons learned before expanding to additional units, which reduces the risk and disruption compared to a single all-at-once, organization-wide go-live. This staged risk management is the primary advantage that favours a phased approach.   80. This structured follow-up ensures the implementation continues to improve rather than being considered finished the moment the system launches. Option A incorrectly treats go-live as the endpoint of evaluation, and options B and D describe ignoring actual usage data or discarding staff feedback, both of which defeat the purpose of a meaningful post-implementation review.
  81. This early input can also prevent costly design mistakes that would only surface after go-live. Option A understates the well-documented value of engagement, option B wrongly excludes clinical staff from a process that directly affects their workflows, and option D incorrectly frames engagement as merely a delay rather than an investment that improves outcomes.
  82. These are the categories of risk that genuinely determine whether an implementation succeeds.   84. This reduces the risk of discovering a serious problem only after the legacy system is already gone. Option B incorrectly frames this as a permanent, indefinite arrangement rather than a temporary verification period, and options C and D wrongly claim the parallel run eliminates the need for training or data migration, when in fact both are still required.
  86. Technology alone cannot guarantee successful outcomes without the human adoption piece. Option A understates the well-established impact of change management on implementation success, option B incorrectly claims the technical build alone is sufficient regardless of staff attitudes, and option D wrongly limits change management's relevance to only certain types of systems.

This before-and-after measurement approach provides a defensible basis for the evaluation. Option A relies on a single, limited anecdotal opinion, option C measures an input (equipment purchased) rather than an outcome, and option D ignores any post-implementation data, which is essential to a genuine evaluation of whether goals were met.
  88. The correct answer is A (A staff member given advanced training on the new system.): A super user is a frontline staff member who receives more advanced, in-depth training on the new system beyond what general end users receive, so they can serve as an accessible, on-the-floor resource who helps colleagues troubleshoot problems and reinforces correct use of the system during and after go-live. This peer-level expert support is a key practical component of a successful implementation.   89. The correct answer is B (A federally funded, independent organization that works with provinces and territories to advance and coordinate digital health initiatives.): Canada Health Infoway is a federally funded but independent, not-for-profit organization that works collaboratively with provincial and territorial governments to advance and coordinate digital health initiatives across the country, rather than delivering healthcare or holding direct constitutional authority over it. The correct answer is A (Constitutional responsibility for healthcare delivery rests with the provinces and territories.): Under the Canadian constitutional structure, the delivery and administration of healthcare services is a provincial and territorial responsibility, which is why Canada Health Infoway operates as a collaborative funding and coordinating partner working with those jurisdictions rather than delivering care itself or overriding provincial authority. This structural division explains why Infoway's role is supportive and advisory rather than directive. This coordinating function is precisely why a national body remains valuable within a decentralized healthcare system.
