  94. The correct answer is B (To proactively identify and address privacy and security risks associated with a new system or process.): A privacy impact assessment is a structured, proactive process used to identify and evaluate the privacy and security risks associated with a new system, process, or significant change involving personal health information, and to identify appropriate mitigation measures before the system is actually implemented and begins processing real patient data. This forward-looking approach is considered a best practice, and in some provinces may be a specific regulatory expectation, precisely because it is generally far more effective and less costly to address risks at the design stage than to remediate problems after a system is already operational and potentially exposing patient information. Conducting this assessment does not eliminate the ongoing need for ordinary security monitoring once the system is live, but rather complements it by ensuring the system was thoughtfully designed with privacy and security in mind from the outset. This type of assessment is relevant to systems and vendors regardless of where they happen to be physically located.
  95. The correct answer is A (It allows the specific individual who accessed the room at a given time to be identified.): Using individually assigned access cards rather than a single shared card for the records storage room allows the organization to identify specifically which individual accessed the room at a given time, mirroring the same accountability benefit that individual electronic login credentials provide for system access, and supporting a meaningful audit trail if an issue such as a missing chart needs to be investigated. A shared access card would make it effectively impossible to determine who specifically entered the room on any given occasion, significantly weakening this accountability. Physical access cards have no bearing on encryption, which is a separate technical safeguard relevant to electronic, not paper, information. The room should still remain locked whenever unattended regardless of the type of access card used, since the locking mechanism itself is the primary preventive control, with the individualized card supporting accountability on top of that baseline protection.
  96. The correct answer is B (A private, well-secured dedicated connection generally presents a lower interception risk than transmission over the public.): A private, properly secured dedicated network connection between an organization's own facilities generally presents a comparatively lower risk of interception by an unauthorized third party than transmission over the shared public internet, since the private connection is not exposed to the same broad range of potential external threats. However, this does not mean encryption or other appropriate technical safeguards become unnecessary on a private connection, since defence in depth principles still call for layered protection even where baseline risk may be somewhat reduced, and private networks are not inherently immune to all forms of compromise. Treating all network types as carrying identical risk, or assuming private connections eliminate any further need for safeguards, would both understate the nuanced risk assessment genuinely required. Sound HIM and IT security practice involves evaluating the specific network architecture and applying proportionate additional safeguards accordingly.
  97. The correct answer is A (Strong authentication of the patient's identity before granting access to their own sensitive health information through.): Before launching a patient-facing application providing access to sensitive health information, confirming that strong authentication of the patient's identity is in place, such as a secure login process potentially supplemented by multi-factor authentication, is critical to ensure that only the correct, verified patient, or their properly authorized representative, can actually access the sensitive information displayed through the application. Removing any login requirement entirely would eliminate this essential protection and create a serious risk of unauthorized access by anyone who obtains the device or application link. Arbitrary time-of-day restrictions have no genuine bearing on the core security risk being addressed, and allowing any user unrestricted access to any patient's information would represent a severe and unacceptable security design failure rather than a legitimate feature. Robust identity verification is foundational to safely extending any form of direct patient access through a digital tool.
  98. The correct answer is B (Properly de-identified information, where individuals can no longer reasonably be identified.): When health information is properly de-identified, meaning direct and indirect identifiers are removed or altered such that individuals can no longer be reasonably identified either directly or through combination with other reasonably available information, it generally falls outside the definition of personal health information under most provincial statutes, which can support certain secondary uses, such as research or planning, without needing to rely on individual patient consent for that specific use. This does not mean all other requirements disappear entirely, since research involving even de-identified data may still be subject to research ethics board review, data use agreements, or other institutional governance depending on the context and applicable rules. The legal significance depends entirely on the de-identification being genuinely robust, not merely removing an obvious identifier like a name while leaving other information that could still indirectly identify the individual. This is a nuanced area requiring careful technical and legal assessment rather than a simple assumption.
  99. The correct answer is A (Anonymization is intended to permanently and irreversibly prevent re-identification of an individual.): Anonymization is generally understood to involve processing data so thoroughly that re-identification of the specific individual becomes no longer reasonably possible, even by the organization that originally held the data, whereas pseudonymization involves replacing direct identifiers with a substitute code or token that could, in principle, be re-linked back to the individual by someone holding a separately maintained key or mapping table. This distinction matters significantly for legal and risk analysis, since pseudonymized data generally still carries a meaningful residual re-identification risk and is often still treated as personal information requiring appropriate protection, unlike properly and irreversibly anonymized data. Understanding this distinction helps health information management professionals correctly assess whether a given secondary use genuinely qualifies for treatment outside standard personal health information protections. Confusing the two concepts can lead to a false sense of security about the actual privacy protection achieved.
  100. The correct answer is B (Not necessarily, since the combination of remaining indirect identifiers, such as exact birth date.): Simply removing obvious direct identifiers like name and address does not by itself guarantee that a dataset is genuinely de-identified, since a combination of remaining indirect identifiers, particularly a precise date of birth, a detailed postal code, and a rare or unusual diagnosis, can in many cases still allow a determined party to re-identify a specific individual, especially in a smaller population where few people share that exact combination of characteristics. This illustrates why robust de-identification requires careful, often statistically informed assessment of the full dataset's re-identification risk, not just a checklist removal of the most obvious fields. Genuine de-identification is achievable with proper methods, but achieving it correctly requires real technical expertise rather than an assumption that removing a couple of fields is automatically sufficient. Health information management professionals involved in preparing data for secondary use should collaborate with privacy and statistical expertise to properly assess this risk.
  101. The correct answer is D (Because genuine re-identification risk depends on the specific combination of remaining variables.): A formal, context-specific risk assessment is necessary because genuine re-identification risk is not determined by a single simple rule, such as merely removing a name, but depends on a range of factors including which specific variables remain in the dataset, how large and diverse the underlying population is, how rare or distinctive certain combinations of characteristics might be, and the realistic potential for an adversary to link the dataset with other publicly or otherwise available information to re-identify individuals. This kind of nuanced, technically informed evaluation is why organizations with mature data governance programs require a documented assessment rather than relying on an informal or generic assumption. There is no single universal fixed checklist that applies identically to every dataset regardless of its specific content and context. This requirement reflects the same care and rigour applied to other genuinely technical health information management functions rather than being a mere ceremonial formality.
  102. The correct answer is D (A hospital publishes aggregate statistics on average length of stay by diagnosis category with no identifiable.): Publishing aggregate statistics, such as average length of stay broken down by diagnosis category, without any individual-level identifiable information included, is a legitimate and common example of using properly de-identified, aggregated health data to support planning, research, or public reporting purposes without compromising any individual patient's privacy. The other three examples each involve genuinely identifiable or readily re-identifiable individual-level information being disclosed inappropriately, whether through direct identifiers like names, a combination of precise indirect identifiers like exact addresses and birth dates, or outright publication of complete individual charts, none of which constitute proper de-identification or a legitimate secondary use. Distinguishing between genuinely de-identified aggregate reporting and inappropriately identifiable disclosure is an important, practical competency for health information management professionals supporting research, quality improvement, and public health reporting functions. Aggregation to a sufficiently high level is one of several recognized techniques that can help achieve genuine de-identification.
  103. The correct answer is C (The level of granularity should be limited to avoid creating small, identifiable population cells.): A key de-identification principle in preparing public health surveillance reports is limiting the granularity of geographic and demographic breakdowns to the extent necessary to avoid creating very small population cells, sometimes described as small cell sizes, where the combination of a specific location, time period, and demographic characteristic could realistically allow re-identification of a specific individual, particularly in less densely populated areas or rare disease categories. This often involves techniques such as suppressing or aggregating cells below a defined minimum threshold rather than presenting maximally granular detail without regard to this risk. Ignoring this principle in the pursuit of maximum technical detail would undermine the very purpose of treating the report as de-identified in the first place. This is a well-established, practical consideration in public health and epidemiological reporting that health information management professionals supporting these functions should understand.
  104. The correct answer is B (A limited or partially de-identified dataset that still carries some residual re-identification risk.): A dataset that removes direct identifiers but retains certain indirect identifiers, such as service dates and general geographic region, is generally understood as a limited or partially de-identified dataset that, while carrying meaningfully reduced risk compared to a fully identifiable dataset, still retains some residual re-identification risk depending on the specific combination of remaining variables and the size of the underlying population. This is why such datasets are typically still governed by additional safeguards, such as a formal data use agreement restricting the researcher's further use, disclosure, and any attempt at re-identification, along with appropriate security requirements for storing and handling the data. Treating this type of limited dataset as equivalent to either fully identifiable data or fully anonymized public data would both misstate the actual risk profile involved. This intermediate category reflects a common, practical approach used to balance legitimate research needs against genuine privacy protection.
  105. The correct answer is A (A data use agreement can establish binding contractual obligations on the recipient.): A formal data use agreement establishes binding contractual obligations on the receiving party, commonly including restrictions on any further disclosure of the data, an explicit prohibition on attempting to re-identify individuals within the dataset, requirements for appropriate technical and administrative security safeguards while handling the data, and provisions addressing eventual secure destruction or return of the data once the approved purpose is complete. This type of agreement functions as an important additional layer of protection working alongside, not instead of, the technical de-identification measures already applied to the dataset itself, recognizing that de-identification alone may not eliminate all residual risk, particularly for limited datasets retaining some indirect identifiers. This governance tool is relevant to both domestic and international data sharing arrangements, not exclusively cross-border ones. Health information management professionals often play a role in reviewing or drafting these agreements as part of responsible secondary use governance.
  106. The correct answer is D (Because so few individuals in the population share this rare condition.): When a dataset includes information about a condition so rare that only a very small number of individuals within the relevant population have it, even after standard direct identifiers are removed, someone with some outside knowledge, such as awareness that a specific acquaintance has that particular rare condition, could potentially infer the individual's identity simply because so few people could plausibly match that description. This illustrates an important, sometimes counterintuitive de-identification risk, since rarity generally increases rather than decreases re-identification risk, the opposite of what might be intuitively assumed. Careful assessment of this kind of small population cell risk, and consideration of whether to further aggregate, generalize, or suppress this specific data element, is an important part of a thorough de-identification risk assessment. This scenario demonstrates why a mechanical checklist approach to de-identification is insufficient without genuine analytical judgment.
  107. The correct answer is B (Approaches can range from removing only direct identifiers.): De-identification is best understood as a spectrum of possible approaches rather than a single fixed technique, ranging from simply removing direct identifiers like name and medical record number, to more sophisticated generalization or suppression of indirect identifiers such as precise dates or detailed geography, up to rigorous statistical anonymization methods specifically intended to reduce re-identification risk to a negligible level appropriate for broader release. The appropriate point along this spectrum for a given dataset depends on factors such as the sensitivity of the information, the intended recipient and use, and the acceptable level of residual risk for that specific context, meaning a dataset intended for release to the general public generally warrants a more rigorous approach than one shared under a restrictive data use agreement with a trusted research partner. De-identification techniques and considerations can apply to both structured numerical data and unstructured text-based clinical notes, though each format presents its own particular technical challenges. Recognizing this spectrum helps HIM professionals apply proportionate, context-appropriate safeguards rather than a one-size-fits-all approach.
  108. The correct answer is B (No, the fundamental principle that genuine de-identification requires careful.): The fundamental need to properly and carefully assess re-identification risk when preparing a dataset described as de-identified does not disappear simply because the intended use is internal, such as supporting a quality improvement analytics tool, rather than external, such as sharing with an outside researcher, since the underlying privacy risk to patients from inadequate de-identification exists regardless of who within or outside the organization ultimately uses the data. While an internal use may sometimes carry a somewhat different overall risk profile, for example due to stronger existing organizational security controls, this does not eliminate the need for a genuine, careful assessment appropriate to the specific data and use case. Quality improvement activities, while important and often treated distinctly from direct treatment under certain provincial frameworks, are not simply legally identical to direct patient treatment for privacy purposes. Sound health information management practice applies consistent, principled de-identification rigour regardless of whether the ultimate audience is internal or external to the organization.
  109. The correct answer is D (A method requiring that each combination of certain identifying characteristics in a dataset be shared.): At a conceptual level, k-anonymity is a recognized technical de-identification approach that seeks to ensure that within a given dataset, each combination of certain quasi-identifying characteristics, such as age range, general location, and gender, is shared by at least a defined minimum number of individuals, referred to as k, so that no single record can be uniquely picked out and distinguished from at least that many other similar records based solely on those characteristics. This approach helps reduce, though does not necessarily eliminate entirely under all possible attack scenarios, the risk that a specific individual could be singled out within the dataset through those particular characteristics. It is a statistical and structural technique applied to a dataset's content, distinct from encryption, which is a technical safeguard protecting data during storage or transmission rather than altering the underlying identifiability of the records themselves. A general conceptual familiarity with this and related techniques supports informed collaboration between health information management professionals and technical or statistical experts on de-identification projects.
  110. The correct answer is D (A fully public release generally warrants a more rigorous.): Because a fully public release on an open data portal cannot rely on any contractual restriction limiting who may access the data, how they may use it, or any prohibition against attempting re-identification, this type of release generally warrants a more rigorous, conservative approach to de-identification compared to a restricted release shared with a specific, known recipient under a binding data use agreement that includes its own additional legal safeguards and accountability. This reflects the broader principle that the appropriate point along the de-identification spectrum should be calibrated to the specific context and realistic risk of the intended release, with public release representing a higher-risk context requiring correspondingly stronger protection. Assuming public government-adjacent release requires no de-identification consideration at all would be a serious misunderstanding, since even public bodies remain bound by applicable privacy obligations regarding identifiable health information. This distinction is an important, practical consideration for health information management professionals supporting open data initiatives.
  111. The correct answer is B (Formal review remains important because determining whether de-identification is genuinely adequate for the specific intended use.): Formal information governance review remains important even for proposed releases of de-identified data because properly determining whether a given de-identification approach is genuinely adequate for the specific intended use and context requires expert, case-specific judgment rather than a simple assumption, and because appropriate additional governance safeguards, such as a data use agreement or further risk assessment, should still generally be considered depending on the nature of the release. This reflects that de-identification exists on a spectrum with varying levels of residual risk, and a formal review process helps ensure a consistent, defensible, and genuinely protective standard is applied across the organization rather than leaving these determinations to ad hoc, inconsistent individual judgment. This kind of governance process serves a genuine protective purpose supporting both patient privacy and the organization's own accountability, rather than existing merely as an obstacle to legitimate research or other secondary uses. Health information management professionals often play a central role in operating and supporting this kind of formal review process.
  112. The correct answer is A (Properly aggregated statistical data, such as a total count or average calculated across a sufficiently.): Properly aggregated statistical data, such as a total patient count, an average value, or a rate calculated across a sufficiently large underlying group, generally carries substantially lower re-identification risk than an individual-level record that has merely had a name field removed, since a well-constructed aggregate figure no longer corresponds to any single specific individual's data at all, whereas an individual-level record, even without a name, may still contain enough remaining indirect identifiers to allow re-identification as discussed in the broader de-identification context. This distinction is why aggregation to an appropriately high level is often considered one of the more robust approaches along the de-identification spectrum, particularly for public-facing reporting. Aggregation techniques are widely applicable within health information contexts, including public health surveillance and hospital quality reporting, not merely limited to financial data. Understanding this distinction helps health information management professionals select an appropriately protective approach based on the specific purpose and audience for a given data release.
  113. The correct answer is D (Exact admission and discharge dates could potentially be cross-referenced with other available information.): Exact admission and discharge dates, even without an accompanying name, can still pose a meaningful re-identification risk because they represent a form of indirect identifier that could potentially be cross-referenced with other available information, such as a public news report describing a notable incident involving hospitalization on a specific date, social media posts, or other independently known facts about a specific individual's hospital stay, potentially allowing someone to narrow down or confirm which specific record in the dataset belongs to that individual. This is a well-recognized category of risk in de-identification practice, sometimes addressed through techniques such as generalizing exact dates into broader date ranges or shifting dates by a random offset while preserving the overall interval between events. Assuming that removing a name eliminates all risk regardless of what other precise information remains reflects exactly the kind of incomplete analysis that a thorough de-identification risk assessment is meant to catch. This scenario reinforces why exact date fields deserve specific, careful consideration in any de-identification review.
  114. The correct answer is D (No, PIPEDA is a federal law that applies to federally regulated organizations and to provinces without their own substantially similar law.): PIPEDA, the Personal Information Protection and Electronic Documents Act, is a federal law that generally applies to federally regulated organizations engaged in commercial activity and to organizations in provinces that have not enacted their own substantially similar private sector privacy legislation, but most Canadian provinces have instead enacted their own health-specific privacy statutes, such as Ontario's PHIPA or Alberta's HIA, which govern the handling of personal health information within that province instead of PIPEDA applying directly. This layered, jurisdiction-specific framework means there is no single national law governing all provincial health records uniformly, and a health information management professional must correctly identify which specific statute actually governs their particular organization and province. Misunderstanding this structure and assuming PIPEDA universally applies is a common and consequential error. Understanding one's own applicable provincial framework, rather than defaulting to federal law, is a foundational competency for Canadian HIM professionals.
  115. The correct answer is A (The Personal Health Information Protection Act, Ontario's own provincial health-specific privacy legislation.): In Ontario, the Personal Health Information Protection Act, commonly abbreviated PHIPA, is the province's own health-specific privacy legislation that primarily governs how health information custodians, including hospitals and physicians, must collect, use, disclose, and safeguard personal health information within the province, generally displacing the need to apply PIPEDA directly to this type of information in this context. The federal Access to Information Act governs access to records held by federal government institutions and has no direct application to Ontario hospitals or physicians. Correctly identifying PHIPA, rather than defaulting to a federal statute, as the governing framework is essential for any health information management professional practicing in Ontario. Each province with its own health-specific privacy legislation has its own distinctly named statute, meaning this specific answer would differ if the same question were asked about a different province.
  116. The correct answer is B (Provincial and territorial governments, typically through professional regulatory colleges.): regulation and licensing of individual health professionals falls under provincial and territorial jurisdiction, typically delegated to self-regulating professional colleges or associations established under provincial legislation, such as a college of physicians and surgeons or a college of nurses. This means licensing requirements and scopes of practice can differ between provinces for the same profession. The federal government does not directly license individual health professionals.
  117. The correct answer is A (To protect the public by setting and enforcing standards of practice and conduct.): the primary mandate of a self-regulating professional college is public protection, achieved by setting entry-to-practice requirements, standards of professional conduct and competence, and by investigating complaints and enforcing discipline against members who fail to meet those standards. This is distinct from a professional association or union, which may focus on advocating for members' economic interests. Understanding this public-protection mandate is central to health human resources regulation concepts.
  118. The correct answer is A (Scope of practice, the legally authorized range of activities a professional may perform.): scope of practice refers to the legally defined range of activities, procedures, and responsibilities that a regulated health professional is authorized to perform, determined by their education, training, demonstrated competence, and the governing provincial legislation and regulatory college standards. Scope of practice can differ between provinces for the same profession and can evolve over time as regulations change. This concept is essential to understanding how different regulated professionals work together within a care team.
  119. The correct answer is A (Evolving and expanding scopes of practice for regulated health professionals to improve access to care.): many provinces have expanded the scopes of practice for professions such as registered nurses, nurse practitioners, and pharmacists in recent years, allowing them to take on responsibilities like prescribing certain medications, as a strategy to improve access to care and make more efficient use of the health workforce. These changes are enacted through provincial legislation and regulatory college updates rather than federal mandate, and they can vary considerably between provinces. This trend reflects ongoing health human resources planning responses to access challenges.
  120. The correct answer is D (A profession whose practice and standards are governed by provincial legislation and a regulatory body.): a regulated health profession is one for which provincial or territorial legislation establishes a governing regulatory body, licensing requirements, defined scope of practice, and standards of conduct that members must meet to legally practice, such as medicine, nursing, and pharmacy. Not every health-related occupation is formally regulated in every province, and regulation status can vary. Understanding which professions are regulated, and by what body, is foundational to health human resources knowledge.
  121. The correct answer is A (Uneven distribution of professionals, especially rurally, combined with recruitment and retention pressures.): health human resources planning in Canada faces ongoing challenges including geographic maldistribution of providers, particularly shortages in rural and remote communities, alongside broader recruitment, training capacity, and retention pressures, all complicated by a decentralized system where each province plans its own workforce independently. These challenges have prompted various interprovincial and federal-provincial collaboration efforts over time. This is a commonly tested contextual concept in the health care system domain.
  122. The correct answer is A (National certification sets competency standards, while provincial law governs specific legal obligations.): a national certifying body can establish consistent professional competency standards and a shared credential across Canada, while day-to-day legal obligations, such as privacy protection, record retention timelines, and specific reporting duties, are set by the provincial or territorial legislation applicable to where the professional practices. Both layers apply simultaneously and are not in conflict but rather complementary. This dual structure is directly relevant to how CHIM professionals themselves are credentialed and governed.
  123. The correct answer is C (A family health team where physicians, nurse practitioners, dietitians, and social workers jointly manage patient care.): interprofessional collaborative care models bring together multiple types of regulated and unregulated health providers, such as physicians, nurse practitioners, dietitians, and social workers, working together within a coordinated team to manage a patient's overall health needs, often within primary care settings such as family health teams. These models aim to make more effective use of each provider's scope of practice and improve continuity of care. This approach has become increasingly common in Canadian primary care reform efforts.
  124. The correct answer is C (A registered nurse, since nursing is a regulated profession in every province.): nursing is a regulated health profession in every Canadian province and territory, requiring licensure through the applicable provincial or territorial college of nurses before an individual may legally practice and use a protected title such as registered nurse. Personal support workers, hospital porters, and general administrative staff are typically not regulated health professions in most provinces, although some provinces have introduced voluntary registries for certain support roles. Recognizing which roles require formal licensure is a practical health human resources concept.
  125. The correct answer is A (Because scope of practice is set through provincial legislation and regulatory college standards.): since health professional regulation, including the definition of scope of practice, falls under provincial jurisdiction and is established through each province's own legislation and regulatory college standards, the same profession can have a broader or narrower authorized scope of practice depending on the province. This explains, for example, why pharmacist prescribing authority has expanded further and faster in some provinces than others. This variability is a direct consequence of Canada's decentralized approach to health professional regulation.
  126. The correct answer is C (Continuing competence requirements, the ongoing education a professional must complete to keep a licence.): continuing competence requirements are the ongoing professional development obligations, such as continuing education hours, self-assessment, or practice reviews, that regulatory colleges require members to complete to maintain their licence and demonstrate ongoing competence throughout their career. These requirements are a core mechanism through which regulatory colleges fulfill their public protection mandate over time, not just at initial licensure. This concept is directly relevant to how CHIM professionals themselves maintain their own credentials.
  127. The correct answer is A (Targeted recruitment and retention incentives, such as rural placement bursaries, for underserved regions.): addressing geographic maldistribution of a health profession typically involves targeted strategies such as rural placement incentives, bursaries tied to service commitments in underserved areas, or expanded training opportunities located closer to rural communities, aimed specifically at improving recruitment and retention where shortages are most acute. General workforce reduction or eliminating licensure requirements would not address a geographic distribution problem and could worsen quality and safety. This type of targeted planning reflects real health human resources strategies used across Canadian provinces.
  128. The correct answer is B (A professional association advocates for members, while a college's mandate is public protection.): professional associations generally focus on advocacy, professional development, networking, and representing members' interests, while regulatory colleges hold the legal authority and public protection mandate to license, set standards for, and discipline members of a regulated profession. These are distinct organizational types even though a professional may interact with both throughout their career. Understanding this difference is directly relevant to how CHIM professionals engage with their own professional bodies.
  129. The correct answer is D (Planning must account for training capacity, mobility, credential recognition, and demographic shifts.): effective health human resources planning requires provinces to consider multiple interacting factors together, including the capacity of training programs, the ease or difficulty of health professionals moving between provinces, timely recognition of internationally trained professionals' credentials, and demographic trends such as an aging population increasing both patient demand and workforce retirements. Treating a workforce shortage as caused by a single isolated factor tends to miss the systemic nature of the challenge. This systems-level understanding is important for interpreting workforce planning discussions in the Canadian context.
  130. The correct answer is D (Labour mobility provisions generally support credential recognition across provinces, subject to some requirements.): Canada has labour mobility frameworks intended to facilitate recognition of a regulated professional's credentials when they move between provinces, reducing duplication of requirements, although individual regulatory colleges may still apply certain jurisdiction-specific registration steps or orientation requirements. This is not a fully automatic or unconditional process, but it is significantly streamlined compared to starting credentialing entirely from scratch. These frameworks apply broadly across many regulated health professions, not solely physicians.
  131. The correct answer is A (As a single, fixed lump-sum allocation regardless of the volume of patients treated.): a global budget provides a hospital or health organization with a predetermined, fixed total funding amount for a defined budget period, typically a fiscal year, intended to cover its overall operating costs regardless of the precise volume or mix of patients treated during that period. This differs from activity-based funding, where payment is directly tied to the specific volume and type of services delivered. Global budgeting has historically been the dominant hospital funding approach in many Canadian provinces.
  132. The correct answer is D (Activity-based funding, payment tied directly to the actual volume of patients treated.): activity-based funding, sometimes called patient-based funding, allocates hospital funding according to the actual volume and mix of patients treated, using standardized case weights to reflect the relative resource intensity of different case types, so hospitals are paid more for treating more or more complex patients. This differs fundamentally from global budgeting, which provides a fixed amount unrelated to actual patient volume. Several Canadian provinces have adopted activity-based funding, at least partially, to create incentives tied to patient volume and efficiency.
  133. The correct answer is A (Case-mix-based funding, payment calculated from standardized weights tied to patient complexity.): case-mix-based funding uses standardized patient classification systems to group patients into categories reflecting similar clinical characteristics and expected resource use, then applies relative weights to determine funding, allowing hospitals to be compensated according to the complexity and volume of the specific patients they actually treat. This approach underlies most activity-based funding models used in Canada and depends heavily on accurate clinical coding and abstracting performed by health information management professionals. Case-mix methodology is one of the most directly relevant funding concepts to CHIM practice.
  134. The correct answer is C (Whether the data is genuinely aggregated and non identifiable while still serving the stated planning purpose.): The core ethical question for any proposed data sharing arrangement is whether the data truly protects patient privacy through genuine aggregation and non identifiability while still meaningfully serving its stated legitimate purpose. The agency's general reputation, potential financial benefit to the hospital, and what other facilities have done are secondary considerations that do not address the fundamental privacy safeguard question. Her assessment should center on the actual privacy protection built into the proposal.
  135. Waiting for seniority is not necessary since the ethical obligation to report accurately applies from the very first day of employment.
  137. Relying entirely on someone else's review or documenting doubts privately does not satisfy the ethical obligation to ensure the report reflects genuine, verified findings. Substituting another uninvolved signatory does not resolve the underlying gap in verification.
  140. Proceeding on schedule or adding a note after merging both allow the serious error to actually happen first. Preventing the error is the appropriate ethical priority.
  141.
